Changelog
=========

All notable changes to this project will be documented in this file.

___

Unreleased
----------

### Breaking

- `require_auth` and `api_key_auth` now require a verifier. Supply one per route via `verifier=` or globally via `SecurityConfig.auth_verifier`. Without a resolvable verifier, requests are rejected with 401 (fail-closed). Previously any `Bearer`/`Basic` prefix or any API-key header value was accepted without validation (GHSA-82x7-w4xq-w99f, CWE-287).
- The old header-presence/scheme-prefix behavior is no longer the default for `require_auth`. If you only wanted a header-presence gate, use the new `require_authorization_header(scheme=...)`, documented as NOT authentication.

### Added

- `is_ip_allowed` and `check_ip_access` (`guard_core/utils.py`) are now exported at the top level as `guard_core.is_ip_allowed` and `guard_core.check_ip_access`, resolved through the existing lazy `__getattr__` re-export mechanism used by every other public name in `guard_core.__all__`. Both already took a raw IP string plus a `SecurityConfig`, with no `Request`/`Response` coupling, so framework-agnostic and websocket consumers can now call them directly instead of reaching into `guard_core.utils`.
- `check_rate_limit_by_ip(ip, config, redis_handler=None, endpoint_path="")` (`guard_core/handlers/ratelimit_handler.py`, exported at the top level as `guard_core.check_rate_limit_by_ip`, mirroring `is_ip_allowed`): a request-free rate-limit primitive for callers with only a raw IP, such as websocket consumers. Returns `True` when the call is allowed, `False` when rate-limited; every call records a hit in the sliding window, the same side effect the HTTP pipeline has. It never constructs or mutates the shared `RateLimitManager` singleton, the counting logic that singleton used (`_get_redis_request_count`/`_get_in_memory_request_count`) is now lifted into module-level pure functions that both the singleton and this primitive call, backed by a dedicated in-memory store so ad hoc calls can never repoint the HTTP pipeline's limits. With the default `endpoint_path=""` the Redis key collapses to `{prefix}rate_limit:rate:{ip}`, the same bucket the HTTP pipeline's global rate limit uses for that IP, so the two share one budget by design; pass a non-empty `endpoint_path` (e.g. `"ws"`) for an isolated budget disjoint from the pipeline's, isolation guaranteed by input validation, `ip` must parse via `ipaddress.ip_address` and `endpoint_path` must not contain a colon, or the call raises `ValueError` before any counting side effect, including before the `enable_rate_limiting` early return. The in-memory fallback is process-local and never shares counts with the pipeline singleton's own in-memory store, Redis-backed deployments do.
- `SecurityConfig.enable_rate_limit_auto_ban` (default `False`): opt-in wiring that feeds rate-limit violations into the same auto-ban engine penetration detection already uses. When enabled together with `enable_ip_banning`, each active-mode (non-passive) rate-limit violation increments the `rate_limit` category of the existing suspicious-count structure and runs the same threshold logic as the pen-detection path, `threat_ban_config["rate_limit"]` first, then the flat `auto_ban_threshold`/`auto_ban_duration`, landing the ban through `ip_ban_manager.ban_ip` with reason `"rate_limit_exceeded"` (`penetration_attempt` stays the reason for the pen-detection path, distinct for telemetry); `"rate_limit"` is now also a valid `threat_ban_config` key alongside the existing pattern-detection categories. Passive mode never feeds the counter, matching the pen-detection path. The threshold-check-plus-ban logic previously duplicated between penetration-detection escalation and the suspicious-activity check was consolidated into one shared helper that both of those and this new path now call, with no behavior change to the two existing callers. The suspicious-count structure is in-memory, per-process, and 10k-IP FIFO-capped, so multi-replica deployments do not share auto-ban counts, a pre-existing property of the pen-detection path that now also applies to rate-limit traffic when this flag is on.
- `check_rate_limit_by_ip` now also feeds the auto-ban engine: when a call returns `False` (rate-limited) and both `enable_rate_limit_auto_ban` and `enable_ip_banning` are set, the violation is counted and, on crossing the threshold, banned through `ip_ban_manager.ban_ip` with reason `"rate_limit_exceeded"` (`"rate_limit_exceeded:rate_limit"` under a `threat_ban_config["rate_limit"]` override), the same resolution and reason strings as the middleware path above. No new knob; this reuses `enable_rate_limit_auto_ban`/`enable_ip_banning` and previously did nothing for callers of the primitive, such as websocket consumers, since A2 only wired the HTTP middleware. The violation count is a dedicated, in-memory, per-process counter private to this primitive (`_by_ip_autoban_counts` in `ratelimit_handler.py`/its sync mirror), separate from and never merged with `middleware.suspicious_request_counts`: a deployment that mixes the HTTP pipeline and direct primitive calls for the same IP tracks two independent violation counts. `passive_mode` suppresses counting here too. The threshold-resolution-plus-ban core shared with the two existing middleware callers was factored out of `_try_threshold_ban` into `_resolve_and_apply_threshold_ban` (`guard_core/core/checks/helpers.py`, pure aside from the `ban_ip` call, no logging), so there is still one threshold implementation, not three; `_try_threshold_ban` now only adds its `log_activity` call on top, with no behavior change to the penetration-detection escalation or `RateLimitCheck` callers. Once the IP is already banned, a further rate-limited call to the primitive short-circuits before counting or re-banning, so a caller with no upstream banned-IP check (as the HTTP pipeline has via `IpSecurityCheck`) does not refresh the ban TTL, grow the counter, or emit a ban log line on every subsequent over-limit call; the first threshold-crossing ban still fires as before.
- `DynamicRules.auto_ban_threshold`, `DynamicRules.auto_ban_duration` (both `int | None`, `ge=1`) and `DynamicRules.enable_rate_limit_auto_ban` (`bool | None`): Guard Agent dynamic rule pushes can now tune auto-ban at runtime instead of requiring a config redeploy. Applied with the same if-not-`None` setattr-plus-log convention as the existing `enable_ip_banning` toggle, in `DynamicRuleManager._apply_feature_toggles`; all three restore to their pre-push value through the existing `_SNAPSHOT_FIELDS` rollback when a later rule application fails (`auto_ban_threshold` was already snapshotted, `auto_ban_duration` and `enable_rate_limit_auto_ban` are newly added to that tuple). No new resolution tier: a push mutates `SecurityConfig` directly, so the existing threshold-check-plus-ban helper's resolution (`threat_ban_config` per-category first, flat fallback) is unchanged. Ordering with emergency mode is deliberate: an explicit `auto_ban_threshold` override applies first, then emergency-mode halving applies to the resulting value, since `_apply_feature_toggles` already runs before `_activate_emergency_mode` in `_apply_rules`.
- `DynamicRules.expires_at` is now read by `DynamicRuleManager`: a dynamic rule push with a non-null `expires_at` auto-reverts its config mutations once that time passes, instead of the mutations persisting until the next push or a process restart (the field already existed on the model but was previously never consulted). `_check_rule_expiry` runs on every dynamic-rule poll tick, under the same lock `_apply_rules` uses, and always runs before a newly-fetched rule is processed, so an expired rule reverts first and a new rule then applies against the restored base. On the first no-rule to active-rule transition, `_apply_rules` captures a base snapshot of the `_SNAPSHOT_FIELDS` (the pre-rule config) and retains that snapshot across any superseding pushes, so a later expiry always restores the original pre-any-rule base rather than an intermediate pushed value; the restore also undoes an emergency-mode halving, since `emergency_mode` and `auto_ban_threshold` are both snapshot fields. `expires_at=None`, the default, means no expiry and leaves existing rules unaffected. Expiry is keyed on `expires_at` only, `ttl` is a separate fetch-cache field and plays no part in it; naive (tzinfo-less) `expires_at` values are treated as UTC, with that handling's own fix detailed under Fixed, below. A rule whose `expires_at` is already past when `update_rules` receives it now never activates: `_reject_if_already_expired` runs right after the fetch and before the version check, reusing the same naive-as-UTC coercion `_check_rule_expiry` uses (extracted into the shared `_coerce_naive_utc` helper both call), rejecting the rule outright instead of applying it and reverting on the next tick. This closes a gap an adversarial critic found live: an agent that keeps serving the same already-expired rule on every poll previously drove a revert-then-reapply loop every tick, forever, with config never settling at base, an event pair (`dynamic_rule_updated`/`dynamic_rule_applied`) firing per tick, and a repeated `[EMERGENCY MODE]` CRITICAL log for a rule carrying `emergency_mode`, since the `get_dynamic_rules` agent protocol places no obligation on the agent to withhold an expired rule. The rejection warn-logs once per distinct `(rule_id, version)` via a single-slot last-skipped marker, so a stale agent polling with the same expired rule cannot spam the log; a different incoming already-expired rule overwrites the marker and gets its own warning, a valid (non-expired) rule applying in between never touches the marker at all. Because the marker is a single slot rather than a per-rule-id history, two distinct already-expired rules polled alternately (X, Y, X, Y, ...) each warn on every occurrence, not just the first, a deliberate minimal shape rather than an unbounded per-rule dedupe cache.
- A dedicated `Detection Gate` CI workflow (`.github/workflows/detection-gate.yml`) runs on every pull request and master push: it installs with the same optional extras as the Dockerfile (`--frozen` is deliberately omitted because `uv.lock` is gitignored in this repository, so a fresh checkout has no lockfile), fails on generated sync-tree drift (`scripts/unasync.py --check`), and runs both two-sided detection benchmarks (pattern layer and the `detect_penetration_attempt` entry point) as a named, isolated job whose failure message names the regressed category. A best-effort step posts the report as a PR comment, on failures too, with the long documented-gap annotations elided and the body capped under GitHub's comment size limit; the step is `continue-on-error` so a fork PR's read-only token can never redden the gate for a reason unrelated to detection. The gate was proven able to fail before being trusted, locally in the session that authored it: reintroducing the historical `file_inclusion` lookbehind bug drove the benchmark to `file_inclusion false-positive attribution rose: baseline=0 actual=27`, and the revert was verified byte-identical; the same red-then-green proof is intended to be repeated on the gate's own first PR.
- `SecurityConfig.auth_verifier`: global default verifier callable for `require_auth` and `api_key_auth`.
- `require_authorization_header(scheme=...)`: presence/scheme-prefix-only decorator (NOT authentication).
- Verifier contract: `verifier(request, credential) -> Principal | None`. Sync or async in async (ASGI) deployments; sync verifiers only in WSGI deployments (an async verifier or any verifier that raises is rejected with 401 fail-closed rather than bubbling to the pipeline error handler). The authenticated principal is stored on `request.state.auth_principal`.
- **Known-mechanism disclosure: UTF-16/UTF-32 wide-encoded attack payloads are detected, but that coverage is an accident of a different feature, not a designed capability, and is now pinned so it cannot silently disappear.** There is exactly one body decode in the package, `body_bytes.decode("utf-8", errors="replace")` (`guard_core/utils.py:1505`), and no wide-encoding-aware decoding exists anywhere. A UTF-16 or UTF-32 payload decoded as UTF-8 with `errors="replace"` survives intact because every NUL byte between the wide-encoded characters is itself valid UTF-8 and passes through untouched; what turns that NUL-interleaved noise back into a matchable payload is `ContentPreprocessor.remove_null_bytes` (`guard_core/detection_engine/preprocessor.py:283`, invoked from `preprocess()` at line 511), which exists for null-byte-injection defense, an unrelated purpose, and reassembles the payload as a side effect. Measured against `<script>alert(1)</script>` (25 chars) and `1' UNION SELECT username, password FROM users--` (47 chars): UTF-16LE/BE interleave 25 and 47 NUL bytes respectively, UTF-32LE/BE interleave 75 and 141; all four shapes detect across the xss, sqli, and cmd_injection categories, as do a UTF-16 payload carrying a BOM and a UTF-16LE payload with a CJK character mixed into otherwise-ASCII markup. This is now pinned by `tests/test_detection_engine/test_wide_encoding_detection.py` (mirrored under `tests/test_sync/`, 19 tests): a 3-family x 4-encoding detection matrix routed through the real `detect_penetration_attempt` entry point in enhanced mode, plus a mechanism test that asserts on the preprocessed intermediate directly, encoding a payload wide, decoding it exactly as the production path does, confirming the raw decode does not yet contain the literal payload, then confirming `remove_null_bytes` alone reconstructs it, so the pin fails at the mechanism, not just at a final boolean, if null handling is ever scoped or narrowed. Verified adversarially: with `remove_null_bytes` neutralized to a no-op in an isolated scratch copy, all 19 pins failed, the mechanism tests specifically with the message "wide-encoding detection depends on null-removal in ContentPreprocessor.remove_null_bytes; if you scoped or narrowed null handling, all UTF-16/32 coverage is now silently gone"; restoring the method returned all 19 to green. This is not full wide-encoding support: UTF-16/UTF-32 with a leading BOM stripped by a proxy before guard-core sees it, mixed-width or partially-wide payloads that dilute the ASCII-per-2-or-4-bytes ratio, and any encoding that does not place NUL bytes between ASCII characters are not covered by this pin and were not verified. Separately, a swept sample of 39 common Python codecs (Windows/Mac/ISO-8859 code pages, KOI8, the CJK double-byte families, UTF-7, UTF-8-with-BOM) found none of them miss, because the `<script>alert(1)</script>` and SQLi markers used are pure ASCII and every one of those codecs maps ASCII to the identical byte values UTF-8 does; the only misses found were the EBCDIC family, which does not: `cp037`, `cp273`, `cp500`, `cp875`, `cp1026`, `cp1140`, and `cp424` (every EBCDIC codec registered in this Python's stdlib `encodings` package) all miss both markers outright, with zero NUL bytes produced, so null-removal cannot help. This requires the upstream application to itself decode the request body as EBCDIC while guard-core decodes UTF-8, an unusual precondition outside legacy mainframe-integration deployments; it is reported here as scoping data, deliberately left unfixed in this change.
- **Known-limitation disclosure: an attack probe path embedded inside a full prose sentence (`Malicious crawler attempted /wp-admin/install.php`, `Note: the scanner hit /wp-admin/install.php on our staging host`) is not detected, a deliberate, measured non-goal rather than a silent gap.** The whole-string path anchoring landed in v3.12.0 to stop benign documentation prose from false-positiving on a bare `wp-admin`/`wp-login`/`xmlrpc`/`phpinfo`-shaped mention is exactly what makes a real probe path invisible once it is embedded in an ordinary sentence instead of delivered as a bare path or URL: the two shapes are structurally indistinguishable to a pattern-matching layer. Three corroboration strategies were measured against a committed 32-malicious/43-benign-exposure corpus before settling on the non-goal: an unanchored bare-token match (dropping the whole-string anchor) recovered 31/32 recall but at 34/41 false positives against the benign corpus as it stood at measurement time, reintroducing the exact false-positive shape the anchor exists to prevent; a keyword co-occurrence heuristic (requiring an attack-implying word such as "attempted" or "exploit" near the path) reached 28/32 recall at 0 false positives but missed the headline case, a neutral incident-report sentence like "Note: the scanner hit /wp-admin/install.php" carries no alarming keyword, and a real attacker can phrase the identical probe without one; and weighted corroboration through the existing pattern-weight/anomaly-scoring mechanism, which only fires when a single payload names two distinct probe paths, reached 1/32 recall at 0/43 false positives, too narrow to matter. The 34/41 and 0/43 denominators are both correct, measured at different points against a growing benign corpus: 41 cases at the first measurement, 43 once a separate unit appended three more benign cases (a CI YAML, a crontab line, and a Makefile recipe) in between. None of the three clears both the recall and false-positive gates, so this stays a documented, measured gap rather than a shipped mitigation. The corpus cases (`*_embedded_prose_*` in `tests/test_sus_patterns/test_detection_benchmark.py`, mirrored under `tests/test_sync/`) remain in the test suite, still counted in the recall denominator, as the record of the gap.
- **New mechanism, not yet wired to any built-in pattern: `guard_core.detection_engine.scan_window` (`bounded_search`, `bounded_finditer`, mirrored under `guard_core.sync.detection_engine.scan_window`) fixes the quadratic blowup in `PREFIX[^x]*TERMINATOR`-shaped patterns without narrowing the negated class and without a length cap.** 22 built-in patterns share that dominant shape (`<script[^>]*>[^<]*<\/script\s*>` is the canonical example) and are O(n) per `PREFIX` occurrence, O(n²) overall when `TERMINATOR` never appears, because the negated class does not exclude `PREFIX`'s own characters; two cheap-looking fixes are explicitly rejected here rather than reintroduced by a future edit: an unbounded quantifier is CVE-2025-53539, and a `{0,N}` cap just relocates the bypass to N+1 (CVE-2025-54365). `regex` and atomic groups are not available (not a permitted dependency; Python 3.10 floor). Call as `bounded_search(text, compiled, prefix, terminator)`, a drop-in replacement for `compiled.search(text)`. All three arguments beyond `text` are compiled `re.Pattern`s the caller builds once per built-in pattern (not per call): `compiled` is the pattern's existing, byte-for-byte unmodified regex, `prefix` matches the pattern's leading anchor (what marks a fresh attempt, e.g. `<script`), and `terminator` matches whatever closes the pattern's *last* unbounded gap, not an intermediate anchor. For `<script[^>]*>[^<]*<\/script\s*>` that is `<\/script\s*>`, not the bare `>` that closes the opening tag; using the intermediate `>` truncates the scan window before the real close and silently drops the match. For the two-gap `<!(?:ENTITY|DOCTYPE)[^>]+SYSTEM[^>]+>` (idx59: gap1 up to the `SYSTEM` literal, gap2 up to the final `>`) it is enough to hand it the single final `>`; both gaps share one negated class, so bounding the outer window once and handing the bounded span to the unmodified `compiled` pattern lets its own engine resolve `SYSTEM` internally; bounding gap1 alone and leaving gap2's search unbounded is the mistake this closes, since `SYSTEM` is a findable literal that repeated prefixes reach even with gap1 bounded. `terminator` may be a literal, an alternation, or a character class (`[/\\]` for `\.\.;[^/\\]*[/\\]` needs no special-casing, since it is matched the same way as any other compiled pattern). Mechanism: for each `prefix` match, the window is bounded to `endpos = min(next prefix occurrence, terminator occurrence)`, quote-aware, then `compiled.match(text, start, endpos)` runs the caller's real, untouched pattern against that bounded span; a wider `endpos` than strictly necessary is always safe (Python's `endpos` behaves like slicing for matching purposes), only a too-narrow one causes a false negative, which is what the quote-aware scan exists to prevent. Quote-awareness: `"..."` and `'...'` spans (backslash-escapes respected) are skipped wholesale while hunting for the next `prefix`/`terminator` candidate, per WHATWG HTML 13.2.5.34 (attribute-value-double-quoted state accepts a raw `<` verbatim), so `<script x="<" src="//evil/x.js">` and the other 11 rows in `embedded_terminator_rows.py` keep matching; a non-quote-aware boundary search was verified to truncate `<script data-x="<script" src="evil.js">` before the real `>` and is pinned as a regression test (`test_naive_unquoted_scan_would_have_truncated_before_the_real_terminator`). No length cap exists anywhere in this mechanism: the match is whatever `compiled` finds inside the bounded window, so there is no N+1 bypass to relocate to. Total cost is O(n): windows between consecutive `prefix` occurrences do not overlap, and per-window cost is bounded by the window's own size, not the remaining input length. Proven linear (not just asserted) in a killable subprocess (`tests/test_detection_engine/test_scan_window_timing.py`, `subprocess.run(..., timeout=30)`): on `<script[^>]*>` against an unclosed-tag adversarial fill doubled through 32768/65536/131072/262144 chars, the raw pattern's own `.search()` measures ~4x wall time per doubling (quadratic, ~3.5s at 262144) while `bounded_search` measures ~2x per doubling (~0.03s at 262144), a >100x gap at the largest size that the test asserts with generous machine-variance margins. This lands the mechanism only; no built-in pattern definition is touched by this change, and none of the 22 candidates are wired to it yet.
- **`scan_window` round two: the per-position Python loop over `prefix` occurrences is replaced with a loop over `terminator` occurrences, since two converted patterns (`..;` and `#{`) have irreducibly short prefixes that opened roughly 87k and 131k windows at the 262144 cap, and the Python-level loop over that many windows was itself the cost (47ms and 71ms, one over the 50ms ceiling). The public API is unchanged: `bounded_search`/`bounded_finditer(text, compiled, prefix, terminator)`, same drop-in contract, so callers pick this up by re-copying the file.** Mechanism: `terminator.finditer(text)` runs once, C-level; zero terminator matches (the catastrophic `"#{"*n`/`".."*n`/`"<script"*n` fills, none of which contain their terminator at all) returns immediately with zero Python-level iteration. Otherwise, quoted spans are found once (`"..."`/`'...'`, backslash-escapes respected, an unterminated quote treated as running to end-of-string exactly like the original per-position scan's `_skip_quoted_span` did) and the text is partitioned into segments at unquoted-prefix-starts and unquoted-terminator-ends (both sorted lists, `bisect`-searchable); a segment is skipped outright unless it contains at least one terminator match of either quotedness, since the compiled pattern can complete on a *quoted* terminator character (it is quote-blind, same as raw `re`) even though quote-awareness stops a *quoted prefix recurrence* from truncating an earlier segment's ceiling. Within a surviving segment, prefixes (quoted and unquoted both, since a quoted prefix can still be the only opener the outer loop reaches) are tried left to right, first `compiled.match` success wins, matching the original per-position scan's left-to-right outer loop exactly. Complexity is now `O(t log p + p log p)` for `t` terminators and `p` prefixes, dominated by two C-level `finditer` calls and a sort, not the P-Python-iterations that made `..;`/`#{` expensive despite already being individually bounded. Cyclomatic complexity of the old single `bounded_finditer` (rank C, 14) forced a tree-wide `xenon guard_core/` failure once already; the fix is split into `_segment_edges` (rank B) and `_matches_in_segments` (rank A), both under the `guard_core/`-wide max-absolute-B ceiling, since a per-file xenon pass does not catch this and the tree pass is what gates. **Parity was proven, not assumed**: all 12 `embedded_terminator_rows.py` payloads plus 5 adversarial shapes (nested prefix, prefix embedded in a quoted attribute, a terminator-lookalike character inside quotes, the two-`<!ENTITY`-in-gap-2 case, and an `<object>` attribute-value-spanning case) match the pre-rewrite helper byte-for-byte, span for span, pinned as `test_several_prefixes_before_one_terminator_with_one_quoted` and the other regression tests in `test_scan_window.py`. A 50,000-trial fuzz comparison against the pre-rewrite helper (short random strings mixing `<script`, `>`, `<`, quotes) found zero outcome-level disagreements (detect vs. not-detect always agreed) but did find residual disagreement in the exact reported match *span* on pathological multi-quote adjacency absent from every one of the 17 required shapes and from all 22 real patterns (e.g. two prefixes separated only by a second, quoted prefix, where which one the two algorithms pick first can differ while both still detect); this is disclosed here rather than silently accepted, per the standing rule that a bypass or behavioral gap does not get quietly worked around. Timing is measured as process CPU time (`time.process_time()`, minimum of 5 runs), not wall clock, because identical code measured wall-clock times 6x apart on the same machine purely from scheduling contention during this round; the killable `subprocess.run(..., timeout=90)` wrapper is a hang guard, not the measurement. On the two named problem fills at 262144: `..;` (dir_traversal-shaped) dropped from 47ms to 1.37ms CPU, `#{` (template-ssti-shaped) dropped from 71ms to 0.08ms CPU. On the `<script[^>]*>` doubling series (32768/65536/131072/262144), CPU time: raw `.search()` 0.054s/0.217s/0.864s/3.46s (quadratic, ~4x per doubling), `bounded_search` 11µs/21µs/42µs/81µs (linear, ~2x per doubling, >40,000x faster than raw at the largest size).

### Changed

- The CI test matrix now enforces what was previously only measured: `--cov-fail-under=100` (branch coverage was already measured via the pyproject `addopts`, but nothing failed the build when it dropped) and `-W error`, so a new warning fails the build instead of accumulating. Both flags were validated against the full local suite before landing (7336 passed, 100 percent line and branch, zero warnings surviving `-W error`). A `quality` job runs `make quality` on Python 3.10.
- `make lint` now runs `ruff format --check .` instead of rewriting files in place. The write-mode step could never fail, so formatting drift passed `make lint` locally while `pre-commit` caught it in CI; check mode makes the target an actual gate. Use `make fix` to apply formatting.

- **Built-in detection patterns no longer compile with `re.MULTILINE`.** The global flag was what let `^`/`$` match any line boundary, the root cause behind five categories of false positives that 3.12.0 fixed one anchor at a time. After that rework, an audit shows exactly three anchor usages remain across the 93 built-in patterns, all in SSRF patterns whose adjacent `\s` alternative already consumes newline boundaries, so dropping the flag is a proven no-op: zero outcome divergences across the full benchmark corpora plus targeted multi-line shapes, compiled both ways. A pin test now fails if any built-in pattern ever compiles with `MULTILINE` again. User-supplied patterns keep `re.IGNORECASE | re.MULTILINE` unchanged for compatibility, pinned by its own test; that covers both `add_pattern(custom=True)` and the `custom=False` path Guard Agent dynamic rules use, since both carry operator-supplied pattern text whose semantics must not change under them.

- **`block_cloud_providers` accepts all six fetchable providers.** `DigitalOcean`, `Linode`, and `Vultr` join `AWS`, `GCP`, and `Azure` as valid values; the fetch, cache, and blocking machinery has supported all six since their fetchers landed, but the config validator's allowlist predated them and rejected the three newer names. Region carve-outs (`:!region`) validate for all six; region metadata exists only for AWS and GCP, so a carve-out on Azure, DigitalOcean, Linode or Vultr exempts nothing and the whole provider stays blocked, which is stated in the field's description and pinned by tests. Consequence for Guard Agent dynamic rules: a dashboard-pushed rule naming `DigitalOcean`, `Linode`, or `Vultr` now takes effect, where it was previously dropped as an unknown provider by the same shared allowlist. BEHAVIOR CHANGE for the argless `@block_clouds()` decorator: it now blocks all six providers (it derives from the shared allowlist instead of a hardcoded three-name set), so existing argless call sites start blocking DigitalOcean, Linode, and Vultr traffic on upgrade; pass an explicit list to keep the previous scope.

- **Post-construction assignment to any of `SecurityConfig`'s 15 dynamic-rule snapshot fields now raises `ValueError` on an out-of-range or wrong-type value instead of silently accepting it: `blocked_countries`, `whitelist_countries`, `rate_limit`, `rate_limit_window`, `endpoint_rate_limits`, `block_cloud_providers`, `blocked_user_agents`, `enable_penetration_detection`, `enable_ip_banning`, `enable_rate_limiting`, `emergency_mode`, `emergency_whitelist`, `auto_ban_threshold`, `auto_ban_duration`, `enable_rate_limit_auto_ban`.** Direct `setattr` on a pydantic model bypasses field validation, and `DynamicRuleManager._apply_feature_toggles` and its sibling apply methods mutate `SecurityConfig` this way on every dynamic rule push, so an invalid value from a compromised or buggy rule source previously landed in the running config unchecked (an out-of-range `auto_ban_threshold`, a non-bool feature flag, a malformed `endpoint_rate_limits` entry, a non-frozenset `blocked_countries`). Each field now revalidates on assignment through the existing `_FIELD_REVALIDATORS`/`__setattr__` mechanism already used for `whitelist`, `blacklist`, `threat_ban_config`, and the rest. Assignment validation is intentionally stricter than construction, not identical to it, the same posture those pre-existing revalidators already had: pydantic's construction-time lax coercion (a numeric string to `int`, `0`/`1` to `bool`, a tuple interchanged for a `list`-typed field) never runs on a bare `setattr`, so a runtime mutation now requires an already correctly-typed value where construction would silently have coerced one. `validate_assignment` was deliberately not turned on model-wide, avoiding its performance and behavior blast radius. `DynamicRuleManager._apply_rules`'s existing rollback restores every already-mutated field to its exact pre-push value when a later field in the same push now fails this validation, and the same restore runs when an active rule's `expires_at` passes in `_check_rule_expiry`, so a push that trips the new validation stays fully atomic rather than leaving a partially-applied config (this exactness needed the `block_cloud_providers` restore-fidelity fix below to hold for every field, not just the other 14).

- **`SecurityConfig.auto_ban_threshold` and `auto_ban_duration` now declare `ge=1` and reject `0` or a negative value at construction.** Both were previously unbounded `int` fields, unlike their own siblings: `DynamicRules.auto_ban_threshold`/`auto_ban_duration` and `ThreatBanConfig.threshold`/`duration` already enforce `ge=1`, and the emergency-mode halving path (`max(1, original_threshold // 2)`) has clamped to that same floor since dynamic rules shipped, evidence that 1 was always the intended floor, just never declared on `SecurityConfig` itself. Existing code constructing `SecurityConfig(auto_ban_threshold=0)` or a negative `auto_ban_duration` now fails at construction with a `ValueError`; pass 1 or above.

### Fixed

- **`guard_core/handlers/behavior_handler.py` and its sync mirror had drifted to radon MI rank B (16.95), below the project's rank-A floor.** Every method in `BehaviorTracker` was already rank A or B on `radon cc`; the drift was structural, not any one function's complexity: MI is computed from a file's aggregate Halstead volume and LOC, and one 405-line class carrying three unrelated responsibilities (response-body pattern matching, JSON path matching, and passive/active action dispatch) in a single file pushed the whole-file score down regardless of how simple each method was in isolation. Split by responsibility into three private mixins `BehaviorTracker` now composes, following the same mixin-composition shape already used for `DynamicRuleManager`/`DynamicRuleApplicationMixin`/`DynamicRuleEventSenderMixin`: `BehaviorJsonPatternMixin` (`_parse_pattern`, `_handle_array_match`, `_traverse_json_path`, `_match_json_pattern`; `guard_core/handlers/_behavior_json_pattern.py`), `BehaviorResponsePatternMixin` (`_check_response_pattern`, `_read_response_body_prefix`, `_log_body_unavailable`, plus the `_BoundedResponseBodyReader` protocol; `guard_core/handlers/_behavior_response_pattern.py`), and `BehaviorActionDispatchMixin` (`apply_action`, `_log_passive_mode_action`, `_execute_ban_action`, `_execute_active_mode_action`, `_send_behavior_event`; `guard_core/handlers/_behavior_action_dispatch.py`). `behavior_handler.py` itself keeps `BehaviorRule`, `_hash_identity_segment`, `config_to_rule`, and `BehaviorTracker`'s `__init__`/`initialize_redis`/`initialize_agent`/`get_recent_event_count`/`track_endpoint_usage`/`track_return_pattern`. No behavior changed and no public API moved: every relocated method keeps its exact name, signature, and body as a bound method reachable on `BehaviorTracker` exactly as before (including the private ones several tests call directly, `tracker._check_response_pattern(...)`, `tracker._match_json_pattern(...)`, etc.), `_check_response_pattern`'s load-bearing `try/except Exception` around the `errors="surrogateescape"` response-body decode is untouched, and `BehaviorRule`/`BehaviorTracker`/`config_to_rule` all still import from `guard_core.handlers.behavior_handler` (`guard_core.sync.handlers.behavior_handler` in the sync tree) exactly as framework adapters already depend on. Both `behavior_handler.py` files go from B (16.95) to A (38.04); all three new modules land at A (40.01-46.32) in both trees. All extracted methods stayed top-level mixin methods rather than nested closures specifically because `radon cc`'s `show_closures=true` (this repo's `[tool.radon]` setting) and xenon's harvester (`show_closures=False`) can disagree on the same code, and `xenon --max-absolute B --max-modules A --max-average A` passes clean on the whole `guard_core` tree, checked as a whole-tree run rather than per-file, since a small extracted module can pass individually while pulling the tree's *average* into B. Regenerated via `scripts/unasync.py`, `--check` clean. 100% line and branch coverage preserved on all eight touched files (four per tree); `ruff format`, `ruff check`, `mypy`, `vulture`, and `bandit` all clean.
- **Two LDAP detector sites had the same fixed-length-window defect: a corroborating token placed far enough past the trigger character silently evaded detection on a genuine LDAP filter breakout or conjunction injection, because the scan collapsed "found nothing" and "ran out of budget before it could look" into the same `False`.** Site one, `_ldap_wildcard_chain_is_injection`'s forward scan (`_ldap_breakout_forward_window`): for `_LDAP_PAREN_BREAKOUT_RE` matches, which start at the `)` itself rather than at an adjacent `*` (unlike the other two LDAP candidate patterns, where the backward window already contains its own `*`), a bare-`=` breakout (`)(attr=value`) was flagged only if its corroborating wildcard landed inside a fixed 40-character forward budget; `admin)(cn=` + 34 filler chars + `*)` (wildcard at offset 39) was detected on every mechanism, the same shape at offset 40+ was not, an exact step function, on this release's new-this-release breakout pattern (the pinned pre-3.13 base tree, `26357018`, has no equivalent pattern for a bare-`=` breakout at any offset, so this was never a working prior behavior to regress from). Site two, `_ldap_paren_conjunction_is_injection`'s lookahead: a genuine `(&`/`(|` conjunction followed by an attribute name 40 characters or longer (RFC 4512 section 1.4 places no length limit on `AttributeDescription`) pushed the corroborating `=` past a fixed 40-character lookahead slice, and the followup regex, evaluated only against that pre-truncated slice, never saw it; **this site is pre-existing, present unmodified in the pinned base tree since before this release, not a 3.13 regression.** Both measured through `detect_penetration_attempt()` (never the `sus_patterns_handler.detect()` sub-component) as wire bytes with `content-length` set, across `raw_body`, `form_body`, `multipart_body`, `json_body`, and `query_param`; `url_path`, `header`, and `cookie` are unaffected either way by an unrelated, pre-existing mechanism: `_CTX_LDAP` does not include `header` or `url_path`, so the `ldap` category is skipped outright for those three mechanisms regardless of this bug or its fix. An initial fix attempt gave both scans a bounded tri-state (mirroring the backward window's existing `depth_unresolved`, itself added earlier this release for the identical reason) and treated "no token found, scan was cut off by the cap rather than a real boundary" as detected. This closed both bypasses but, measured with a purpose-built benign mirror (`admin)(cn=` + 41+ boundary-free filler chars with **no** attack token anywhere in reach, and the site-two equivalent `(|` plus a space plus 41+ letters with no `=`), introduced a real false positive past the same 40-character cap: any coincidental, locally-unbalanced `)(`/`(&`/`(|` shape followed by enough ordinary text now flagged too, since the fix could not distinguish "this was going to be a token" from "this was always going to be ordinary text" once scanning was cut off. Rejected rather than shipped, per [GHSA-rrf6-pxg8-684g](https://github.com/rennf93/fastapi-guard/security/advisories/GHSA-rrf6-pxg8-684g): trading a length-cap miss for a length-cap false positive is the same defect, not a fix, and no wider cap closes it since a cap relocates the cliff rather than removing it. Fixed instead by replacing the fixed-length window on both sites with a scan to the end of the enclosing LDAP filter expression: balanced parens plus the existing quote/newline boundary chars, additionally bounded by the start of the same pattern's own next match (`_ldap_next_candidate_scan_limit`) so a string of unclosed, repeated breakout shapes cannot make every candidate scan to the content cap and reintroduce the quadratic cost this release has spent multiple rounds removing elsewhere. This has no magic number and therefore no cliff to relocate: a corroborating token is found regardless of distance, and `admin)(cn=` followed by any amount of ordinary token-free text stays clean, because there both is no closure and no real attack token is present within the same filter expression either way. Neither scan needs an unresolved tri-state any more, since every scan now terminates at a real grammatical boundary, a genuine next-candidate start, or end of string, never at an arbitrary character count; both `_ldap_breakout_forward_window` and `_ldap_wildcard_chain_is_injection`'s final check are back to the simple two-window `or` form. Confirmed linear (not quadratic) by CPU time (child-process `time.process_time()`, minimum of five, isolated to the LDAP candidate-matching path) across three adversarial fills at 131072/262144/524288 characters: microseconds-per-character stayed flat across the full size range for all three fills, confirming linear scaling; one fill (`(|` plus a space plus an unbroken word-character run with no `=`, exercising site two's lookahead) initially cost 12.3ms at 262144 chars from an unrelated pre-existing defect exposed by the newly-unbounded scan, `_LDAP_PAREN_CONJUNCTION_FOLLOWUP_RE`'s attribute-name alternative backtracking through the entire non-matching run looking for a `=` that was never there (invisible at the old 40-char cap, an O(40) worst case; an O(n) worst case once the cap was removed), fixed by a cheap `"=" in tail` pre-check before attempting that alternative, dropping the same measurement to 1.2ms. Regression tests (`test_paren_breakout_fires_regardless_of_trailing_junk_length`) pin site one's four offsets (39/40/41/200) through the wire entry, mirroring the backward side's existing `test_wildcard_breakout_fires_regardless_of_leading_junk_length`; site two's pins live in the corresponding measurement corpus rather than duplicated into the pytest suite.
- **The glued-backtick command-injection corroborator's context window was a fixed 40-character count in both directions (`_BACKTICK_CONTEXT_WINDOW_CHARS`), so a corroborating shell metacharacter (`;`, `|`, `||`, `&&`, `$(`, `${`) placed 40+ characters from the glued `` `token` `` pair fell outside the slice and silently stopped being detected, identically on base (pre-existing, not a 3.13 regression) and on this lane before the fix.** Measured through `detect_penetration_attempt`, enhanced mode, across `raw_body`/`form_body`/`multipart_body`/`json_body`: a glued pair (`` `custom_col` ``, a plausible bare identifier not in the shell-command denylist) followed by N filler bytes then `; echo` detected at N=39 and stopped detecting at N=40/41/200/4096, both on `313-base-26357018` and pre-fix on this lane. `query_param` was unaffected by the bug (and is unaffected by the fix): that context is in `_AMBIGUOUS_BACKTICK_INJECTION_CONTEXTS`, so any glued pair is flagged there via a separate, pre-existing fallback that never reaches the window. Replaced the fixed character count with `_backtick_window_start`/`_backtick_window_end`, a scan bounded by the construct's own natural delimiters (a backtick, single quote, double quote, newline, or carriage return) instead of a count, applied to both call sites that used the old constant (`_backtick_pair_context_window`'s metacharacter scan and `_strong_sql_keyword_glued_to_pair`'s SQL-keyword-exemption scan). A bare backtick is itself one of the delimiters, so this simultaneously satisfies "bounded by the next candidate" without a second mechanism: the next real `_GLUED_BACKTICK_CANDIDATE_RE` match, if one exists nearby, necessarily starts with a backtick, which the delimiter scan already stops at or before. This is deliberately not a wider fixed count: raising 40 to any other N only relocates the same cliff to N+1, the exact shape of GHSA-rrf6-pxg8-684g (CVSS 7.8) against a sibling project, now cited directly in the code path this bug lived in. Measured CPU-linear on a dense repeated-glued-backtick-candidate fill (`` `a` `` back to back, the shape a naive unbounded backward scan would make quadratic on) via `time.process_time()` in a killable subprocess, minimum of 5 runs: 1.86ms / 3.71ms / 7.44ms at 131072/262144/524288 bytes, ~2.0x per doubling, statistically indistinguishable from base's own already-linear fixed-window cost at the same sizes -- the previous/next candidate's own backtick is immediately adjacent in this fill, so each scan terminates in O(1), and the fix adds no measurable overhead. The two-sided detection benchmark corpus (`defect_corpus.py`/`gate.py`) is clean end to end against base: zero new-miss, zero new-FP, aggregate and per-mechanism; the corpus carries no case that samples this specific offset-cliff shape, so this clean gate is not independent proof of no false positive for this change, only the targeted rows below are. Rows (attack and matched benign-mirror side, all 5 corpus mechanisms, offsets 39/40/41/200/4096, `base`/`current` verdicts, `grammar` citing POSIX 2.9.3/2.6.3 for why a real shell accepts the padding either side of the corroborator) are in `backtick_window_rows.py`; `test_glued_backtick_far_shell_metacharacter_detected_in_request_body` and its sibling benign/no-metacharacter and delimiter-bounded pins are in `tests/test_sus_patterns/test_cmd_injection_corpus.py` (mirrored under `tests/test_sync/`). One of those pins, `test_backtick_window_reaches_the_full_gap_when_no_delimiter_present`, asserts `_backtick_pair_context_window` returns the entire content string for the benign no-metacharacter shape, proving the scan genuinely traverses the full padding rather than being short-circuited by an earlier gate (`_backtick_pair_glued`, the implausible-SQL-identifier check, or the chained-shell-command check on the token itself), the specific mistake that let a previous fix in this same bug class (the LDAP forward-window fix, see above) land a benign pin that could not fail. **Disclosed, pre-existing false-positive surface, widened in reach by this fix but not created by it**: this validator's corroborating signal is bare shell-metacharacter *presence*, with no requirement that a recognizable dangerous command follow it, so ordinary prose containing one of `;`/`|`/`&&` near a glued backtick mention (a markdown code span immediately followed by more prose that happens to contain a semicolon) was already a false positive within the old 40-character window (measured `True` on base at offset 10 and 39) and remains one now at any distance up to the next quote/newline/backtick (measured `True` post-fix at offset 41 and 200, where base was `False`, since those offsets fell outside the old window). This is not a new defect class introduced by this change: the heuristic cannot distinguish `; cat /etc/passwd` from `; it handles pagination fine` by design, at any window size, character-count or structural; removing the length cliff, itself a real bypass, necessarily widens the reach of this pre-existing ambiguity, and narrowing the corroborating signal set to close it was out of scope for this fix (a length-cliff bug and a signal-precision tradeoff are independent decisions). That precision tradeoff is owned separately and lands in the same release: the glued-backtick signal is narrowed to require a command-shaped token after the metacharacter, in the same change that removes the shell-command denylist from this category, so the corroborator becomes structural rather than a list of binary names, with the two prose shapes above pinned as benign rows and `; cat /etc/passwd` pinned as the attack row.
- **Four of seven built-in patterns carrying a `{0,N}`/`{1,N}` content-length cap are converted to a scan-window mechanism instead, closing the same GHSA-rrf6-pxg8-684g/CVE-2025-54365-vs-GHSA-j47q-rc62-w448/CVE-2025-53539 tradeoff already established in this file: `_LDAP_NULL_BYTE_ATTR_RE`, `_LDAP_NULL_BYTE_DECODED_ATTR_RE`, `_QUOTE_SPLICE_CANDIDATE_RE`, and `_GLOB_WILDCARD_ATOM_RE`.** A `{0,N}` cap is linear but a length cliff, an unbounded quantifier is uncapped but quadratic, neither is acceptable alone, and a cap is CVE-2025-54365-shaped by construction regardless of whether a specific attack currently depends on it, so leaving one in place after this file already established the scan-window alternative would be reintroducing a defect class it has twice closed before. Measured, not assumed: two of the four (`_LDAP_NULL_BYTE_ATTR_RE`'s `{0,63}` attribute-name cap, `_QUOTE_SPLICE_CANDIDATE_RE`'s `{1,12}` first-token cap) turn out to be dead on a uniform-character-class run specifically, because `.search()` retries at every position and a same-class prefix (all letters, or all `\w` before the first quote) lets it restart within the last N characters of an arbitrarily long run and still complete the match; a purpose-built parity test proves both caps already accepted a 2x/10x-over-bound payload before this change (`test_ldap_null_byte_attr_2x_and_10x_name_bound_still_detected`, `test_quote_splice_2x_and_10x_token_bound_still_detected`), so removing them is closing a dormant CVE-2025-54365 shape, not a live bypass on that specific axis. `_GLOB_WILDCARD_ATOM_RE`'s `{0,100}` caps on both sides of `[?*]` are dead for the same reason (`[?*]` is itself a member of the surrounded class, so both sides can match zero-width and `.search()` finds a match starting anywhere within 100 characters of the marker regardless of how much text precedes it). The two genuinely live caps are `_LDAP_NULL_BYTE_ATTR_RE`/`_LDAP_NULL_BYTE_DECODED_ATTR_RE`'s `{0,255}` value cap: unlike the attribute name, the value sits after the pattern's one and only `=` anchor, so there is no earlier restart position that still includes the required `=`, and a value padded past 255 characters before the `*)...`+null-byte tail genuinely evaded detection pre-fix (`test_ldap_null_byte_attr_2x_and_10x_value_bound_now_detect`, old-capped pattern confirmed `None` on the padded payload, new one detects).
  - **Mechanism, all four.** None of the four reuses `bounded_finditer` (`guard_core/detection_engine/scan_window.py`) directly: their "prefix" is a broad character class (`[a-zA-Z]`, `\w`) shared with content inside the pattern's own later gap, not a distinctive literal, and driving `bounded_finditer` off that class was tried first and produces real false negatives, confirmed on `"uid=alice*)\x00"`/`"c'a't"`-style candidates, because the class recurs inside the value/second-token content and the window truncates at that recurrence, before the pattern's own required tail. `_ldap_null_byte_attr_finditer` instead drives off `tail_re.finditer(text)` (the rare, specific `\*\)+(?:...)` / `\*\)+\x00` anchor), walks backward from each tail hit through the value's `[\d\w\s]` run to find where it starts, checks for an immediately preceding `=`, then walks backward again through `[\w-]` to find the attribute-name start and its required `[a-zA-Z]` lead character, and only then attempts `compiled.match` bounded to `[name_start, tail_match.end())`; a `"*" not in text or ")" not in text` guard skips the whole function on fill that cannot possibly contain the tail. `_quote_splice_finditer` drives off `_QUOTE_SPLICE_QUOTE_RUN_RE.finditer(text)` (the quote run, rarer than a bare word character) with a symmetric backward walk to the preceding word run's start, plus a one-character forward peek (does a word character immediately follow the quote run?) that skips the expensive `compiled.match` call outright when it cannot possibly succeed. `_glob_wildcard_finditer` processes by maximal same-class run (`_GLOB_WILDCARD_CLASS_RUN_RE.finditer`, one call) rather than by `[?*]` occurrence, checks each run once for whether it contains a wildcard marker, and attempts at most one `compiled.match` per qualifying run bounded to the run's own span; driving by marker occurrence instead (tried first) reintroduces quadratic cost on a text that is one long run threaded with many `[?*]` characters, since `[?*]` is itself inside the class and every occurrence would re-walk the same run. All backward/forward walks are plain character-class stepping with no fixed distance bound, so none reintroduces a `{0,N}`-shaped cliff: a walk terminates at the nearest structurally-required boundary (a non-class character, `=`, or text start), never after a fixed count, and disjoint terminator/quote-run occurrences keep the total work linear in text length.
  - **Wiring.** These four patterns' compiled `re.Pattern` objects are no longer reachable through the normal `.search()`/`.finditer()` path: `_check_regex_pattern` now looks up `pattern.pattern` in a new `_WINDOWED_PATTERN_FINDERS` registry before touching either the `PatternCompiler`-backed enhanced path or the legacy per-pattern retry path, and on a hit delegates to a new `_check_windowed_pattern`, which runs the finder in the existing `shared_regex_executor()` thread pool under the same `detection_compiler_timeout` and success/timeout reporting (`report_scan_success`/`report_scan_timeout`) the rest of this file already uses, then feeds whatever matches it finds through the existing, unmodified `_first_accepted_regex_threat`. Because that is the same function every other pattern's matches flow through, the two existing candidate-rejection validators keyed to `_QUOTE_SPLICE_CANDIDATE_RE` and `_GLOB_WILDCARD_ATOM_RE` in `_CANDIDATE_REJECTION_VALIDATORS` (`_quote_splice_token_is_dangerous_command`, `_glob_wildcard_token_is_dangerous_command`, both string-exact-match against a command denylist and untouched by this change) still gate acceptance identically; a windowed match's `.group()` is the same substring the old, unmodified `compiled` pattern would have matched at that position; nothing about candidate validation changes.
  - **The two existing whole-suite ReDoS safety nets, `test_builtin_pattern_is_not_catastrophic` and `test_every_builtin_passes_the_safety_validator` (`tests/test_detection/test_builtin_pattern_safety.py`) and `test_validator_rejects_zero_built_in_patterns` (`tests/test_sus_patterns/test_custom_redos_hardening.py`), all iterate `_pattern_definitions` and run each pattern's raw, un-windowed `.search()` directly, which is now the wrong test for a windowed pattern by design: its whole point is that raw `.search()` is no longer how it is ever invoked.** All three now skip patterns present in `_WINDOWED_PATTERN_FINDERS` (a plain filter on the parametrize/loop input, not a `skip`/`xfail` marker), paired with a new `test_windowed_builtin_pattern_is_not_catastrophic` that runs the exact same adversarial-input battery, this time through each pattern's actual dispatched finder in a forkserver subprocess with the same 6-second-per-input budget, and a new `test_windowed_patterns_are_exactly_the_scan_window_converted_four` pinning the registry's expected size so a future addition cannot silently widen the exemption without a reviewer noticing (see the in-progress note below for the other three). Confirmed red-first against the unmodified tree: with the cap still in place, `test_every_builtin_passes_the_safety_validator` failed on `_QUOTE_SPLICE_CANDIDATE_RE` alone once uncapped (`PatternCompiler.validate_pattern_safety` timed out at its own internal 2000-character probe), confirming the fix is load-bearing rather than redundant with pre-existing coverage.
  - **Timing, process CPU time (`time.process_time()`), minimum and median of 5 runs each, at the full 262144-character reach-probe fill (`tests/test_sus_patterns/_scan_window_cap_removal_timing_probe.py`, run as a killable subprocess by `test_scan_window_cap_removal_timing.py`, doubling ratios asserted linear at every step):** `ldap_null_byte_attr` 0.00005s, `ldap_null_byte_decoded_attr` 0.00003s (both against a genuine reach-probe of `"uid=alice*) "` repeated with the null-byte tail withheld), `quote_splice` 0.0040s (dense alternating `"a'" * k`, the densest shape that still avoids the pre-check short-circuit), `glob_wildcard` 0.0002s; all four comfortably inside the 50ms budget with wide margin. Wall-clock was measured first and discarded per project doctrine: process CPU time is what the acceptance bar means by "under 50ms", since wall-clock on a machine running many concurrent lanes measures contention, not the code.
  - **Correctness.** For each pattern, the OLD capped regex is recompiled inline in the test file as a literal (not imported, since the cap no longer exists in source) and compared against the new windowed finder on realistic payloads (`test_*_old_payload_still_matches`, asserting identical `bool()` and identical `.group()`), on payloads padded to 2x and 10x the removed bound (`test_*_2x_and_10x_*_now_detect` / `*_still_detected`, see the dead-cap-versus-live-cap distinction above for which direction each assertion goes), and against representative benign strings that must stay unmatched. `test_ldap_null_byte_attr_value_containing_letters_still_detected` pins the specific false-negative trap found and fixed mid-implementation: an earlier draft of the LDAP finder used the attribute-name class (`[a-zA-Z]`) as `bounded_finditer`'s prefix directly, which truncated the scan window at the first letter inside the VALUE (e.g. the `a` in `"alice"`) before ever reaching the real `*)...`+null-byte tail, silently losing `"uid=alice*)\x00"`, `"uid=alice*)%00"`, and every other realistic payload whose value contains a letter, exactly the shape both `LDAP_NUL_FAMILY_BODIES` and `test_ldap_conjunction_breakout.py`'s corpus already exercise; caught before landing by tracing the mechanism against that exact payload, not by the eventual test suite. Full local suite and `unasync --check` both clean on the resulting tree; both `guard_core/handlers/suspatterns_handler.py` and its generated sync mirror pass `ruff`, `mypy` (strict), `xenon --max-absolute B --max-modules A --max-average A`, `bandit`, `vulture`, and `deptry` with zero new findings.
  - **In progress, not yet converted: the remaining three capped patterns, `_TEMPLATE_MUSTACHE_KEYWORD_CAPPED_RE`/`_TEMPLATE_MUSTACHE_MATH_CAPPED_RE` (`{{...}}` SSTI) and `_TEMPLATE_JSP_EL_DOLLAR_BRACE_CAPPED_RE` (`${...}` SSTI), still wired into `_pattern_definitions` exactly as before this change, the only three of the seven that ARE independently quadratic on adversarial input regardless of the cap** (their prefix, `{{`/`${`, is a two-character literal that can recur at every offset of a repeated-prefix fill, and a pre-fix measurement at 80000 characters of `"{{" * 40000`, already present in `test_builtin_pattern_is_not_catastrophic`'s own `_ADVERSARIAL_INPUTS`, timed out past that test's 6-second budget outright). The intended uncapped replacements, `_TEMPLATE_MUSTACHE_KEYWORD_RE`/`_TEMPLATE_MUSTACHE_MATH_RE`/`_TEMPLATE_JSP_EL_DOLLAR_BRACE_RE`, and their finder functions (`_template_mustache_keyword_finditer` and siblings; prefix `\{\{`/`\$\{`, terminator `\}\}`/`\}`, routed through the shared `bounded_finditer`, guarded by a cheap upfront `in`-plus-`.search()` necessary-condition check for the pattern's own required keyword/operator marker so a fill containing neither the marker nor the terminator never enters the expensive path) are already written and are the intended final shape, kept in the tree unused rather than deleted so the design work is not repeated, but deliberately not yet referenced by `_pattern_definitions` or `_WINDOWED_PATTERN_FINDERS`: an independent fuzz of the shared `bounded_finditer` implementation found it silently drops real matches (`raw=MATCH, bounded=None`) on nested-delimiter input for the canonical `<script>`-shaped pattern this mechanism was designed around, and a targeted fuzz of these three template patterns against the same implementation reproduced the identical failure mode at a lower but nonzero rate (2/20000 and 1/20000 trials respectively, both `raw=MATCH, bounded=None`, e.g. `'${ysy(cm(b${}"m yst\'b$y(}c"\'a}\'(\' ss"t\'s'` on the JSP-EL pattern). Wiring these three in now, on an implementation known to drop matches, would trade a disclosed length-cliff bypass for an undisclosed and less predictable one, so the cap stays live in production until a corrected shared implementation is available and re-copied into this tree; no timing number for these three is recorded here, since a number measured against an implementation proven to silently lose matches is not evidence of anything. `test_windowed_patterns_are_exactly_the_scan_window_converted_four` and this file's own timing/correctness suites currently cover only the four live conversions for the same reason.

- **`SemanticAnalyzer` was quadratic in input length on plain, syntax-free text, reachable through `detect_penetration_attempt()` with no crafting at all.** Measured first-party on the pinned tip: `SemanticAnalyzer.analyze("a" * n)` went 0.25s / 0.99s / 3.96s / 15.86s at n=2500/5000/10000/20000 (4.0x per doubling), and through the real `detect_penetration_attempt()` entry point a plain-letter request body cost 0.27s / 1.04s / 4.11s / 4.16s, plateauing near 4.1s of CPU per request once the existing content-length cap truncates the body, not because the underlying cost stopped growing. `cProfile` on `analyze("a" * 10000)` named `extract_suspicious_patterns` (1.10s of the 3.97s total) and `_check_code_pattern_risks` (0.64s) as the hot frames, both calling `re.finditer`/`re.search` with unbounded-then-required-literal regex shapes against the full, uncapped content. Four of the five patterns in `attack_structures` shared the same defect: `tag_like` (`<[^>]+>`), `function_call` (`\w+\s*\([^)]*\)`), `path_traversal` (`\.{2,}[/\\]`), and `url_pattern` (`[a-z]+://`) each open with an unbounded greedy quantifier whose character class does not exclude the characters that make up the run itself, so on adversarial fill (a run of the pattern's own start character with no terminating literal anywhere in the string) the backtracking engine retries an O(n) failed match at each of O(n) start positions; `command_chain` (`[;&|]{1,2}`) was already bounded and unaffected. Confirmed per-pattern in isolation: `function_call` and `url_pattern` are both already quadratic on the exact `"a" * n` shape the report used (4.0x per doubling each); `tag_like` and `path_traversal` are linear on that shape but equally quadratic on their own adversarial fill (`"<" * n` and `"." * n` respectively, both 4.0x per doubling), confirming this as a structural property of the four patterns, not a coincidence of one fill alphabet. `_get_structural_pattern_boost` and `_check_code_pattern_risks` each additionally hardcoded a second, separate copy of the `tag_like`/`path_traversal`/`function_call` regex text instead of referencing `attack_structures`, so the same defect existed in triplicate. Fixed by bounding all four quantifiers to generous but finite maximums matching realistic content shapes (`tag_like` to 512 chars of tag body, `function_call` to a 64-char identifier and 256-char argument list, `path_traversal` to 20 dots, `url_pattern` to a 32-char scheme), which caps the worst-case backtrack at each start position to a constant instead of O(n), making all four linear; and by collapsing the three duplicate hardcoded copies in `_get_structural_pattern_boost` and `_check_code_pattern_risks` to reference `self.attack_structures[...]` directly, so the fix applies once instead of needing three synchronized edits. Two other candidate sites were profiled with adversarial fill and confirmed NOT to share the defect, so left untouched: `detect_encoding_layers`'s `&[#\w]+;` (its own character class excludes `&`, which structurally bounds each candidate's backtrack span regardless of fill) and `_check_code_pattern_risks`'s `[\{\}].*[\{\}]` (empirically linear under a many-braces-then-long-tail adversarial fill, up to 10000 chars). `looks_like_binary_content` was already linear (a single `sum()` pass with no regex) and needed no change. Re-measured after the fix: direct `analyze("a" * n)` at n=2500/5000/10000/20000/40000/80000 now runs 0.014s / 0.024s / 0.048s / 0.094s / 0.186s / 0.298s (approximately 2.0x per doubling, the remaining sub-linear tail at 80000 from `extract_tokens`'s existing 50000-char cap truncating further growth), and through `detect_penetration_attempt()`, 0.032s / 0.055s / 0.108s / 0.152s at the same four sizes, down from a 4.1s plateau. The other six fill shapes from the original report (`quote`, `paren`, `sqltok`, `union`, `space`, `mixq`) were re-measured before and after and stayed in the same sub-0.015s range at every size, within normal timing noise (all already linear, none traded for the `char` fix). Output equivalence was verified across all 1319 cases in `MALICIOUS_CORPUS`, `BENIGN_CORPUS`, and both production e2e corpora (`test_detection_benchmark.py`, `test_detect_penetration_attempt_benchmark.py`) plus a set of realistic edge cases: `attack_probabilities`, `is_obfuscated`, `code_injection_risk`, and the derived `threat_score` are identical before and after for every one of those 1319 cases. Only two synthetic cases deliberately constructed to exceed the new bounds (a single 600-char HTML tag, a single 400-char function-call argument list, neither shape present anywhere in either benchmark corpus) lose their structural-pattern score boost; both stay correctly flagged `is_obfuscated=True` regardless, since that signal is driven by entropy and consecutive-non-space-run checks this change does not touch. Both detection benchmarks are unchanged end to end: `test_detect_penetration_attempt_recall_and_false_positive_rate` still reports 293/327 recall and 24/335 false positives, `test_detection_benchmark`'s category sweep still reports 281/314 recall and 23/331 false positives, both re-run against the fixed tree, not just asserted against their floor/ceiling baselines.

- **Correction to the entry above: the "1319 cases, zero diffs" neutrality claim was vacuous, and a follow-up cap-boundary measurement found a real recall loss on `tag_like`, now closed structurally rather than by raising the cap.** `MALICIOUS_CORPUS`, `BENIGN_CORPUS`, and both e2e corpora contain no case longer than the new bounds (512/64+256/20/32 chars), so re-running them could not, structurally, have observed anything the bounding changed; reporting that run as evidence of behavioral neutrality was an error. A purpose-built cap-boundary corpus was built instead, deliberately excluded from the existing suites, at 0.5x/0.9x/1.0x/1.1x/2x/10x of each bound, in matched real-attack and benign-shape families (152 cases across the five `attack_structures` axes, plus 90 boundary-crossing variants naming `eval`, `exec`, `system`, `popen`, `Function`, `setTimeout`, `document.write`, `innerHTML`, SQL-in-a-call, command chains, and `javascript:`/`data:` URIs, plus a dedicated `name("a" * n)` sweep from 200 to 2560 chars across six names). This found one real regression: a canonical XSS payload (`<img src=x onerror=alert(document.cookie) data-p="...">`) padded past the 512-char `tag_like` cap lost `is_threat` in the semantic channel alone (`threat_score` 0.73 -> 0.59, `xss` attack-probability 0.633 -> 0.333), confirmed through the real `SusPatternsManager.detect()` pipeline too: at 563 chars the same payload's `threats` list drops from `["regex", "semantic"]` to `["regex"]` only, so overall `is_threat` stayed `True` in this instance only because the pattern also independently trips the unrelated, unbounded regex-layer event-handler rule, not because the semantic layer kept working. Any finite cap on `tag_like` is defeatable by more padding, so raising it was rejected in favor of removing the cap entirely: `tag_like` is back to unbounded `<[^>]+>`, and the backtracking risk is closed structurally instead, per `SemanticAnalyzer._tag_scan_window`, content is only ever regex-scanned up to the position of its own last `>` character (one `str.rfind` call), so a start position that cannot possibly reach a closing `>` is never attempted, turning the earlier O(n)-failed-match-at-each-of-O(n)-positions cost into the cost of that single upfront scan. This is applied at all three call sites that read `tag_like`, including `extract_tokens`, a third site the original fix's duplicate-collapsing missed because it ran the pattern inside a `ThreadPoolExecutor` at the time (see below). Re-verified linear on `"<" * n` (zero closing chars, the worst case) at n=2500/5000/10000/20000/40000/80000: 0.0018s / 0.0022s / 0.0043s / 0.0075s / 0.0136s / 0.0233s, and on a realistic long tag at the same first four sizes: 0.0095s / 0.0191s / 0.0373s / 0.0749s, both linear, and the 152-case cap-boundary sweep now shows zero `is_threat` or `attack_probabilities` deltas anywhere on the `tag_like` axis. The other three bounded patterns (`function_call`'s 64-char name and 256-char argument list, `path_traversal`'s 20 dots, `url_pattern`'s 32-char scheme) were kept as-is: the same cap-boundary sweep, run through both the isolated `SemanticAnalyzer` and the real `.detect()` pipeline, found zero `is_threat` regressions on any row for these three, at any multiplier up to 10x the bound, including real dangerous calls (`eval`, `exec`, `system`, `popen`) padded up to 2560 characters, because the regex layer independently and unconditionally catches those keywords regardless of argument length. The margin genuinely does shrink, though: `eval(` + 512 `a`s + `)`'s `code_injection_risk` drops 0.7 -> 0.5 and semantic `threat_score` drops 0.615 -> 0.525 once the argument passes 256 chars, neither figure ever crossing the 0.7 `semantic_threshold` that gates `is_threat` in either tree, so this is a disclosed, measured shrinkage, not a bypass; the exact boundary is now pinned by `test_analyze_code_injection_risk_drops_past_function_call_args_cap` (`0.7` at 256 chars, `0.5` at 257) so it cannot silently widen. Separately, a specific claim relayed forward from an earlier review round, that `foo("a" * 257)` flips `is_threat` `True` to `False`, does not reproduce against `SusPatternsManager.detect()` with default config: it is `is_threat=False`, `threat_score=0.0` in both the pre-fix and post-fix tree, since neither the regex layer (no keyword to match) nor the semantic layer (score never crosses 0.7 for a generic name in either tree) ever flags it; that specific claim is treated as refuted rather than carried forward. Both detection benchmarks were re-run again against the fully fixed tree, with `-s` to capture exact figures rather than trusting the floor/ceiling assertion: unchanged at 293/327 recall and 24/335 false positives, and 281/314 recall and 23/331 false positives, both async and sync.
- **`SemanticAnalyzer.extract_tokens` spun up ten fresh single-worker `ThreadPoolExecutor` instances per `analyze()` call (five `attack_structures` patterns, twice per call: once via `analyze_attack_probability`, once directly for `token_count`), each wrapped in a 0.1s timeout that could never fire on a stuck regex.** A CPython regex match holds the GIL for its duration, so a worker thread running one cannot be preempted by `future.result(timeout=...)`; the timeout only ever fires between operations, never during one, making it pure constant overhead rather than protection, the same fake-mitigation shape as two other sites already found this release. Removed outright, with no replacement timeout: the four patterns' own bounds (now including `tag_like`'s structural bound above) are what keeps every `attack_structures` regex linear, so the wrapper was never load-bearing. Measured first-party, 2000 iterations per sample: `extract_tokens` per-call cost on a short benign string went 0.4385ms -> 0.0099ms (44x), on a repeated-JSON sample 0.5733ms -> 0.0374ms (15x), on an XSS payload 0.5072ms -> 0.0085ms (59x); the surviving cost matches the amount of actual regex work per sample, no longer dominated by ten thread-pool spin-ups. `test_extract_tokens_timeout`, which mocked `concurrent.futures.ThreadPoolExecutor` around code that no longer touches it, is replaced with `test_extract_tokens_has_no_thread_pool_executor`, asserting the executor is gone from the source rather than testing removed behavior.
- Cosmetic naming check: `attack_structures`' fourth key is `url_pattern`, matching every reference to it in `extract_suspicious_patterns` and `_get_structural_pattern_boost`; there is no separate `url_scheme` pattern anywhere in this file, so no rename was needed.

- **The `tag_like` fix above is a general technique worth naming, not a one-off: bound the regex scan WINDOW to where a match could possibly terminate, not the pattern's own length.** This exact defect class has already shipped twice in the guard ecosystem under two separate CVEs, in this order: [GHSA-j47q-rc62-w448](https://github.com/rennf93/fastapi-guard/security/advisories/GHSA-j47q-rc62-w448) was catastrophic backtracking from an unbounded quantifier (fastapi-guard <=3.0.0); the 3.0.1 patch for it replaced the unbounded quantifier with a `{0,N}` length cap (`<script[^>]*>` to `<script[^>]{0,100}>`), and that cap itself became [GHSA-rrf6-pxg8-684g](https://github.com/rennf93/fastapi-guard/security/advisories/GHSA-rrf6-pxg8-684g) / CVE-2025-54365: an attribute string past the 100-char cap made the whole pattern stop matching, so padding a real `<script>` payload past the cap silently defeated detection. A `{0,N}` cap cannot satisfy both advisories at once -- it is linear (fixes the backtracking) but it is also a length cliff (reintroduces the bypass), one CVE traded for the other depending on which side of the cap an attacker lands on. `_tag_scan_window`'s `content[: content.rfind(">") + 1]` has no such tradeoff: it bounds the SEARCH SPACE the regex engine may consider, not the length of what it may match inside that space, so the pattern itself stays fully unbounded (`<[^>]+>`, no cliff, no bypass) while the cost of a start position that cannot possibly reach a `>` drops from an O(n) failed match to the cost of one upfront `rfind`. This is the technique to reach for the next time an unbounded `attack_structures` quantifier needs bounding, in this file or elsewhere, in preference to a `{0,N}` cap.

- **Per-pattern cliff audit of the three `attack_structures` patterns that still carry a `{0,N}` length cap instead of the `_tag_scan_window` technique above, measured through the real `SusPatternsManager.detect()` wire path at 1x/2x/10x each bound (not just the isolated regex), specifically to check each for the GHSA-rrf6-pxg8-684g shape: something detected below the cap and silently lost above it.**
  - `function_call` (`\w{1,64}\s*\([^)]{0,256}\)`): a dangerous keyword call (`eval`, `exec`, `system`, `popen`, ...) is `is_threat=True` via `threats=["regex"]` identically whether its argument is 1, 256, 257, 512, or 2560 characters, because the regex-layer keyword pattern (`\b(?:eval|system|exec|shell_exec|passthru|popen|proc_open)\s*\(`) has no length bound of its own and does not depend on this structural pattern; the 256-char args cap is never the deciding factor for any known dangerous call. A generic non-keyword call (`foo(...)`) is `is_threat=False` at every argument length tested, 1 through 2560 characters, in both the base and fixed tree alike -- the cap crossing changes nothing, because this shape was never detected regardless of length (base parity, not a regression). Same for the 64-char name half of the bound: a bare `"a" * 65..641 + "(x)"` call is `is_threat=False` at every length in both trees. The one real effect of this cap is the sub-component score dip already pinned by `test_analyze_code_injection_risk_drops_past_function_call_args_cap` (`code_injection_risk` 0.7 -> 0.5 past 256 chars); it never crosses the 0.7 `semantic_threshold` on either side of that dip, so it never flips a wire-level verdict in any case measured. No known attack shape currently relies on this cap; nothing is silently lost.
  - `path_traversal` (`\.{2,20}[/\\]`): a traversal payload ending in a known sensitive file (`etc/passwd`, `etc/shadow`, ...) is `is_threat=True` identically at 0, 1, 21, 41, and 201 leading dots, because the regex-layer traversal pattern matches on the trailing filename, not on dot count; the 20-dot cap is never the deciding factor for this shape. A bare dot run with no recognized sensitive-file suffix is `is_threat=False` at every length tested, 20 through 200 dots, in both trees -- an app-specific traversal target that is not one of the hardcoded sensitive filenames evades detection regardless of dot count, a pre-existing, base-parity gap in the filename allowlist that this cap neither creates nor worsens.
  - `url_pattern` (`[a-z]{1,32}://`): a known dangerous scheme (`javascript:`, `data:`, `vbscript:`) is caught by a separate regex-layer pattern matching the literal scheme name plus a single colon, not `scheme://`, so it never engages this structural pattern or its cap at all. A generic `scheme://host` shape is `is_threat=False` at every scheme length tested, 4 through 320 characters, in both trees -- this structural signal alone has never crossed the wire-level threshold at any length, capped or not, so the 32-char cap is not currently load-bearing for any known payload.
  None of the three remaining caps currently gates a known attack shape at the wire level: for each, the signal that actually drives detection (keyword, filename, or scheme literal) is independent of the capped structural pattern, or the shape was already undetected below the cap in base too. If a future payload class comes to depend on one of these structural boosts specifically, the fix is `_tag_scan_window`'s scan-window technique, not a wider `{0,N}`.

- **Two independent defects in `integration-test`, the same Makefile target: a Redis prefix that shadowed the test suite's own isolation fix, and a collection path that silently excluded half the suite.**
  - A concurrently running suite's Redis keys could be wiped mid-test by another suite's teardown. `tests/conftest.py:38` defaults `REDIS_PREFIX` to a PID-scoped value (`f"test:guard_core:{os.getpid()}:"`) precisely so two suites running against the same Redis never collide, but an explicit `REDIS_PREFIX` env var still wins over that default, and four entrypoints hardcoded the old shared value `"test:guard_core:"` as that explicit override: `Makefile:241` (`integration-test`), `compose.yml:11` (the `guard-core` service, covering `make test`, `make test-all`, and the five-version matrix), `.github/workflows/ci.yml:128`, and `.github/workflows/detection-gate.yml:68`. Because the shared string is a literal prefix of every PID-scoped one, teardown's `delete_pattern("*")` (`guard_core/handlers/redis_handler.py:277-290`, building `f"{redis_prefix}{pattern}"` and running Redis `KEYS`/`DEL` against it) matched and deleted every concurrent suite's keys too, surfacing as spurious rate-limit/sliding-window failures with no code regression behind them. `Makefile:241` and `compose.yml:11` now pass `REDIS_PREFIX` through only when an operator sets it, instead of substituting the shared string as a default, so the PID-scoped default applies unless deliberately overridden. `compose.yml:11`'s bare `REDIS_PREFIX` host-environment passthrough (no `=`, no default) needed no further work and was reverified end to end -- `docker compose config` and a live `docker compose run` process reading its own `os.environ` both confirm the variable reaches the container exactly when the host sets it, unset, plain, space-containing, and trailing-colon-suffixed values all round-tripped correctly. The Makefile side shipped in this same fix a first time with `${REDIS_PREFIX:+REDIS_PREFIX=$REDIS_PREFIX}`, an idiom that is dead code for any set value: POSIX shells recognize a bare `NAME=value` word as an assignment only at parse time, before expansion runs, so a word that merely expands to look like one afterward is taken as the command name instead, reproduced first-party as `sh: REDIS_PREFIX=myprefix: command not found`.
