The unconstrained shape was measured and rejected first: it regressed the existing benign corpus (`recon_internal_tools_list_multiline`, "confluence\nhelpdesk\njira", false-positived as a `c`-module/callable pair) and collided 28/20,000 on random-byte base64 wrapped at a PEM/MIME 76-column line length; the marker now also requires the shape to end in a REDUCE/BUILD-plus-STOP tail with no space or tab in between (`[^ \t]{0,100}?[Rb]\.`), which a real pickle stream's opcodes always carry and prose never does, taking both false-positive sources to 0/323 and 0/20,000. Ruby `Marshal`'s existing `BAh[Jv]` marker only covered the two top-level types real gadget chains dominantly use (object `o`, ivar-wrapped `I`); the char class is extended to `BAh[Jv7bV]`, adding Array (`[`), Hash (`{`), and user-marshal (`U`), at 0/50,000 random-base64 collisions for the extended class and for each new variant individually. PHP's existing `O:\d+:"` object-injection marker is joined by `C:\d+:"` (`Serializable`, real `phpggc` chains) and `E:\d+:"` (PHP 8.1 enum serialization), the identical syntax shape at the same measured precision. `.NET`'s existing NRBF (`AAEAAAD`) marker is joined by a literal `<ObjectDataProvider\b` marker for the `DataContractSerializer`/`ysoserial.net` XAML gadget shape (`<ObjectDataProvider MethodName="Start">...`), which NRBF never covered since it never touches `BinaryFormatter`. **Non-goal, ruled explicitly: a marker for ASP.NET ViewState/`LosFormatter` (`/wEPDw`, the base64 form of the `\xFF\x01\x0F\x0F` `ObjectStateFormatter` header) was designed and measured clean (0/50,000 random-base64 collisions) but dropped.** Unlike the other three, that header is not an attack signature, it is emitted by every default ASP.NET WebForms page regardless of tampering; a benign, never-touched ViewState and a MAC-forged one carrying an `ObjectDataProvider` gadget are byte-identical at the pattern layer; only the page's MAC signature tells them apart, and verifying it needs the server's validation key, which guard-core does not have and is not positioned to acquire. Shipping the marker would flag effectively all ASP.NET WebForms traffic as `deserialization`, not the tampered subset; this is disclosed as a stated gap rather than shipped silently. `deserialization` recall on the pattern-layer benchmark rises from 6/6 to 12/12 detected (`cshutil\nrmtree\n(S'/tmp/x'\ntR.`, base64 Ruby `Marshal` Array and Hash dumps, a PHP `C:` `Serializable` payload, a PHP `E:` enum payload, and the `ObjectDataProvider` XAML gadget join the existing six); the benign corpus stays at 323 cases and 0 new false positives.

- **Four engine-wide preprocessor/truncation evasions closed in `ContentPreprocessor` and `_read_capped_body` (`guard_core/detection_engine/preprocessor.py`, `guard_core/utils.py`), affecting every detection category, not just `deserialization`: an adversarial review confirmed each lets an otherwise-caught payload of ANY category through.** (1) A newline or MIME line-wrap inside a base64 blob (`\n`/`\r\n` splitting a long base64 run, the canonical PEM/76-column wrap) defeated `_BASE64_RE`, which required the whole run to be contiguous alphabet characters; the regex now tolerates embedded `\r\n` between characters, and the matched token has its whitespace stripped before `base64.b64decode`. Verified clean against real multi-line content that must NOT be corrupted by this widening: PEM certificates, JWTs, multi-line source, base64 log dumps, and a 13KB benign JSON array all stay undetected, and a genuine PEM cert now decodes as ONE merged token instead of one attempt per 64-char line, which measured slightly FASTER (-20% to -23%) than before on that shape; on unrelated non-newline content the extra whitespace-strip and gzip-magic check cost ~230us absolute on a 5KB payload, noise-level. (2) Gzip-then-base64 (`base64(gzip(payload))`) evaded entirely, since the preprocessor never gunzipped: a decoded blob starting with the gzip magic (`\x1f\x8b`) is now inflated (`zlib.decompressobj`, output bounded to 8192 bytes) before the UTF-8/printable-ratio checks run. Bounding the output cap alone was not enough: a 256KB body packed with hundreds of small, cheaply-constructed compressible chunks forced hundreds of bounded decompressions per request, measured at 583ms for one call before a second guard, a per-decode-pass attempt budget (8 gunzip attempts), cut that to 31ms; a single massive bomb (50MB of zeros to a 65KB base64 blob) was already bounded at ~8ms by the output cap alone. (3) A payload placed past `detection_max_content_length` (10000 chars, independent of the `detection_max_body_inspect_bytes` body-read cap) survived being read but was silently dropped by `truncate_safely` whenever it fell in the gap between the end of the fixed 262144-byte scan window `_MAX_FULL_SCAN_BYTES` hardcoded and wherever `detection_max_body_inspect_bytes` had been raised to, since that scan window never tracked the knob: content at or under the scan cap is fed to the downstream matcher whole, unwindowed, while content over the cap is scanned for the 25 coarse `attack_indicators` substrings first, keeping any hit region (with surrounding context, budgeted to the cap), and a body with no coarse-indicator hit anywhere falls back to a head-plus-tail cut, the leading `cap - 4096` bytes plus the last 4096 bytes, dropping everything in between; a boolean-blind `OR '1'='1'` marker sitting in that dropped middle, invisible to the coarse-indicator list, demonstrated the gap independent of any single category, and it stayed open even after `detection_max_body_inspect_bytes` was deliberately raised well past 262144 specifically to scan further, because the preprocessor's own scan cap stayed pinned at 262144 regardless. `ContentPreprocessor` now takes its scan cap from the caller instead of hardcoding it, and `SusPatternsManager` wires it to `detection_max_body_inspect_bytes` at both the singleton and the per-request preprocessor construction sites, defaulting to the same 262144 bytes when the knob is left at its default so existing behavior there is unchanged, and following the knob up to its documented 10485760-byte ceiling otherwise, so raising `detection_max_body_inspect_bytes` now genuinely extends whole-body scan coverage that far and closes the mid-body gap for any body up to the configured cap. Scanning more bytes in full costs more, but that cost stays deterministic as the cap grows: every built-in pattern is individually verified non-catastrophic on adversarial input (`tests/test_detection/test_builtin_pattern_safety.py`), custom patterns still run through `detection_compiler_timeout`'s per-pattern wall-clock cutoff regardless of content size, and the semantic analyzer still only ever sees a separate `detection_max_content_length`-sized slice, unaffected by how large the full-scan cap is; the tradeoff is now disclosed on `detection_max_body_inspect_bytes` itself, raising it costs CPU time as well as memory per inspected request. (4) `_read_capped_body` treated a Content-Length header that was present but malformed (`abc`, `-1`, `0`) identically to one that validly declared a too-large body, `return None` before ever trying the adapter's bounded prefix reader, even though the missing-header case already fell through to that same reader successfully. It now falls through on `parsed is None` the same way missing-header does; only a validly-parsed, too-large value still skips (the existing, unchanged, documented memory-bound tradeoff). Zero perf/FP cost, a pure control-flow fix. A companion depth increase, iterative base64 decode's `max_decode_iterations` raised from 7 to 16 to cover deeper realistic nesting (verified detecting nested-base64 depths 1 through 16, was 1 through 7; the loop already exits early once content stops changing, so this costs nothing on ordinary, non-nested content) surfaced a real compounding-cost bug while raising it: the gzip-attempt budget above was originally reset every decode iteration, so raising the iteration cap multiplied the worst-case decompression cost (16 x 8 = 128 attempts instead of 7 x 8 = 56); a printable-decompressing adversarial 256KB body that forces the full iteration budget measured 444ms with the naive per-iteration reset. The budget is now shared across the whole `decode_common_encodings` call (one list threaded through every iteration) instead of reset per pass, capping total gunzip attempts at 8 regardless of iteration count or candidate count; the same adversarial body now measures 79ms. Covered end-to-end via `detect_penetration_attempt`, enhanced-mode, real pipeline, no mocks, by a new dedicated test file, `tests/test_detection_engine/test_encoding_truncation_evasion.py` (and its generated sync mirror), plus internal-branch coverage added to `tests/test_sus_patterns/test_preprocessor.py`; 100 percent line and branch coverage on every added/changed line in both files, both trees. Not in scope for this change and left as-is: an adapter that supplies neither a Content-Length header nor `read_body_prefix` still skips body scanning entirely, a pre-existing, tested, documented capability gap (the adapter has no way to hand guard-core any bytes), not a logic bug this change touches.
- **`_BASE64_RE`'s `{20,}` character-run floor left every SHORT base64-wrapped payload undecoded, silently evading every detection category, not just `deserialization`.** `ContentPreprocessor._decode_base64_candidates` (`guard_core/detection_engine/preprocessor.py`) only treats a run of 20-or-more base64-alphabet characters as a decode candidate; a payload whose encoded form falls under that floor is never handed to `base64.b64decode` at all, so its plaintext attack signature never reaches the pattern set. `1' OR '1'='1` (12 bytes) base64-encodes to a 16-character blob, single- or double-encoded (double is 22 non-padding characters, above the old floor, but its OUTER decode just reproduces the still-too-short 16-character inner blob, which then also falls through); both stayed undetected end-to-end (`detect_penetration_attempt`, enhanced/production `SecurityConfig()`, `is_threat=False`) before this change. The floor is lowered from `{20,}` to `{12,}`, chosen by measurement rather than guesswork: a synthetic 2034-case corpus of realistic short base64-ish benign strings across 84 categories (UUIDs dashed/undashed, git SHAs at 7 and 40 hex characters, MD5/SHA hex hashes, numeric IDs, alphanumeric session tokens, product keys, JWT header/alg fragments, PNG/JPEG base64 fragments, API keys, strong/weak/bare ETags, CSP nonces, slug fragments, recovery codes, tracking numbers, spanning lengths 4 to 25) was run through the real production `SusPatternsManager.detect()` pipeline at every threshold from the 20-character baseline down to 4; false positives stayed at exactly 0/2034 at every single threshold measured, including 4 (the number of items that become decode CANDIDATES rises from 14 at 20 to 110 at 4, but none of them ever flips `is_threat` true). The existing 323-case pattern-layer `BENIGN_CORPUS` and the 66-case `BASE64_CORRUPTION_CORPUS`/JWT positive-control corpus (`tests/test_preprocessor_base64_corpus.py`) were re-run the same way: 0 new false positives at every threshold, JWT segments still decode to their claims at every threshold. `{12,}` was kept as the floor rather than descending further to 10/9/8/6/4, all of which also measured 0 FP: it sits below the 16-character minimum the reported gap actually needs, giving margin for slightly shorter payloads, while every threshold below it bought no additional measured attack coverage yet kept multiplying decode-candidate churn (46 candidates at 12 vs. 110 at 4 in the synthetic corpus) and per-call preprocessing cost for no offsetting benefit. Full pattern-layer benchmark (`test_detection_benchmark.py::test_detection_benchmark_recall_and_false_positive_rate`): malicious recall RISES from the 256/302 baseline to 268/302 (twelve previously-undetected base64-wrapped malicious corpus cases now decode and detect), the false-positive rate holds EXACTLY at the existing 21/323 baseline (zero new false positives), wall time 10.741s against the 30-second ceiling (64% headroom). Full end-to-end benchmark (`test_detect_penetration_attempt_benchmark.py::test_detect_penetration_attempt_recall_and_false_positive_rate`, all seven delivery mechanisms -- `raw_body`, `form_body`, `json_body_nested`, `multipart_body`, `header`, `query_param`, `url_path` -- through `detect_penetration_attempt`, enhanced/production config, no Redis required): malicious recall RISES from the 209 baseline to 254 (forty-five more previously-undetected base64-wrapped malicious payloads now detected across delivery mechanisms), zero UNDOCUMENTED new false positives (the reported 17/319 are all pre-existing, disclosed known-false-positive cases; the test's `assert not unexpected_false_positive_case_ids` passed), wall time 27.016s against the 70-second ceiling (61% headroom, roughly 43 seconds to spare). Perf, measured directly: `ContentPreprocessor.preprocess()` on a synthetic 5KB/10KB body packed almost entirely with short base64-alphabet tokens (UUIDs, hex runs, alphanumeric session tokens, digit runs), an adversarial worst case far denser in decode-candidates than real traffic -- 10KB body measured 1.38ms/call at the old `{20,}` floor, 3.31ms/call at the new `{12,}` floor (roughly 2.4x, still sub-4ms absolute per call). Detect-proof, both layers: `1' OR '1'='1'`, single- and double-base64-encoded (16- and 24-character blobs, both under the old 20-character floor), verified via `detect_penetration_attempt(request, SecurityConfig())`: `is_threat=False` at the old threshold, `is_threat=True` at the new one. Applied to both the async source and its generated sync mirror (`guard_core/sync/detection_engine/preprocessor.py`, `scripts/unasync.py`); no other part of the regex changed. Covered by new tests in `tests/test_detection_engine/test_encoding_truncation_evasion.py` (short single-/double-base64 SQLi marker now detects end-to-end; a representative-category parametrized test -- UUID, git SHA, hex hash, session token, product key, JWT fragment, base64 image fragment, API key, ETag, nonce, numeric ID, slug -- stays clean) and boundary-pinning unit tests in `tests/test_sus_patterns/test_preprocessor.py` (a 12-character base64 run is now a decode candidate, an 11-character run still is not), both with generated sync mirrors; 100 percent line and branch coverage on the changed line in both trees.
- **Three more executable extensions join `file_upload`'s dangerous-extension list: `asmx` (ASP.NET web-service handler, server-executes), `cer` (IIS treats a `.cer` in a web-accessible path as a certificate/script handler target on some configurations), and `phps` (Apache's disabled-PHP-source-view handler, historically re-enabled by misconfiguration to serve source, and a known extension-confusion upload bypass).** `shell.asmx`, `shell.asmx.jpg` (via the double-extension arm), `shell.cer`, and `shell.phps` now detect where they previously did not. `config`, `htaccess`, `inc`, `svgz`, and `pgif` were considered and deliberately left out: `config`/`htaccess`/`inc` are common benign filename fragments (`app.config`, `webpack.config.js`, `.htaccess` itself as a legitimate dotfile mention, `header.inc.php` as a benign include-naming convention) with a high false-positive rate for the value they'd add, and `svgz`/`pgif` are not established executable-upload attack shapes.
- **A file-upload field's Content-Disposition name is attacker-controlled, and the fallback label used when it is absent collided with any admin-configured `excluded_detection_body_fields` exclusion, silently disabling detection.** `_multipart_text_parts` (`guard_core/utils.py`) labeled every part with no real `name=` parameter `"file"`, the same literal string an admin would plausibly add to `excluded_detection_body_fields` to skip legitimate file-upload noise; an attacker who simply omits `name=` from the Content-Disposition header (still a filename-carrying, RFC-tolerated multipart part) inherited that exclusion for free, on every category, regardless of what the part's filename or content actually was. The fix separates the exclusion KEY from the display LABEL: `_multipart_text_parts` now returns a 3-tuple (`exclusion_key, label, value`) instead of 2 (`label, value`), where `exclusion_key` is `None` unless the part carries a REAL Content-Disposition `name`; `_scan_multipart_body` only consults `excluded_detection_body_fields` when `exclusion_key is not None`, so a part scanned under the fallback label is now always scanned, never suppressible by a field-name exclusion, while a genuinely named field (`name="file"`) is still correctly suppressed when excluded. The same nested-`multipart/mixed` shape that already used the fallback label for its inner attachment part (which structurally never carries a `name=` of its own) is affected identically and by the same construction: it too is now always scanned regardless of exclusion configuration. Separately, a leaf MIME part with NO Content-Disposition header at all (name `None` AND filename `None`) was unconditionally `continue`-skipped, dropping its content unscanned even when it carried an attack payload as a bare text part; it is now included under the same fallback label and the same never-excluded rule, so no leaf part's content escapes scanning. Covered by new cases in `tests/test_detection/test_multipart_file_part_scanning.py` and `tests/test_detection_exclusion_integration.py` (the latter's pre-existing `test_multipart_part_without_name_is_skipped`, which asserted the old drop-on-no-name behavior, is renamed `test_multipart_part_without_name_is_scanned` and now asserts detection).
- **The double-extension `file_upload` bypass pattern's benign-terminal whitelist included archive extensions (`zip`, `gz`, `tar`, `rar`, `7z`), false-positiving on ordinary backup and release-artifact filenames.** `backup.sh.tar.gz`, `myapp.war.gz`, `data.py.zip`, and `install.sh.gz` all flagged as `file_upload` bypass attempts purely because their SECOND extension was an already-dangerous type (`sh`, `war`, `py`) and their THIRD extension was an archive format, a shape any CI artifact, database dump, or config backup produces routinely. Archive extensions are removed from the benign-terminal whitelist; it now covers only served-media types the double-extension trick actually targets (images, documents, audio/video), matching how a webshell bypass is delivered in practice, a browser rendering the final extension as content. `shell.php.jpg`, `x.asp.png`, `a.phtml.gif`, `shell.pht.jpg`, and the null-byte/semicolon/trailing-dot truncation variants all still detect, unaffected (the separate unconditional truncation arm, `_FILE_UPLOAD_TRUNCATION_RE`, is untouched by this change). **Disclosed, accepted tradeoff:** a double-extension shape whose final segment is neither a dangerous type nor a served-media type (`shell.php.bak`, `a.jsp.html`, `x.php.svgz`) is not caught by this specific pattern; closing it would require re-adding a whitelist broad enough to swallow the archive false positives this change exists to remove, so it is left as a residual gap rather than chased. `tests/test_sus_patterns/test_file_upload_extension_bypass.py` gains cases pinning all four archive false positives clean and the required-still-detected shapes unaffected.
- **File-part content in a multipart upload is now ALWAYS scanned; the `_looks_like_text` heuristic gate that previously skipped scanning content estimated to be mostly binary is removed outright, because it was evadable: padding a webshell payload with roughly 25 percent random junk bytes pushed the non-printable ratio over the gate's 20-percent threshold, so the content was never handed to the detection engine at all (`<?php system($_GET['cmd']); ?>` plus 31 random bytes measured `is_threat=False` end-to-end before this change).** Scanning is still bounded by the pre-existing `detection_max_body_inspect_bytes` cap, unchanged; only the binary/text heuristic gate is gone. Removing the gate exposed genuine noise: a benign-binary corpus (realistic PNG/JPEG/GIF/PDF/ZIP byte content, plus `os.urandom` and `bytes(range(256))` blobs, assembled in a scratch script and scanned through the real `detect_penetration_attempt` multipart path) surfaced four regex false-positive sources, all now tightened at the root rather than gated behind text-detection: (1) the glued-backtick cmd_injection candidate's inner token had no length bound and no character-class restriction, so two backtick bytes occurring anywhere in a multi-kilobyte binary blob (a near-certainty at that size) captured everything between them as the "token," which almost always contains punctuation and is thus classified an implausible SQL identifier, hence an injection; the token now requires printable-ASCII content (`\A[\t\x20-\x7e]*\Z`) before any further check runs, which every existing real payload (`` `whoami` ``, `` `cat /etc/passwd` ``, `` `rm -rf /` ``, and the ten-command denylist additions) already satisfies by construction, since a real shell command is plain ASCII text. (2) The bare LDAP paren-conjunction pattern (`\(\s*[&|]\s*`, matching `(&`/`(|`) is a two-to-three-byte shape with a nonzero chance of appearing coincidentally in binary noise, but cannot be lengthened or character-restricted without losing the minimal real payload it exists for (`(|(&`, a truncated nested-filter bypass, carries no attribute or `=` at all); it is now gated by a validator requiring the shape to be immediately followed (through optional whitespace) by another paren, `!`, `*`, or an `attr=`/`~=`/`<=`/`>=`-shaped LDAP operator within 40 characters, satisfied by every named malicious case and by pure chance far less often in noise. (3) The LDAP wildcard-equals pattern's word-run (`[\s\d\w]+`) was unbounded and Unicode-`\w`-wide, letting two unrelated positions of the same binary blob, separated by hundreds of noise characters, pair up as a fake attribute assignment; the run is bounded to 32 ASCII word/space characters, ample for any real LDAP attribute name. (4) The file_inclusion bare-`//` protocol-relative pattern accepted a host shape as short as a single alphanumeric character with no dot, so `//` followed by any one alphanumeric byte, a coincidence random binary reaches with base-rate frequency, matched; it now requires at least one dot-separated label beyond the first (a real host shape, `evil.com`/`evil.io`), which every protocol-relative RFI test case already carries and a bare `//x`/`//abc` no longer matches. No fifth pattern was found in the constructed corpus, though a wider (~280-sample) statistical sweep surfaces additional very-low-probability matches from a handful of other patterns (glob-wildcard, quote-splice, dollar-substitution, and two template-injection shapes) on random binary content. Running text-detection patterns over arbitrary binary bytes has an unbounded low-probability false-positive tail, so this residual is an accepted, disclosed limitation rather than chased to zero; operators who accept binary uploads on a route can exclude those fields via `excluded_detection_body_fields`. All four tightened fixes were verified to keep their real-positive coverage (existing `test_cmd_injection_corpus.py`, `test_detection_benchmark.py`, and `test_file_inclusion_corpus.py` cases pass unchanged) while the full benign-binary corpus now measures zero threats. Removing the text gate also exposed a second, unrelated false-positive source in a different subsystem: `SemanticAnalyzer.detect_obfuscation` (`guard_core/detection_engine/semantic.py`) flags content as obfuscated whenever its Shannon entropy exceeds 4.5 bits/char, a threshold calibrated against text-domain obfuscation (base64/hex/URL-encoded payloads, which every existing obfuscation test in `test_semantic.py` uses); raw compressed binary (PNG/JPEG/PDF-stream/ZIP payload bytes) is inherently near-maximum entropy regardless of intent, so real image, PDF, and archive uploads were scored "suspicious" (~0.71, just over the default 0.7 semantic threshold) purely from their compressed content, independent of and in addition to the four regex sources above. A new `looks_like_binary_content` helper (module-level in `semantic.py`, reusing the same printable/replacement-character ratio heuristic the removed `_looks_like_text` gate used) short-circuits `detect_obfuscation` for binary-majority content, and `SusPatternsManager._check_semantic_threats` (`guard_core/handlers/suspatterns_handler.py`) now additionally skips semantic analysis entirely when the RAW pre-preprocessing content is binary-majority (the preprocessor's control-character stripping alone was not sufficient to bring already-preprocessed binary content's ratio back over the gate, so the check runs against the original content instead); a genuinely obfuscated text payload is unaffected, since it is never binary-majority by this measure. Detect-proof after both fixes: the padded webshell above now measures `is_threat=True` end-to-end (`cmd_injection`, matched on `system(`), regardless of padding fraction (verified at 50 percent and 80 percent padding); the full benign-binary corpus (PNG, JPEG, GIF, PDF, ZIP, `os.urandom`, `bytes(range(256))`) measures zero threats, regex and semantic combined. Covered by new cases across `tests/test_detection/test_multipart_file_part_scanning.py`, `tests/test_sus_patterns/test_cmd_injection_corpus.py`, `tests/test_sus_patterns/test_ldap_corpus.py` (new file), `tests/test_sus_patterns/test_file_inclusion_corpus.py`, and `tests/test_sus_patterns/test_semantic.py`; the now-dead `_looks_like_text` function and its tests are removed rather than left unreachable.

- **A non-UTF-8 request body skipped the ENTIRE body scan, in every released version, an all-category detection bypass: appending or prepending a single invalid byte (`\xff`) to any attack body was enough to evade every body-delivered pattern.** `detect_penetration_attempt` (`guard_core/utils.py:1489`, and its generated sync mirror `guard_core/sync/utils.py`) decoded the capped body bytes with plain `body_bytes.decode()`, strict UTF-8, inside a `try`/`except Exception: return _build_detection_miss()`; any byte sequence that is not valid UTF-8 raised `UnicodeDecodeError` and the except swallowed it, returning a clean miss before `_scan_request_body` ever ran, regardless of what the body actually contained. The decode is now lossy: `body_bytes.decode("utf-8", errors="replace")`, so invalid bytes become U+FFFD replacement characters and the surrounding attack text is still scanned; the now-unreachable `try`/`except` was removed rather than left as dead code (a lossy `errors="replace"` decode on `bytes` cannot raise). Detect-proof, before/after, via the real pipeline (`detect_penetration_attempt(request, SecurityConfig())`, enhanced mode, no Redis): a latin-1 body carrying `1' OR '1'='1` (`é`-containing text encoded latin-1, invalid as UTF-8), a body opening with `\xff\xfe` before a `<script>alert(1)</script>` XSS payload, a valid SQLi payload with one trailing `\xff` byte, and a binary blob with an embedded `rO0AB` (Java-serialized, CWE-502) base64 marker all measured `is_threat=False` before this change and `is_threat=True` after, on all four. False positives, measured 0 new: a real PNG binary upload (raw bytes, not base64), a latin-1 body of accented benign text (`café résumé naïve`), a deterministic protobuf-shaped binary blob, a deterministic pseudo-random binary blob (SHA-256 digest stream, not `os.urandom`, to keep the case reproducible), and a body of nothing but invalid bytes (`\xff` x 50, decoding to 50 replacement characters and nothing else) all stay `is_threat=False`; the replacement character (U+FFFD) itself matches no built-in pattern. Perf, measured directly (`bytes.decode()` vs `bytes.decode("utf-8", errors="replace")`, 20000 iterations on a 60KB body at the default inspection cap): no measurable cost, within timing noise. This is a CWE-693 (Protection Mechanism Failure) class bug: the failure mode of a decode step silently disabled the security control meant to inspect the data, rather than falling back to a safer scan; a GHSA is being drafted separately. Covered by a new dedicated test file, `tests/test_utils/test_non_utf8_body_scan.py` (and its generated sync mirror), plus a rename of the pre-existing `tests/test_utils/test_body_inspection_cap.py::test_at_cap_body_invalid_utf8_is_read_but_not_scanned` to `..._is_read_and_scanned_lossily`, whose old name asserted the bug's own behavior; 100 percent line and branch coverage on the changed line in both trees. The same strict-decode-then-silently-skip shape existed a second place: the `honeypot_detection` decorator's trap-field check (`guard_core/decorators/advanced.py:73,83`, and its generated sync mirror) decoded the request body with plain `raw_body.decode()` inside `except Exception: pass`, so a non-UTF-8 body carrying a filled trap field was never even parsed and the honeypot silently failed to fire. Both the form and JSON branches now decode with `errors="replace"`; the form branch's `try`/`except` was removed as dead code (`parse_qs` on a `str`, confirmed by direct testing, never raises), the JSON branch keeps its `except` since `json.loads` can still legitimately reject non-JSON text. Detect-proof: a form body (`trap_field=bait\xff`) and a JSON body (`{"trap_field": "bait\xff"}`), both invalid UTF-8 with the trap value glued to the corrupt byte, now return a 403 instead of `None`. Covered by two new tests in `tests/test_decorators/test_advanced_edge_cases.py` (and generated sync mirror) plus a rename of the pre-existing `test_honeypot_form_exception_caught`, whose old name and body asserted a decode exception being swallowed, to `test_honeypot_form_non_utf8_body_without_trap_value_not_blocked`; the valid-body positive and negative paths for both content types were already covered by `tests/test_decorators/test_mixins.py` and are unchanged by this fix. 100 percent line and branch coverage on `guard_core/decorators/advanced.py` in both trees.

- **Encoded path-traversal separators are now detected by comparing the decoded view against the raw view, at any encoding depth or scheme, instead of by an enumerated list, in `path_traversal`.** `_PATH_TRAVERSAL_ENCODED_DOT_RE`, the only `path_traversal` pattern that existed, required an encoded dot pair immediately followed by a literal `/`; any encoding of the separator itself, or any single-segment (`..X` rather than `..X..X`) shape, evaded detection even when the exact same preprocessing pipeline the engine already scans against decodes it to a working `../`. A new check, `SusPatternsManager._check_decoded_view_path_traversal` (`guard_core/handlers/suspatterns_handler.py`), now counts occurrences of `\.\.[\\/]` in the fully-decoded content and in the raw, signal-preserving view (NFKC-normalized only, no percent-decoding) computed for the same request; if the decoded view has more matches than the raw view, decoding revealed a traversal sequence the attacker tried to hide, so it flags `path_traversal` regardless of which encoding or how many layers produced it. A literal `../assets/logo.png` or `../../src/index.js` has the identical count in both views and stays unflagged. To make the decoded view actually contain the separator for encodings the preprocessor previously dropped or ignored, `ContentPreprocessor.decode_common_encodings` (`guard_core/detection_engine/preprocessor.py`) gained two decode steps that benefit every detection category, not just this one: `%uXXXX` (IIS-style percent-u Unicode escapes, e.g. `%u002e` -> `.`, `%u2215` -> the division-slash lookalike) is now decoded, and a lenient overlong-UTF-8 reconstruction runs on percent-byte runs that Python's strict UTF-8 decoder rejects and `urllib.parse.unquote(..., errors="ignore")` was silently dropping, covering the 2-byte (`%c0%af`, `%c1%9c`), 3-byte (`%e0%80%af`), and 4-byte (`%f0%80%80%af`) overlong lead-byte families; a run that decodes cleanly under strict UTF-8 is left untouched, so genuine multi-byte percent-encoded text (`%c3%a9` -> `é`) is unaffected. A Unicode-lookalike renormalization pass added after those steps folds a freshly-decoded separator lookalike (the division slash from `%u2215`, the fullwidth solidus from `%uff0f`) down to a literal `/` or `\` within the same decode iteration. Verified detected, in both `url_path` and `query_param` contexts: single-segment encoded separators (`..%2f`, `%2e%2e%2f`, `..%5c`, `..%c0%af`, `..%c0%2f`, `..%25%32%66`, `..%00/`, `..%252f`, `..%u2215`, `..%uff0f`, `..%c1%9c`, `%c0%ae%c0%ae%c0%af`), the 4-byte overlong `..%f0%80%80%af`, the IIS-Unicode `%u002e%u002e%2f` and `%u002e%u002e%u2215`, and triple/quadruple/quintuple percent-encoded nesting (`%25252e%25252e%25252fetc%25252fpasswd` and two layers deeper), all the way out to the shared decode loop's existing 16-iteration bound (encoding nested past that bound, e.g. 20 layers of `%25`-wrapping, stays an accepted, undetected residual rather than a regression, pinned by a dedicated test, the same ReDoS-safety bound the rest of the preprocessor already used). Verified clean: `..%20file` (decodes to a space, not a separator), `assets%2Flogo.png` and `redirect=%2fdashboard` (no preceding `..`), `a..%2Cb` (a comma, not a separator), and the full benign corpus, 0 new false positives. The `..;/` semicolon path-parameter bypass is unrelated to this mechanism, by construction: a semicolon is never percent- or Unicode-decoded to a path separator, so no decode-based comparison can catch it. It is instead a new, dedicated `dir_traversal`-category pattern, `_PATH_TRAVERSAL_SEMICOLON_SEP_RE` (`\.\.;[/\\]`), added alongside this change. Five prose corpus entries (an NFKC-normalizing ellipsis, a `Loading........//please wait`-style progress indicator, a document section reference, a version string, and a glob query value, all combining a dot run with a double slash) are pinned clean as regression guards for this round's work, even though no pattern in the current set was ever at risk of matching them. On the pattern-layer benchmark: `path_traversal` recall rises from 5/5 to 13/13 detected (the corpus grew by 8 single-segment encoded-separator cases, all detected); `dir_traversal` recall moves from 8/9 to 9/10 (the corpus grew by exactly the one new `..;/` case, which is detected; the pre-existing, unrelated documented gap `dir_traversal_embedded_prose_honeypot_etc_passwd` is unaffected either way). The benign corpus's false-positive count is unchanged, 21/332, across the same three pre-existing attributed categories.
- **Two more benign corpus entries, unrelated to the fix above, newly landed on `url_path` in the end-to-end benchmark's round-robin delivery once the corpus insertions for that fix shifted every later entry's assigned index, the same phenomenon already documented for the kebab-identifier backtick pins.** `ref`user`list` (`cmd_injection_glued_plausible_token_ref_user_list`) is the same ambiguous-gated glued-backtick tradeoff as the kebab-identifier shapes, just a plain word token instead of a kebab one. A JSON body whose only field value is a bare internal source path (`{"path": "/opt/app/worker.py"}`, `sensitive_file_json_payload_ending_source_path`) is a new shape: the end-to-end entry point's embedded-JSON field scanner isolates that value and re-scans it on its own under an unrestricted context, where it is character-identical to the `sensitive_file` bare-path shape that pattern exists to catch; an ordinary file-watch or build-event payload cannot be told apart from a sensitive-file probe by shape alone, and the same JSON body stays correctly benign when scanned as a whole string instead of field-by-field. Both are now pinned in `tests/test_sus_patterns/test_detect_penetration_attempt_benchmark.py`'s known-false-positive table at the `url_path` mechanism where they were measured; neither is caused by, or requires any change to, the fix above.
- **A `VAR=val` prefix before a newline-delivered shell `-c` invocation is now detected in `cmd_injection`, bounded to 8 chained assignments, consistent with the existing newline-shell-`-c` detection posture, and this widens the false-positive surface onto benign CI/crontab/Makefile-style bodies.** `_CMD_INJECTION_NEWLINE_SHELL_DASH_C_RE` accepted an optional `env` token before the shell name but no variable assignment, so `x\nFOO=bar /bin/sh -c id` (and its URL-encoded-newline form, `x%0AFOO=bar%20/bin/sh%20-c%20id`) evaded detection even though the same payload without the assignment already matched. The pattern now accepts `(?:[^=\s;|&]+=[^\s;|&]+\s+){0,8}` between the leading newline and the optional `env`/shell-path segment, one or more `VAR=val` assignments, chained up to 8 deep. The `{0,8}` cap is deliberate, not incidental: an earlier unbounded `(?:...)*` version of this fragment was rejected for a single-request ReDoS, measured at 0.8s per `search()` call against a 10000-char adversarial body (`"\nA=A " * 2000`, the default `detection_max_content_length`) versus 0.005-0.01s for the bounded form, and `cmd_injection` runs inline on the event loop with no timeout. A chain of 9 or more assignments before the shell invocation is a disclosed detection ceiling: it is not matched by this pattern and stays undetected, the deliberate ReDoS-safety tradeoff of the bound. 1, 2, and 8-assignment chains detect; the existing bare, literal-newline, and env-prefixed cases are unaffected. The same env-var-prefixed shell shape is character-identical to bodies that show up naturally in CI/config contexts: a GitHub Actions/CI YAML run step (`steps:\n  - run: |\n      FOO=bar BAZ=qux bash -c 'echo hi'`), a crontab line (`5 4 * * * root\nPATH=/usr/bin:/bin bash -c 'do_backup.sh'`), and a Makefile recipe (`target:\n\tFOO=1 BAR=2 bash -c 'do_thing'`) all now flag `cmd_injection`, and are the kind of shape users allowlist; the widened surface is accepted with honest disclosure rather than narrowing the `{0,8}` bound back and losing recall on the chained assignment-prefixed shell injection shape it catches. All three are pinned in `tests/test_sus_patterns/test_detection_benchmark.py`'s `BENIGN_CORPUS` as documented, accepted false positives under `_ENV_VAR_PREFIXED_SHELL_DASH_C_CI_CONFIG_KNOWN_FP_REASON`, and the benign corpus's false-positive figure moves from 21/332 to 24/335 (`cmd_injection` attribution from 9 to 12) to reflect them honestly. The crontab pin also incidentally exercises a second, pre-existing and unrelated pattern: its `* * *` schedule syntax, once whitespace-normalized, reads as an LDAP filter wildcard-equals shape (`* root PATH=`), so it is pinned under a second reason, `_CRONTAB_VAR_ASSIGNMENT_INCIDENTAL_LDAP_WILDCARD_KNOWN_FP_REASON`, and the `ldap` attribution moves from 0 to 1/335; this collision is not caused by, and required no change to, the `{0,8}` widening itself.
- `DynamicRuleManager._check_rule_expiry` treats a timezone-naive `expires_at` as UTC instead of raising `TypeError` when compared against the aware `datetime.now(timezone.utc)`. A naive value is plausible from a JSON/ISO timestamp with no offset, or an agent-side caller using `datetime.now()`; before this fix the comparison raised on every poll tick, the generic exception handler in `update_rules` swallowed it silently, and the rule never expired. Both trees.

- **`block_cloud_providers`'s `None` default silently drifted to `frozenset()` on explicit-`None` construction, reassignment, `model_copy` update, and on any dynamic-rule rollback or expiry restore that happened to touch the field while it was untouched, found auditing the new assignment-validation entry above.** `_validate_block_cloud_providers_value` mapped `None` to `frozenset()` unconditionally, so `SecurityConfig(block_cloud_providers=None)` disagreed with the field's own unset default (`SecurityConfig().block_cloud_providers is None`), and `DynamicRuleManager._apply_rules`'s rollback and `_check_rule_expiry`'s expiry restore, both of which reapply a `deepcopy` of the pre-push snapshot through that same validator, silently flipped an untouched `None` to `frozenset()` on every rollback or expiry, even when the push never set `block_cloud_providers` at all. Every real consumer already gates on truthiness before use (`cloud_blocking_enabled`, the cloud-provider check in `guard_core/utils.py`, `HandlerInitializer`'s lazy-init and refresh paths, the route resolver), confirmed by grepping every `config.block_cloud_providers` read site, so this was never a crash, only a silent identity and equality drift. `_validate_block_cloud_providers_value` now passes `None` through unchanged; construction, assignment, and `model_copy` update all agree with the unset default post-fix. The pre-existing test that pinned the old `None -> frozenset()` coercion, `test_block_cloud_providers_none_becomes_empty_frozenset`, is renamed to `test_block_cloud_providers_none_construction_stays_none` and now asserts the opposite.

- **Nine detection categories matched only the literal spelling of an attack technique and missed structurally-equivalent spellings of the identical behavior; both `guard_core/handlers/suspatterns_handler.py` and its sync mirror.** Wire-measured through `detect_penetration_attempt(request, SecurityConfig())`, base `26357018`. Every fix adds a new discriminator (assignment vs. read, non-standard external reference, control-character-tolerant scheme comparison, JSON value shape gated on field-nesting, a scoped module/timer/constructor reference, or a closed enumerable syntax family) rather than another literal alias appended to an existing alternation, since a longer alias list only relocates the bypass to the next unlisted spelling.
  - `proto_pollution` matched only a bare `__proto__`/`constructor` token pair and missed `Object.prototype.X = value` assignment and the standard-library `Object.setPrototypeOf()`/`Reflect.setPrototypeOf()` mutation calls. Added `_PROTO_POLLUTION_PROTOTYPE_ASSIGN_RE` (`Object.prototype.<ident>` followed by a bare `=`, not `==`/`===`) and `_PROTO_POLLUTION_SET_PROTOTYPE_OF_RE` (`Object.setPrototypeOf(`/`Reflect.setPrototypeOf(`), the complete closed set of standard JS prototype-mutation primitives. `Object.prototype.toString.call(value)` and `Object.prototype.hasOwnProperty.call(obj, key)` are reads, not assignments, and stay unflagged.
  - `xml` (XXE) required the literal keyword `SYSTEM` and missed `<!DOCTYPE foo PUBLIC "id" "http://evil/evil.dtd">`. Added `_XML_XXE_PUBLIC_EXTERNAL_DTD_RE`, matching a `PUBLIC` doctype whose external identifier is an `http(s)://` URI that is not `w3.org`/`www.w3.org`. The ubiquitous XHTML doctype resolves against the w3.org exclusion and stays unflagged.
  - `xss` `javascript:` scheme detection required the 10 characters contiguous and missed `java\tscript:`/`java\nscript:`/`java\rscript:`, which browsers treat identically per the URL spec's tab/CR/LF stripping. Added `_XSS_JS_SCHEME_CTRL_CHAR_RE` (the literal spelling with an optional `[\t\r\n]*` run tolerated between each letter), registered in `DETECTION_RAW_VIEW_PATTERN_SOURCES` so it runs only against the pre-whitespace-collapse raw view where the control characters still exist. The tolerance is scoped to tab/CR/LF only, never a plain space, so prose mentioning "Java" and "scripted" separately cannot satisfy it.
  - `nosql` quoted-key operators only matched when the value was `""`, `null`, `{`, or `[`, missing bare numeric/boolean operator values (`{"age":{"$gt":100}}`, `{"admin":{"$ne":false}}`, `{"age":{"$lte":18}}`). A first attempt widened the shared value alternation directly and regressed a pre-existing FP pin, `test_nosql_legit_value_not_flagged` (`'the mongo filter {"$gt": 5} returns larger values'`), because a bare, non-nested `"$gt": 5` in prose became indistinguishable from the same shape in an attack. Narrowing to require the operator object nested under a preceding `"<field>":` key fixed that pin but broke a second, more decisive one: `tests/attack_simulation/corpus/benign/legit_json_mongo.txt` is a project-curated false-positive probe list containing `{"filter":{"$gt":5},"limit":20}`, `{"age":{"$gte":18},"active":true}`, and `{"range":{"$lt":100,"$gt":10}}`, all field-nested operator-plus-number shapes representing completely ordinary MongoDB range queries (age filters, pagination, price ranges) that real client applications send as a matter of course; `test_detection_does_not_regress_against_baseline` measured the resulting false-positive rate at 5.4% against a 2% tolerance. `{"age":{"$gte":18}}` (this benign probe) and `{"age":{"$lte":18}}` (the task's own attack example) are the identical shape with the comparison direction reversed; no value-shape or field-nesting regex can tell them apart, because a range comparison on a numeric field is exactly how legitimate NoSQL query filtering works and carries no distinguishing signal from the injection use of the same operators. The fix was narrowed a second time to the one sub-shape that is genuinely distinguishable: a boolean literal (`true`/`false`) as the value of `$ne` or `$eq` specifically, nested under a field key (`"[^"]+"\s*:\s*\{\s*"\$(?:ne|eq)"\s*:\s*(?:true|false)`). Range operators (`$gt`/`$gte`/`$lt`/`$lte`) semantically compare magnitude and are commonly paired with numeric fields in ordinary application traffic; equality operators (`$ne`/`$eq`) paired with a boolean are not a magnitude comparison at all and match nothing but the classic auth-bypass idiom (`"isAdmin": {"$ne": false}`, `"verified": {"$eq": true}`) with no colliding benign usage anywhere in this project's curated corpus. This closes the `{"admin":{"$ne":false}}` case from the original three; **`{"age":{"$gt":100}}` and `{"age":{"$lte":18}}` remain undetected, and stay that way deliberately: closing them would mean flagging the exact query shape a real, currently-shipping benign-corpus probe represents, which is a worse outcome than the miss.** `{"$ne":null}` and `{"$where":...}`-shaped bare (non-nested) payloads were already covered by the pre-existing, untouched row and remain detected. **Numeric range operators (`$gt`, `$gte`, `$lt`, `$lte` with a numeric literal) are not flagged as NoSQL injection: they are indistinguishable from legitimate range queries. Auth-bypass shapes (`$ne` null or boolean, `$gt ""`, `$regex`, `$where`, `$exists`, `$in`) are flagged. Use schema validation or route-level allowlisting for numeric fields.**
  - `file_inclusion` RFI required a literal `=` immediately before the remote URL and missed the identical URL-plus-dangerous-extension payload delivered as a JSON value (`{"template":"http://evil/shell.php"}`). Added `_FILE_INCLUSION_JSON_VALUE_RE`, reusing the existing dangerous-extension alternation but keyed off a JSON string value under one of `template`/`include`/`tpl`/`module`/`layout`, the closed vocabulary of parameter names whose purpose is selecting a server-side resource to load. `{"homepage":"http://example.com/about.php"}`, a display link under a non-inclusion key, is not in that vocabulary and stays unflagged even with the identical `.php` extension.
  - `ssrf` cloud-metadata detection was a literal, fully-qualified hostname list and missed the two bare-hostname aliases real cloud stacks accept unqualified (GCP's bare `metadata`, legacy AWS's `instance-data`). Added `_SSRF_BARE_METADATA_ALIAS_RE`, requiring a `://` scheme delimiter immediately before the bare hostname so the signal is "used as a URL host," not "the word appears." **Known limitation, disclosed rather than silent: the general SSRF-via-DNS class stays unbounded. Any attacker-controlled DNS name can resolve to an internal address at request time, and no request-body pattern can evaluate a DNS answer it never sees. This closes exactly the two named aliases. Use an egress-time resolved-IP check (DNS-rebinding-aware) for fields that accept arbitrary hostnames; pattern matching on the request body cannot close this class.**
  - `cmd_injection` dynamic-exec-alias detection was the literal alternation `eval|system|exec|shell_exec|passthru|popen|proc_open(` and missed 8 aliases across three runtimes performing the identical "hand a string to the OS as a new process" behavior: Node's `child_process.execSync`/`spawn`/`spawnSync`/`fork`; PHP's `assert()` and `create_function()`; Python's `os.execl`/`os.execve`. Added `_CMD_INJECTION_NODE_CHILD_PROCESS_RE` (scoped to a `require('child_process')`/`child_process.` prefix, so bare `fork(` in unrelated code is not enough), `_CMD_INJECTION_PHP_ASSERT_VARIABLE_RE` (`assert(` must be immediately followed by a PHP variable sigil `$`, since PHP's dangerous string-eval path needs a variable/superglobal argument, not a boolean comparison), and `_CMD_INJECTION_PYTHON_EXEC_FAMILY_RE` (the complete, closed 8-member `os.exec*` family). `create_function` was added directly to the bare alternation since it has no legitimate non-dangerous call shape. **Known limitation, disclosed rather than silent: this family is explicitly unbounded. Node's `execFile`/`execFileSync`, Ruby's `Kernel#spawn`/backticks, Perl's list-form `system`, Go's `os/exec.Command`, PowerShell's `Invoke-Expression`, and any project-local wrapper are all structurally reachable and none are covered; closing the residual needs runtime/language context this engine does not have, not a wider regex. Use a language-aware static analyzer or a runtime sandbox for code paths that execute external processes.**
  - `code_injection` dynamic-dispatch detection matched only `getattr(os|subprocess|builtins|importlib, "<method>")(` and missed the identical indirection through dict-style attribute access, `vars(__import__('os')) ['system'](...)`. Added `_PY_VARS_INDIRECTION_RE`, built from the same module/method constants the existing `getattr` pattern already uses. `vars(config) ['DEBUG']` fails both constraints (module and method) at once and stays unflagged. **Known limitation, disclosed rather than silent: `os.__dict__ ['system'](...)`, `globals() ['os'].system(...)`, `operator.attrgetter(...)`, and further chained indirection are all structurally reachable; Python's object model provides no closed enumeration the way `os.exec*` does. Avoid resolving dangerous stdlib callables from request-controlled strings; use an explicit allowlist of callable names if dynamic dispatch is required.**
  - `eval(` dynamic-code-execution detection matched only the literal call and missed six documented equivalents that resolve to the same "compile and run a string" behavior through different JS syntax: `Function('code')`/`new Function('code')` (the Function constructor invoked with a string-literal argument), `window['eval']('code')` (bracket-indirected `eval`), `[].constructor.constructor('code')` (the universal `.constructor.constructor` gadget that resolves to `Function` from any built-in value without ever spelling "Function"), and `setTimeout('code', 0)`/`setInterval('code', 0)` (implicit eval via a string first argument). Added four constants: `_JS_DYNAMIC_EVAL_FUNCTION_CTOR_RE` (`(new )?Function(` followed by a quote), `_JS_DYNAMIC_EVAL_BRACKET_RE` (`['eval'](`/`["eval"](` followed by a quote), `_JS_DYNAMIC_EVAL_CTOR_GADGET_RE` (two consecutive dot- or bracket-notation `constructor` accesses, either notation, immediately called with a quote), `_JS_DYNAMIC_EVAL_TIMER_STRING_ARG_RE` (`setTimeout(`/`setInterval(` followed by a quote as the first argument). All four require an immediately-following string-literal argument, the structural signal that separates "code built from a string" from ordinary use: `Function.prototype.call(...)` and plain `function` declarations/expressions never match (lexically distinct from the `Function`/`new Function` call form), `setTimeout(myCallback, 1000)`/`setTimeout(function(){...}, 100)`/`setTimeout(() => {...}, 100)` never match (no quote immediately follows the opening paren), and a single `obj.constructor.name` or unrelated `obj['someKey'](...)` bracket access never matches (needs the literal token `constructor` or `eval` specifically, twice for the gadget chain). Dot-notation aliasing (`window.eval(...)`, `globalThis.eval(...)`, `top.eval(...)`) was already covered before this fix, since the pre-existing bare `eval(` pattern's `\b` boundary matches after any non-word character including `.`; only the bracket form, the Function-constructor form, the constructor-gadget form, and the string-argument timer form were genuine misses. **Known limitation, disclosed rather than silent: this family has a genuinely unbounded tail and is not fully closed. Any property chain built through string concatenation (`window['ev'+'al']`), a value bound to an alias earlier in the payload (`var x = eval; x(...)`), or a non-literal argument to `Function`/`setTimeout` (`Function(atob(encoded))`, a call expression rather than a bare quoted string) evades every pattern above, because none of these can be resolved without evaluating the expression, which a static regex cannot do. The fix closes what four fixed JS-language-level primitives make structurally visible in source text (all 6 given corpus cases, including the constructor-gadget chain); it does not and cannot close arbitrary data-flow-obscured references to the same primitives. Use a Content-Security-Policy that disallows `unsafe-eval` as the enforcement layer for browser-rendered content; pattern matching cannot resolve an expression it does not evaluate.**
  - Every new/modified pattern across all nine families was probed for ReDoS linearity directly (adversarial no-match input at 25k/50k/100k/200k characters drawn from each pattern's own alternation classes, in-process timing, no external `regex` dependency): all measured 7-9x wall time for an 8x input-size increase, consistent with O(n), and none regressed detection at 2x/10x the length of their own longest literal component. No fix uses a bounded quantifier; every added group is either a fixed-size alternation or an unbounded quantifier against a negated/disjoint character class, the same style every pre-existing pattern in this file already uses.

- **Seven detection categories were scoped to a narrower set of request contexts (`query_param`/`header`/`url_path`/`request_body`) than the attack techniques they detect actually arrive through; both trees.** An audit of all 19 categories' `_CTX_*` context sets, measured on an isolated copy of the pre-change tree (never on the shipping code) against the full `tests/attack_simulation` benign corpus plus the shared 34-case benign set delivered on the specific newly-added context, found eight plausible widenings; one (`sqli` + `url_path`) measured a real false-positive cost (4 SQL-flavored prose strings from the benign corpus newly match once delivered as a path segment) and is excluded, reported, and left alone. The other seven measured zero false-positive delta and are added: `xml` + `query_param` (XXE payloads delivered as a query parameter, e.g. a SOAP/REST endpoint that accepts an XML document reference by URL, were invisible to every `xml` pattern even though the identical payload in a header or body already matched), `xss` + `url_path` (reflected XSS via a rendered path segment), `cmd_injection` + `header`, `ssrf` + `url_path` and `ssrf` + `header`, `template` + `url_path` (SSTI via a rendered path segment, the common Flask/Jinja shape), `deserialization` + `header` (a header or cookie carrying a serialized-object gadget chain). Each addition is pinned with a wire-level `SusPatternsManager.detect(..., context=...)` test proving the payload now detects on the newly-added context specifically (`tests/test_features/test_context_aware_detection.py`, both trees), not merely asserted from the context-set diff; `sqli` + `url_path` is pinned the other direction, confirming it still does not fire post-widening.
  - `cmd_injection`'s widening is more than an additive context: the category was internally inconsistent before this change. It is not one context constant — most rows use the base `_CTX_CMD_INJECTION`, but the dedicated Log4Shell JNDI-lookup row already used a separate, wider `_CTX_LOG4SHELL` that includes `header`, because Log4Shell (CVE-2021-44228) is a header attack by definition; the exploit string is classically delivered through `User-Agent`, `X-Forwarded-For`, or any other header a vulnerable logger echoes. So the category already assumed header delivery was real for one row and denied it for every other row in the same category, including every alias added in the nine-families fix above (Node's `child_process` methods, PHP's `assert`/`create_function`, Python's `os.exec*` family, and the bare `eval|system|exec|...` alternation). The base constant now includes `header` too, resolving the contradiction rather than leaving cmd_injection's coverage dependent on which specific row a header-delivered payload happens to match.
  - Re-verified with the full mandatory wire gate on the refreshed 217-row shared corpus (181 attack rows, including 120 evasion-axis rows across 8 payload families and 15 evasion techniques on all five delivery mechanisms, plus 34 benign and 2 documented-limitation rows): zero aggregate and zero per-mechanism regressions, all previously-fixed cases still fixed, and the `xml_xxe_public_no_system(query_param)` per-mechanism partial-detection gap the gate's own evasion-axis reporting had surfaced against the nine-families fix above is now closed.
  - **The widenings broke three tests in `tests/test_sus_patterns/test_detect_penetration_attempt_benchmark.py` (mirrored in the sync tree), and this is a real, worth-recording interaction, not a false alarm the gate happened to catch.** That test assigns each corpus case to a mechanism deterministically, `crc32(case_id) % len(valid_mechanisms_for_category)`, where the valid-mechanisms list is read directly from `CATEGORY_CONTEXT_MAP`. Widening a category's context set changes that list's length, which changes the modulo, which can reassign an existing case to a different mechanism than the one it was previously tested through. Three already-documented, already-accepted benign-corpus tradeoffs (`cmd_injection_prose_semicolon_bare_shell_control`, `template_fp_date_hash_brace`, `template_fp_call_branch_map_arrow`, each carrying its own `known_false_positive_reason` in `test_detection_benchmark.py`'s shared corpus, explained there in full: dates embedded in template-looking braces, a JS arrow-callback's `.map()` call syntax, and a bare-shell-after-semicolon prose control) landed on `header` and `url_path` respectively, mechanisms the wire test's own per-mechanism false-positive tracking (`_KNOWN_E2E_FALSE_POSITIVE_SOURCES`) had never needed to record for these specific cases before, because those mechanisms were not yet valid for `cmd_injection`/`template` and the crc32 assignment had never landed them there. This is not three new false positives; it is the same three already-accepted tradeoffs becoming visible on an axis the widening newly exposes, exactly the risk this round's own review process was built to catch. Added the three (case, mechanism) pairs to `_KNOWN_E2E_FALSE_POSITIVE_SOURCES`, reusing the existing reason constants rather than writing new ones, so the tracking reflects where these tradeoffs now actually land post-widening.

- **`RateLimitManager.reset()` now also clears `redis_handler`, both trees (`guard_core/handlers/ratelimit_handler.py`, `guard_core/sync/handlers/ratelimit_handler.py`, the sync tree hand-maintained).** Previously `reset()` cleared only `request_timestamps`, leaving the old Redis handle attached; in test contexts this let a mocked handle assigned by one test survive into a later test through the shared singleton, and in production it meant a call to `reset()` did not fully detach a previously-initialized Redis backend. `redis_handler` is now set to `None` at the end of `reset()` in both trees, so a subsequent call must reinitialize it via `initialize_redis()` before Redis-backed rate limiting resumes.

- **`_LDAP_WILDCARD_ATTR_RE`, the attribute-name class inside the `ldap` wildcard-equals pattern (`*attr=`/`=attr*`), had its whitespace tolerance removed instead of relocated, which silently turned four real wildcard-equals shapes into an undetected bypass; the fix restores a bounded gap and replaces the removed tolerance with a finite, named exclusion instead of a case rule.** The pattern used to be `[ \t0-9A-Za-z_]{1,32}`, letters, digits, underscore, and internal space/tab all in one class with no case or position constraint, wide enough that a crontab body's trailing `* root PATH=` (space-normalized) satisfied it and false-positived `ldap`. A prior attempt closed that by requiring a leading letter and dropping whitespace entirely (`[a-zA-Z]\w{0,31}`, glued directly to the `*` with nothing in between), which does stop the crontab false positive, but also silently stopped detecting `* cn=admin`, `*\tcn=admin`, `* cn =admin`, and `*%20cn=admin` (decodes to a space), four wildcard-equals shapes that are real LDAP filter fragments (RFC 4515 puts no whitespace production between `*` and an attribute description, but real payloads, proxies, and copy-pasted filters routinely carry incidental whitespace there), and pinned that loss of detection as intended behavior under a test constant name that asserted its own justification. Losing detection silently is worse than the false positive it avoided. A second attempt restored the gap and closed the crontab false positive with a case-convention discriminator (require the attribute token to contain a lowercase letter, on the theory that RFC 4519 attribute types are lowercase/camelCase and shell/cron env vars are conventionally all-caps) and disclosed `*CN=admin` as a residual evasion. That discriminator is rejected here before shipping: LDAP attribute names are case-insensitive (RFC 4512's attribute-description grammar), so `*CN=admin` is not a residual edge case, it is the exact same attack as `*cn=admin`, and a case rule turns detection into a one-keystroke bypass. Disclosing that gap is not sufficient when a same-effort fix exists. The fix that ships: `\*[ \t]{0,4}` restores the bounded gap (covers the space and tab shapes directly; the `%20` shape is covered because the preprocessor decodes it to a space before this pattern ever runs), the attribute class goes back to plain `[a-zA-Z]\w{0,31}` with no case restriction (so `*CN=admin`, `* Cn=admin`, and `*\tUID=*` all detect, closing the case-insensitivity gap the previous attempt disclosed instead of closing), and a case-sensitive negative lookahead, `(?!(?-i:MAILTO|MAILFROM|PATH|SHELL|HOME|LOGNAME|USER|CRON_TZ|RANDOM_DELAY|START_HOURS_RANGE|TZ)\s*=)`, excludes the finite set of reserved cron/shell environment variable names by exact name rather than by case, since none of those names is an attribute in any LDAP schema. `(?-i:...)` is required, not decorative: every pattern in this table compiles under `re.IGNORECASE`, so without it the name list would itself fold case and silently exclude `*path=admin` too; the same scoped-flag technique already used by `_DESERIALIZATION_JAVA_B64_RE` elsewhere in this file. Mutation-tested both load-bearing pieces: removing the lookahead (keeping the gap) makes the crontab one-liner `* * * * * MAILTO=admin@example.com bash -c 'do_backup.sh'` match again; removing the gap (keeping the lookahead) makes all three previously-undetected spaced shapes stop matching again. Verdicts, both trees: all 10 `LDAP_WILDCARD_EQUALS_STILL_FLAGGED` malicious shapes detect (`*cn=admin`, `*uid=admin`, and 8 numeric/word-prefixed variants); all 15 `LDAP_WILDCARD_EQUALS_CRONTAB_SHAPES_NOT_FLAGGED` shapes stay clean (plain/stepped/ranged schedules, `@reboot`, a comment line, a multi-line env block, tab-separated fields, single-line and newline-separated forms, a no-user-field crontab, an inline one-liner, YAML- and JSON-wrapped bodies); the four previously-pinned-as-benign shapes now detect, under a renamed constant, `LDAP_WILDCARD_EQUALS_WHITESPACE_GAP_FLAGGED` (`LDAP_WILDCARD_EQUALS_WHITESPACE_GAP_NOT_A_FILTER_SHAPE_NOT_FLAGGED` is deleted outright, no constant anywhere pins this bypass as intended non-detection anymore); three new pins, `LDAP_WILDCARD_EQUALS_CASE_VARIANT_FLAGGED` (`*CN=admin`, `* Cn=admin`, `*\tUID=*`), detect and guard the case-insensitivity fix against regression. The genuine ldap corpus is unaffected: this pattern still matches none of the benchmark's 12 ldap-category malicious cases (they detect through `_LDAP_WILDCARD_CHAIN_RE` and `_LDAP_PAREN_CONJUNCTION_RE` instead, unchanged), and the benign corpus's `ldap` false-positive attribution is 0/335, overall benign total unchanged at 24/335; `BASELINE_MALICIOUS_DETECTED_TOTAL` (281) and `BASELINE_BENIGN_FALSE_POSITIVE_TOTAL` (24) both hold with no reconciliation needed, on both `test_detection_benchmark.py` trees.

- **`_LDAP_WILDCARD_EQUALS_RE` is repurposed from a bare wildcard-equals matcher (`*attr=`/`=attr*`) into the whitespace-tolerant LDAP breakout matcher, closing the exact defect the round above disclosed and left open, and structurally retiring the crontab exclusion list instead of extending it.** Measured on the round-7 tree, real detection path, enhanced mode: `*)(cn=admin` detected (via `_LDAP_WILDCARD_CHAIN_RE`), but `* ) ( cn=admin`, `*)\n(cn=admin`, `*)\t(cn=admin`, `* )( uid=*`, and `*)(&` all missed, because `_LDAP_WILDCARD_CHAIN_RE` (`\*\)[|&]?\(+\s*[a-zA-Z][\w-]*\s*=`) is glued between the closing and opening parens with no gap tolerance, so any whitespace inside the breakout defeats it, and a bare `*)(&` has no trailing attribute for that pattern to anchor on at all. `_LDAP_WILDCARD_EQUALS_RE` becomes `\*\s*\)+\s*(?:[|&!]\s*)?\(+\s*(?:[&|!]|[a-zA-Z][\w-]*\s*[=~<>])`: it requires the wildcard to be followed by a closing paren and a subsequent opening paren, tolerating whitespace at every gap, and the clause that follows is either another boolean operator (`&`, `|`, `!`) or an attribute comparison (`=`, `~=`, `<=`, `>=`). Requiring parens is deliberate: a breakout has to construct or escape filter structure, and a crontab body never carries one, so the crontab false-positive class and the entire exclusion mechanism (`_LDAP_WILDCARD_CRONTAB_VAR_NAMES_RE`, `_LDAP_WILDCARD_NOT_CRONTAB_VAR_RE`, `_LDAP_WILDCARD_ATTR_GAP_RE`, `_LDAP_WILDCARD_ATTR_RE`) are deleted rather than extended; the fifteen crontab benign shapes stay clean because they were never paren-shaped, not because a name list excludes them. Both both-direction residuals the prior round disclosed and shipped anyway (the exclusion list not being exhaustive of every scheduler's reserved variable, and the exclusion list itself being a same-name-different-case evasion for `USER`/`PATH`/`HOME`/`SHELL`/`TZ`) are closed as a consequence, not worked around: there is no name list left to be incomplete or to evade. Checked the exact starting-point pattern against the hardened ReDoS validator (`PatternCompiler.validate_pattern_safety` in the sibling ReDoS-hardening unit) before shipping it: verdict `True, "Pattern appears safe"`, so the unbounded `\s*` ships as proposed; a bounded `\s{0,8}` sibling was validated too as a fallback but was not needed. Mutation-tested: dropping the six `\s*` gaps (gluing the pattern back to the old shape) turns exactly the six gap-dependent rows red again (`* ) ( cn=admin`, the tab and newline gap variants of `*)(cn=admin`, and the space, tab, and newline gap variants of `*)(&`) while the already-glued rows (`*)(cn=admin`, `*)(&`, `admin*)((|userpassword=*)`) and the unrelated `*))%00` null-byte row stay exactly as they were, confirming the whitespace tolerance is load-bearing for those six and incidental to everything else. Timed 10 KB, 50 KB, and 100 KB of `*) ( ( ( ` repeated garbage in a killable subprocess: wall time scaled linearly with input size (no quadratic or exponential blowup) for both the shipped pattern and the bounded fallback. Requiring parens has a precision cost the paren-free pattern never had: a benign log line quoting a complete, already-balanced two-clause filter such as `(objectClass=*)(department=Sales)` now satisfies the breakout shape too, since a wildcard used as a legitimate equality value in a closed `(attr=*)` clause is indistinguishable at the regex level from the same wildcard used to break out of one. `_LDAP_WILDCARD_CHAIN_RE` already carries a candidate-rejection validator for exactly this ambiguity, `_ldap_wildcard_chain_is_injection`, which rejects a match when the text immediately before the wildcard is itself a complete `(attr=` clause opener; `_LDAP_WILDCARD_EQUALS_RE` is wired through the same validator rather than a new one, since both patterns match starting at the wildcard and the disambiguation is identical. Found and fixed before shipping, not disclosed as a residual: the two `ldap_ops_log_chained_filter_*` benign corpus cases stay clean, and the fix does not cost any of the eight probe rows or the eighteen new pins, since none of their wildcards sit immediately after a complete attribute clause. The ten synthetic `LDAP_WILDCARD_EQUALS_STILL_FLAGGED` pins (`*cn=admin`, `*uid=admin`, and eight numeric/word-prefixed variants) are removed rather than converted to not-flagged: a bare `*attr=` with no paren is a substring filter, not a breakout, was never the shape this pattern exists to stop, and is not asserted clean either, since nothing in this change claims those bare shapes are benign, only that pinning them as this pattern's job was wrong. The genuine ldap corpus stays at 12/12 detected (all twelve already route through `_LDAP_WILDCARD_CHAIN_RE` or `_LDAP_PAREN_CONJUNCTION_RE` independently of this pattern), the fifteen crontab shapes stay at 15/15 clean, and `BASELINE_MALICIOUS_DETECTED_TOTAL` (281) and `BASELINE_BENIGN_FALSE_POSITIVE_TOTAL` (24) hold unchanged on both `test_detection_benchmark.py` trees.

- **Two `ldap` breakout shapes the round above left uncovered are now detected: extensible-match attribute syntax (RFC 4511 s4.5.1, `attr:dn:matchingRule:=value`) and a close-then-open breakout with no wildcard at all.** Measured before this round: `*)(cn:=admin` and `*)(cn:dn:2.5.13.2:=admin` missed, because every attribute follower in this file required `[=~<>]` directly after the identifier, and extensible match puts a colon-delimited matching-rule chain there instead; `admin)(cn=x)` and `admin)(&)` missed too, because both wildcard-anchored patterns (`_LDAP_WILDCARD_CHAIN_RE`, `_LDAP_WILDCARD_EQUALS_RE`) require a literal `*` before the breakout, and neither shape has one. A third shape, `*)(|(cn:caseExactMatch:=admin`, already detected through the boolean-operator arm of `_LDAP_WILDCARD_EQUALS_RE`, confirming only the bare-attribute arm needed the extensible-match fix. The attribute follower is now a single named constant, `_LDAP_ATTR_EXTENSIBLE_MATCH_RE` (`[a-zA-Z][\w.-]*(?::(?:dn|[\w.-]+))*\s*:?=`), used identically by `_LDAP_WILDCARD_CHAIN_RE`, `_LDAP_WILDCARD_EQUALS_RE`'s bare-attribute arm, and `_LDAP_PAREN_CONJUNCTION_FOLLOWUP_RE`, so the three cannot silently diverge on what an attribute looks like again; matching now requires an identifier, zero or more `:dn`/`:matchingRuleOid` segments, optional whitespace, an optional colon, then `=`, closing both `cn:=admin` (bare extensible equality) and `cn:dn:2.5.13.2:=admin` (DN-scoped, OID-qualified) without a name list. The no-wildcard shape gets a new pattern, `_LDAP_PAREN_BREAKOUT_RE` (`\)\s*\(\s*(?:[&|!]|[a-zA-Z][\w.-]*(?::[\w.-]+)*\s*:?[=~<>])`), matching any closing-paren-then-opening-paren transition into a boolean operator or an attribute comparison, with no wildcard precondition; it keeps `[=~<>]` rather than switching to the shared extensible-match constant because it was tuned separately and accepts a bare `[&|!]` right after the open paren, which `admin)(&)` needs and the shared constant does not provide. `_LDAP_PAREN_BREAKOUT_RE` is routed through the existing `_ldap_wildcard_chain_is_injection` candidate-rejection validator, the same one `_LDAP_WILDCARD_CHAIN_RE` and `_LDAP_WILDCARD_EQUALS_RE` already use, rather than a new mechanism. Reusing it unmodified was tried first and failed: `_LDAP_ATTR_BEFORE_WILDCARD_RE` (`\(\s*[a-zA-Z][\w-]*\s*=\Z`) only recognized a clause ending exactly at `=` (the shape produced when a wildcard-anchored pattern's match starts at the wildcard itself, so the value is never in the prefix), and `_LDAP_PAREN_BREAKOUT_RE`'s match starts at the closing paren instead, so the prefix for a genuinely closed clause like `(objectClass=*)` ends in `=*`, which the unmodified helper did not match, reintroducing the exact false positive on `(objectClass=*)(department=Sales)` the prior round had just closed. Widening the helper to `=\*?\Z` (optional trailing wildcard) fixed that specific pair but broke a different, pre-existing, out-of-scope test: `tests/test_sus_patterns/test_ldap_conjunction_breakout.py::test_benign_payloads_do_not_fire_ldap` pins `(uid=alice)(objectClass=user)` and `(uid=alice)(cn=bob)`, two complete non-wildcard multi-clause filters, as clean, and `_LDAP_PAREN_BREAKOUT_RE` fired on both since neither's closing clause ends in `=` or `=*`. The helper is now `\(\s*[a-zA-Z][\w-]*\s*=[^()]*\Z`: a clause opener followed by any run of non-paren characters up to the match position, which subsumes both prior shapes (zero-width and single-wildcard) as special cases of "the value contained no unescaped parens" and additionally recognizes an arbitrary literal value as a closed clause; a bare value with no preceding `(` at all, `admin` in both `admin)(cn=x)` and `admin)(&)`, still fails to match since there is no `(` in the prefix, so recall on both malicious rows is unaffected. Mutation-tested: reverting the helper to `=\Z` makes all four benign clause shapes (the two round-8 wildcard cases and the two newly-found non-wildcard cases) fire again, unchanged; reverting `_LDAP_ATTR_EXTENSIBLE_MATCH_RE` to the pre-round `[a-zA-Z][\w-]*\s*=` makes `*)(cn:=admin` and `*)(cn:dn:2.5.13.2:=admin` stop matching on both `_LDAP_WILDCARD_CHAIN_RE` and `_LDAP_WILDCARD_EQUALS_RE`; dropping the `[&|!]|` alternative from `_LDAP_PAREN_BREAKOUT_RE` makes `admin)(&)` stop matching while `admin)(cn=x)` is unaffected, confirming both alternation arms are load-bearing independently. Checked both new/changed patterns against the hardened ReDoS validator (`PatternCompiler.validate_pattern_safety`, both this tree's own copy and the sibling ReDoS-hardening unit): all verdicts `True, "Pattern appears safe"`. Timed on the two intersecting garbage shapes the patterns actually share structure with, `)( ` runs (stresses `_LDAP_PAREN_BREAKOUT_RE`'s two adjacent bounded gaps) and `cn::::::` runs (stresses the extensible-match colon-group), across four size doublings (12.5 KB/25 KB/50 KB/100 KB) in a killable subprocess: wall time scaled linearly (roughly 2x per doubling) for every pattern on both shapes, no blowup. Verdicts, both trees: all five previously-missing rows detect, plus spaced and `%20`-encoded gap variants of each (fifteen total new pins across `LDAP_WILDCARD_BREAKOUT_EXTENSIBLE_MATCH_FLAGGED` and `LDAP_PAREN_BREAKOUT_NO_WILDCARD_FLAGGED`); the benign false-positive delta is zero on both candidate-rejection mechanisms and both trees, the two round-8 chained-clause benign cases and the two newly-found non-wildcard multi-clause benign cases in `test_ldap_conjunction_breakout.py` all stay clean, all fifteen crontab shapes stay clean, and `test_detection_benchmark.py`'s own printed figures are unchanged on both trees: `ldap` recall 12/12, `ldap` false-positive attribution 0/335, total recall 293/327, total false-positive rate 24/335.

- **`_ldap_wildcard_chain_is_injection`, the shared candidate-rejection validator all three `ldap` breakout patterns route through, is replaced end to end: `\(\s*[a-zA-Z][\w-]*\s*=[^()]*\Z` false-positived on any ordinary two-clause text (`(a)(b=1)`, `$(cmd)(x=1)`, prose parentheticals) because it only ever checked "is there a `(` somewhere earlier," not whether that `(` was still open at the breakout point.** `(a)(b=1)`: the first `)` is matched by the `(` two characters earlier, so `admin`-style bare values and `(a)`-style balanced ones both search-matched the same helper and were indistinguishable. Replaced with a local, signed paren-depth scan over a bounded window ending at the breakout `)`: `depth <= 0` (no open paren locally available to match this close) is the injection signal, not "a `(` exists somewhere in the prefix." The scan must be LOCAL, not whole-body: an attacker who parks an unrelated `(` in an unrelated field (`{"note": "(hi", "user": "admin)(cn=x)"}`, `?note=(hi&user=admin)(cn=x)`) makes a whole-prefix count read balanced and suppress a real breakout in a different field. The backward scan stops at `"`, `'`, newline, or `&` (field/value boundaries an LDAP filter string cannot itself carry unescaped) and deliberately does not stop at `=`, since every clause contains one and stopping there would re-flag `(uid=alice)(objectClass=user)`. An attacker cannot evade the local scan by balancing the close within their own value: RFC 4515 gives `(` and `)` no unescaped meaning inside a filter string, so a self-supplied `(uid=(admin)(cn=x))` is a parse error at the directory, not a bypass; the depth check is the structural definition of a breakout, not a heuristic an attacker can step around. The scan window is bounded to 40 characters each direction (`_LDAP_BREAKOUT_LOCAL_SCAN_CHARS`, the same bound this file already uses for `_LDAP_PAREN_CONJUNCTION_LOOKAHEAD_CHARS` and `_BACKTICK_CONTEXT_WINDOW_CHARS`): an unbounded backward scan measured genuinely quadratic on a dense, boundary-free `)( ` run (`(a=1)` repeated) and timed out past 20 seconds at 50 KB before the bound was added; bounded, the same input completes in under 100 ms at 100 KB, linear. A second, independently-discovered gap in the depth rule alone: an attacker can supply BOTH parens of a wildcard clause, `(cn=x*)(cn=admin)`, so the local depth reads matched (the `)` after `x*` is closed by the `(` before `cn=`) and the rule wrongly suppressed a genuine injection, confirmed parseable as a real multi-clause RFC 4515 filter via `ldap3`'s own filter parser (`(&(objectClass=person)(cn=x*)(cn=admin))` parses cleanly, so this is exploitable, not a parse error). A second acceptance path was added for this: the local window ending in a non-empty value immediately before the wildcard, `=[^()]+\*\s*\Z` (`_LDAP_BREAKOUT_WILDCARD_CLAUSE_END_RE`), also counts as a breakout. The `+` is load-bearing, not `*`: a bare, empty-valued wildcard clause like `(objectClass=*)` or `(uid=*)`, the exact shape the prior round's `(objectClass=*)(department=Sales)` and `(uid=*)(status=active)` benign guards depend on, also ends in `=*` and would trivially satisfy an unbounded `[^()]*`, silently reintroducing the false positive those two pinned benign cases exist to prevent; `[^()]+` requires an attacker-supplied non-empty prefix (`x`, `abc`, `admin`, `a@b.com`) before the star, which a legitimate bare-existence wildcard never has. Depth alone (even with the wildcard-clause addition) still false-positived on truncated prose carrying a genuinely unmatched close paren with no dangerous content after it (`request completed) (status=200`, `fi) (VAR=value)`, `note) (dept=sales`, `yeah that works) (mode=auto`): these are locally identical to `admin)(cn=x)` under the depth rule alone, because an ordinary added clause is not itself dangerous, only a wildcard, a boolean operator, a null byte, or a nested paren makes one.
