A second, independent condition now gates acceptance alongside the depth condition: an attack token, `\*|\(\s*[&|!]|\x00|\(\s*\(`, must also be present in either window. Two adjacent traps in wiring this in: the token search must use a SEPARATE forward stop-set that excludes `&` (the backward stop-set includes `&` as a field boundary, but reusing it forward truncates `admin)(&)`'s tail before the `&` token is ever read, silently losing that row), and the wildcard-clause condition must itself double as a token source rather than only a depth-bypass (`*)(cn=admin` and `(cn=x*)(cn=admin)` both carry their `*` in the backward window, not the tail, so a token search restricted to the forward window alone goes silent on both). `admin)(cn=x)` is now deliberately not detected: it carries no attack token (no wildcard, no boolean operator, no nested paren, no null byte), base (`d588e92c`) never detected it either, and `ldap3` parses its wrapped form (`(&(objectClass=person)(cn=admin)(cn=x))`) as an ordinary AND-narrowing clause, not a tautology, since it can only add a constraint, never remove one; it is pinned clean under `LDAP_PAREN_BREAKOUT_INERT_BARE_ATTRIBUTE_NOT_FLAGGED` with a reason naming both facts (inert as an injection, and parity with base) rather than any wording that reads as an intended bypass. Mutation-tested: dropping the depth-vs-wildcard-clause `+`/`*` distinction re-flags both round-8 bare-wildcard benign guards; dropping the attack-token requirement re-flags all four truncated-prose benign rows; using the backward (not forward) stop-set for the forward token scan makes `admin)(&)` stop matching; searching only the forward window for tokens (not the backward window too) makes `(uid=abc*)(cn=admin)` stop matching. Checked the new depth/token/clause-end regexes against the hardened ReDoS validator: verdict `True, "Pattern appears safe"`. Timed dense `)(` runs across four size doublings (12.5 KB/25 KB/50 KB/100 KB) in a killable subprocess on four shapes: no attack token present (all candidates rejected after only the bounded backward scan), and three shapes where every candidate is accepted (both the backward and forward bounded scans run on every match) using a wildcard tail, a boolean-operator tail, and a mix; all four scale linearly, roughly 2x per doubling, 100 KB completing in under 200 ms even with tens of thousands of accepted candidates. Verdicts, both trees: all shapes from every round's required set detect (the five round-9 rows, the eight-row round-8 probe, the twelve-case genuine corpus, the prefixed-wildcard-clause set, `admin)(&)` and its field-boundary-hidden variants); all benign guards stay clean (the fifteen crontab shapes, the two round-8 bare-wildcard chained filters, the two round-9 non-wildcard multi-clause filters in `test_ldap_conjunction_breakout.py`, the local-balance set, and the four truncated-prose rows); `test_detection_benchmark.py`'s own printed figures are unchanged on both trees: `ldap` recall 12/12, `ldap` false-positive attribution 0/335, total recall 293/327, total false-positive rate 24/335; the attack table shows zero new misses.

- **`_LDAP_ATTR_EXTENSIBLE_MATCH_RE`'s `(?::(?:dn|[\w.-]+))*` group let an attacker turn a sub-100-byte request body into an unbounded, uncancellable event-loop hang, introduced by the round above.** The alternation's two branches overlap completely: `[\w.-]+` already matches the literal text `dn`, so every `:dn` segment in the input can be attributed to either branch independently, giving `n` such segments `2^n` equivalent parses that all reach the same point and all fail together when the input has no trailing `=`. Measured through the real `detect()` API, payload `"*)(a" + ":dn"*n + "X"`: `n=18` (59 bytes) 0.14s, `n=20` (65 bytes) 0.54s, `n=22` (71 bytes) 2.16s, `n=24` (77 bytes) 7.76s, doubling roughly every two repeats, textbook exponential. Base `d588e92c` has no `:dn` alternative in this position at all and does not hang. Not contained by the compiler timeout: this is a built-in category pattern, so `_check_regex_pattern` calls `create_async_safe_finditer_matcher(inline_safe=True)`, which runs `compiled.finditer(text)` directly on the asyncio event loop with no timeout, no thread, and no cancellation path; a CPython regex holds the GIL until it returns, so the 2-second budget is cosmetic here, it only gets checked (and logged) after the blocking call already finished. The `dn` branch was always redundant, `[\w.-]+` already accepts it as ordinary text, so the fix removes it rather than bounding the repetition: `_LDAP_ATTR_EXTENSIBLE_MATCH_RE` is now `[a-zA-Z][\w.-]*(?::[\w.-]+)*\s*:?=`, one alternative per segment, no ambiguity, no exponential parse count. Re-measured after the fix, same payload and API: `n=18` 0.008s, `n=20` 0.067s, `n=22` 0.105s, `n=24` 0.069s, no growth trend; four size doublings at `n=1000/2000/4000/8000` (3-24 KB) stayed under 0.6s with no super-linear trend either. `PatternCompiler().validate_pattern_safety` was checked on all three relevant forms and is not sufficient evidence by itself here: the standalone attribute pattern reports `(False, 'Pattern timed out...')` both before and after this fix (a separate, pre-existing, non-catastrophic quadratic cost from the leading `[a-zA-Z][\w.-]*` needing to backtrack when no terminator follows, unaffected by the alternation fix and not reachable through any of the three composed patterns that require a literal `*)(`/`)( ` prefix first), and both composed patterns (`_LDAP_WILDCARD_CHAIN_RE`, `_LDAP_WILDCARD_EQUALS_RE`) report `(True, 'Pattern appears safe')` both before and after, because the validator's generic probe strings never contain the mandatory `*)( ` prefix and so never reach the vulnerable branch at all; the real-`detect()` timing table above is the actual evidence, not the validator verdict. A killable-subprocess regression test, `test_builtin_ldap_extensible_match_redos_protected_in_enhanced_mode`, is added next to the existing template-ReDoS guard in `tests/test_detection/test_builtin_redos_protection.py` (both trees), asserting `detect()` returns within 2 seconds on `"*)(a" + ":dn"*30 + "X"`; on the pre-fix pattern this payload would exceed the deadline by roughly two orders of magnitude. All 166 pre-existing LDAP corpus tests and both benchmark baselines (`BASELINE_MALICIOUS_DETECTED_TOTAL=281`, `BASELINE_BENIGN_FALSE_POSITIVE_TOTAL=24`) hold unchanged on both trees.

- **`_ldap_wildcard_chain_is_injection`'s backward paren-depth scan silently treated running out of its 40-character scan window the same as proving the closing paren is unbalanced, false-positiving any legitimate multi-clause filter whose opening paren sits 40 or more characters back, introduced by the round above.** The backward loop stops for two structurally different reasons that the old code could not tell apart: it hit a genuine field boundary (a quote, a newline, `&`, or the true start of the string, all of which make the accumulated `depth` authoritative), or it simply exhausted its 40-character budget with the real opening paren still unseen further back (`depth` at that point is a truncation artifact, not a fact). `depth <= 0` treated both cases identically as proof of a breakout, short-circuiting straight past `_LDAP_BREAKOUT_WILDCARD_CLAUSE_END_RE`, the check that would otherwise have recognized the clause as closed. Exact off-by-one, `filler = "(" + "x"*k + ")(cn=*)"`: `filler=38` and `filler=39` (still within the 40-char window) correctly read `is_threat=False`; `filler=40` and `filler=41` (the real `(` now sits outside the window) flip to `is_threat=True`, and the same flip fires on ordinary prose, `"log_cleanup: (Cleanup completed after scanning all directories for old files older than thirty days per policy)(pattern=*.log)"`, which base returns `False` on. Root fix: distinguish "scanned back and found balance" from "hit the window bound and do not know." `_ldap_breakout_backward_window` (split out of the validator as its own function, see below) now also tracks whether the loop stopped because `position` fell below `backward_start` while `backward_start` itself was still greater than zero, meaning real text existed before the window that was never examined, versus falling off the true start of the string (`backward_start == 0`), which is a complete, authoritative scan regardless of length. Only the former marks depth as unresolved; when unresolved, `depth <= 0` no longer short-circuits to true, so the wildcard-clause-end check and, if that also fails, the `not depth_or_wildcard_clause: return False` exoneration path run exactly as they would have with a full scan. Re-checked after the fix, same table: all four filler values and the prose case now correctly read `is_threat=False`, through the real `detect()` API. No new benign flags: the fix only removes a false-positive-inducing short-circuit and never adds a new acceptance path, so every payload that depended on `depth <= 0` being reachable within the 40-char window (all of them, since every existing corpus payload is well under 40 characters end to end) is completely unaffected; confirmed by the unchanged 166/166 corpus pass rate and unchanged benchmark totals below.

- **A comparator-based LDAP breakout (`~=`, `>=`, `<=`) was undetected in base and in this lane, pre-existing, closed here per standing policy that pre-existing gaps get fixed, not carried forward.** `admin)(cn~=x`, `admin)(sn>=A`, and `admin)(userPassword>=A` all matched `_LDAP_PAREN_BREAKOUT_RE`'s shape (its attribute-follower class already accepts a bare `~`, `<`, or `>` in `[=~<>]`) but were rejected by the shared candidate-rejection validator: the attack-token gate, `\*|\(\s*[&|!]|\x00|\(\s*\(`, recognizes a wildcard, a boolean operator, a null byte, or a nested paren, but nothing about a bare comparator, so all three payloads reached `_ldap_wildcard_chain_is_injection` and were exonerated for lack of an attack token even though the shape is a real, remotely-exploitable blind LDAP injection primitive. `_LDAP_BREAKOUT_ATTACK_TOKEN_RE` now also matches `~=`, `>=`, and `<=` as attack tokens. This cannot reintroduce a false positive on the two wildcard-anchored patterns that share this validator (`_LDAP_WILDCARD_CHAIN_RE`, `_LDAP_WILDCARD_EQUALS_RE`): both require a literal `*` immediately before the breakout, and that `*` is always captured inside the same backward window the token search already scans, so those two candidates were already accepted via the `\*` alternative regardless of this addition. Grepped every benign string in both LDAP corpus files and the 335-entry benchmark benign corpus for `~=`, `>=`, `<=`: zero occurrences, so this closes with no benign cost. Verdicts, real `detect()` API: all three comparator payloads now flag `ldap`; the full clean set (`(objectClass=*)(department=Sales)`, `(uid=*)(status=active)`, `(cn=*)(cn=admin)`, `admin)(cn=x)`, the bare extensible-match single-clause shapes) stays clean.

- **RFC 4515 backslash-hex escapes bypassed every LDAP pattern, pre-existing, closed here per the same standing policy.** `admin\29\28cn=\2a` and `*\29\28cn=admin` (`\29`=`)`, `\28`=`(`, `\2a`=`*`, the filter-escaping mechanism RFC 4515 defines) both returned `False`: `ContentPreprocessor._HEX_ESCAPE_RE` only ever matched `\xNN` (backslash, literal `x`, two hex digits), never RFC 4515's bare backslash-plus-two-hex-digits, and no LDAP-specific unescape existed anywhere in the pipeline, so an escaped payload never reached any `ldap` pattern in decoded form. Fixed at the root named in the defect, the preprocessor: a new `_LDAP_HEX_ESCAPE_RE` (`\\([0-9a-fA-F]{2})`) and `_decode_ldap_hex_escapes`, wired into `decode_common_encodings` next to the existing `_decode_hex_escapes` call, on both trees' `ContentPreprocessor`. No collision with the existing hex/unicode decoders: `\x` and `\u` are never matched by the new pattern because `x` and `u` are not hex digits, so `\x29`/`)` are fully consumed by their own decoders first and leave nothing for the new one to see; a doubly-escaped payload (`\5c29`, `\` then literal `29`) resolves correctly across the existing 16-iteration decode loop, since decoding `\5c` to a literal backslash on one pass produces a fresh `\29` for the next pass to decode to `)`, the same iterate-to-fixed-point mechanism every other escape family already relies on. Scoped like `_decode_hex_escapes`: applied in the shared decode pipeline, not gated to the `ldap` category, matching this file's existing precedent that escape decoding is general and pattern matching is what's category-specific. Grepped the entire test suite for any bare backslash followed by two hex digits outside an existing `\x`/`\u` escape: zero hits, so this introduces no benign-corpus risk. Verdicts, real `detect()` API: both escaped payloads now flag `ldap` (the wildcard-escaped one decodes to the already-covered `*)(cn=admin` shape; the comparator-adjacent one decodes to `admin)(cn=*`, carrying its own `*` attack token); the full clean set is unaffected, since decoding an absent escape sequence is a no-op. **Reconciled against the wire entry (`detect_penetration_attempt`), not the sub-component, after the "Verdicts" line above was found to have been measured only through `sus_patterns_handler.detect()`.** A bare-script call to `detect_penetration_attempt` with no prior `sus_patterns_handler.configure()` leaves the singleton in its legacy default (`preprocessor=None`), where no decode of any kind runs, `_decode_hex_escapes` and every other existing decoder included, so both payloads measure `False` there; this is a property of the cold singleton, not of this decoder, and matches what the module-level defect corpus (`defect_corpus.py`) reports for both base and this change. Real deployments never hit that state: every framework adapter's middleware setup runs `HandlerInitializer._configure_detection`, which calls `sus_patterns_handler.configure(config)` before the first request, putting the singleton into the same enhanced state the test suite's `_force_enhanced_detection_singleton` fixture forces. Under that state, both payloads flag `ldap` through the full wire entry across all five body-delivery mechanisms (raw, form-encoded, multipart, JSON, query param), and the same holds for a doubly-escaped variant, uppercase hex digits (`\2A`), and escaped forms of the comparator payloads this same round fixed (`cn~=`, `sn>=`, `userPassword>=`); reverting the decoder while leaving everything else in place turns all of those back to `False` through that same wire entry, confirmed directly. A benign corpus modeled on the shapes most likely to collide, hex-sharded cache and object-store directory paths (`.git/objects/3f/a9...`, npm's `_cacache`, Docker's `overlay2`), ANSI terminal color codes (`\033[31m`), and C-style octal string escapes, produced no new detections through the wire entry even though the decoder visibly rewrites that content (`\033` decodes as hex `\x03` plus a literal `3`, `\3f` as `?`), because the rewritten control-character runs never happen to land on a pattern shape. Kept on that basis; a wire-level pin (`test_rfc4515_hex_escape_fires_through_wire_entry`, both trees) now exercises `detect_penetration_attempt` directly instead of the sub-component, verified red-first by disabling the decoder and confirming all four cases (both original payloads plus the double-escape and uppercase-hex generalizations) drop to `False` before restoring it.

- **`_ldap_wildcard_chain_is_injection`'s backward depth scan had a length cliff, the CVE-2025-54365 shape: `"x" * n + "*)(uid=*"` detected at every `n` on base but stopped detecting at `n=40`, the exact value of `_LDAP_BREAKOUT_LOCAL_SCAN_CHARS`, both trees.** The validator backs `_LDAP_WILDCARD_CHAIN_RE`, `_LDAP_WILDCARD_EQUALS_RE`, and `_LDAP_PAREN_BREAKOUT_RE`; it scans up to 40 characters backward from a matched close-paren to decide whether that paren closes something opened locally (benign, e.g. `(objectClass=*)(department=Sales)`, a complete clause followed by another) or breaks out of the surrounding filter with nothing local to balance it (an attack). When the scan exhausts the 40-character window without finding a real boundary, it could no longer tell "there may be an opener beyond the window" (the `(xxx...41 chars...)(cn=*)` false-positive case a prior round fixed by *not* treating that as proven) apart from "there is no opener at all, just attacker-controlled junk" (this defect): both produce the identical signature, depth staying at 0 with the window exhausted, so the fix for the first case silently reintroduced the second as a bypass. The distinguishing signal was already available and unused: `_LDAP_WILDCARD_CHAIN_RE` and `_LDAP_WILDCARD_EQUALS_RE` both require a literal `*` immediately adjacent to the matched close-paren by construction of the regex itself, an attack token proven present regardless of anything further back, whereas `_LDAP_PAREN_BREAKOUT_RE` requires no such adjacency and genuinely needs the ambiguity-conservative behavior to avoid the false positive. `_ldap_breakout_backward_window` now returns the raw `depth` and `depth_unresolved` separately instead of a single pre-collapsed boolean; the caller computes `depth <= 0 and (wildcard_adjacent or not depth_unresolved)`, where `wildcard_adjacent = match.group().startswith("*")`. For wildcard-adjacent matches, an unresolved window with a non-positive depth now still counts as proof, so `_LDAP_BREAKOUT_LOCAL_SCAN_CHARS` still bounds the per-position regex work (the ReDoS-safety reason the window exists) without also capping how far the wildcard breakout may sit from content start. The `(objectClass=*)(department=Sales)` case and the `filler_38/39/40/41 + )(cn=*)` false-positive-avoidance case are unaffected: both resolve depth to a positive value (a real local opener is found) or route through the non-wildcard `_LDAP_PAREN_BREAKOUT_RE` path, neither of which this change touches. `admin)(uid=*` (no adjacent `*`) is deliberately left alone: it is undetected on base at every length, so it can never register as a regression, and it is out of scope for this fix. Pinned at 39 (below the cliff, already passing), 40 (exactly the old cliff), 41, and 200 characters of leading junk, through `detect_penetration_attempt`, both trees, verified red-first by reverting to the prior single-boolean logic and confirming 40/41/200 drop to `False` while 39 stays `True`.

- **`_ldap_wildcard_chain_is_injection` is split into three functions to keep cyclomatic complexity within the project's xenon ceiling after the depth-truncation fix above pushed it from B (10) to C (12).** `_ldap_breakout_backward_window(text, close_paren_pos)` owns the backward scan and returns `(backward_window, depth_proves_breakout)`; `_ldap_breakout_forward_window(text, close_paren_pos)` owns the forward scan; `_ldap_wildcard_chain_is_injection` now only combines their outputs with the wildcard-clause-end and attack-token checks. All three land at rank A; behavior is unchanged, confirmed by re-running every proof above after the split. Both trees.

- **The `xss` event-handler pattern and all four `file_upload` `filename=` sites each required the character immediately after `=` to be a quote or a word character, with no tolerance for whitespace, so real HTML5 whitespace around `=` bypassed both, a confirmed all-version detection bypass.** HTML5 permits whitespace on either side of an attribute `=` and browsers execute it identically. `<img src=x onerror= alert(1)>` (one space after `=`, unquoted value) matched none of the `xss` event-handler pattern's value alternation, which required a quote or a non-space character immediately following `=`. All four `filename=` sites in `guard_core/handlers/suspatterns_handler.py`, the standalone dangerous-extension pattern plus `_FILE_UPLOAD_DOUBLE_EXTENSION_RE`, `_FILE_UPLOAD_TRUNCATION_RE`, and `_FILE_UPLOAD_DECODED_TRUNCATION_RE`, required a quote to sit directly against `=`, so `filename = "shell.php"` matched none of them despite carrying the identical dangerous extension. Both now tolerate a bounded run of whitespace (`\s{0,20}`, a shared `_ATTR_EQUALS_WHITESPACE_RE` fragment) between `=` and its value; the bound keeps the addition linear, an unbounded `\s*` here would repeat a quadratic backtracking shape this repo has already shipped once. All four `filename=` sites route through one shared fragment, `_FILE_UPLOAD_FILENAME_EQUALS_RE`, so the fix could not land in only some of the four. `filename=shell.php` (no quotes at all) stays undetected, unchanged and out of scope, since no site's quoted-value shape was widened, only the whitespace tolerance around `=`.
  A follow-up pass on the whitespace-tolerance fix found it exposed a latent, pre-existing defect in the `xss` event-handler pattern, unrelated to whitespace: the pattern matched `on\w+` (any word starting with `on`), not real DOM event names, so ordinary tokens like `online`, `once`, and `oncall` matched whenever they sat inside a `<...>`-shaped span, and a `<` used as a less-than comparison operator with no closing `>` opened a false "tag" for this pattern and its two `<[^<>]*`-shaped siblings (the `javascript:`/`vbscript:`/`data:` URI pattern and the `style=...expression()` pattern). A real `<` that opens an HTML tag is, per the WHATWG tokenizer's tag-open state, always immediately followed by an ASCII letter or `/`; a new shared `_HTML_TAG_OPEN_RE = r"<[A-Za-z/]"` replaces the bare `<` at the start of all three patterns. `on\w+` is replaced by `_HTML_EVENT_HANDLER_ATTRS`, an explicit allowlist of real HTML/DOM event-handler content attributes, grown across several adversarial passes from an initial 122-name WHATWG-derived set to the verbatim-extracted union of PortSwigger's XSS cheat sheet and OWASP's XSS Filter Evasion Cheat Sheet (200 names total, including `onafterscriptexecute`/`onbeforescriptexecute`, added after a second pass against PortSwigger's live page found them missing). `oncustomthing`-shaped invented words and `onpointerlockchange`/`onpointerlockerror` (real `Document` IDL properties confirmed, in a live Chromium build, to never compile into an executable inline handler) are deliberately excluded and recorded as such in `_HTML_EVENT_HANDLER_ATTRS_PROVENANCE`, a string constant at the definition site that also states plainly that the list goes stale, silently, every time a browser ships a new `on*` handler, and needs periodic re-derivation rather than a one-time fix. `xss` recall on the pattern-layer benchmark rises from 23/23 to 39/39 detected across these passes; `file_upload` rises from 22/22 to 32/32. The benign corpus gains zero new false positives from either category beyond one already-documented case: `file_upload_prose_ticket_dangerous_filename_spaced_equals` (a support-ticket sentence quoting `filename = "invoice.php"`) is pinned as a known false positive, both layers, since no structural discriminator tells a real upload's `filename=` value apart from a quoted example in prose (a proposed discriminator based on what follows the closing quote was tried and reverted: `ContentPreprocessor` collapses a real multipart `\r\n` into a single space before this pattern ever runs, so the discriminator also undetected the genuine delivery it was meant to leave alone). Both trees (`guard_core/sync/handlers/suspatterns_handler.py`, `tests/test_sync/`) mirror every fix in this entry and the new corpus pins byte-for-byte via `scripts/unasync.py`.

- **The `sqli` WHERE-clause structural pattern (`_WHERE_CLAUSE_RE`) fired as a 0.5-weight corroboration signal on any `WHERE col <op> value` regardless of whether the value was a real literal or a bare parameter placeholder, and an unquoted numeric tautology appended to a value (`1 OR 1=1--`, `WHERE id = ?;OR1=1--`) had no detection signal at all.** These were the same defect in both directions, not two separate bugs: the pattern only ever asked whether a WHERE clause existed, never whether what followed it was actually dangerous. An interim attempt to fix this by teaching `_WHERE_CLAUSE_RE` to recognize placeholder markers (`:name`, `@name`, `${...}`, `?`, `%s`, `$1`, `#{...}`) and require an unbounded-but-not-a-placeholder value went through four adversarial rounds, each fixing a false positive by narrowing the marker match and each narrowing reopening a different bypass (a prefix-matched marker admitted `:xOR1=1--`; excluding markers from `LIKE`/`IN` never touched those two keywords' own always-true trailing boundary; and so on) without ever addressing the tautology gap, since a bare `1 OR 1=1--` has no `WHERE` clause at all for any placeholder-aware pattern to inspect. That approach is withdrawn in full; `_WHERE_CLAUSE_RE` is reverted verbatim to `r'(?i)\bWHERE\s+[\w."]+\s*(?:=|<|>|<=|>=|LIKE|IN)\b'`, removing every placeholder-marker construct. Reverting alone fixes the false positive: `SELECT id FROM users WHERE id = 5`, `SELECT name, email FROM customers WHERE active = 1`, and `{"query":"SELECT count(*) FROM events WHERE day = 3"}`, all sent as a raw request body, corroborated `sqli` to exactly the 1.0 `threat_score_threshold` under the withdrawn approach (`_SELECT_FROM_RE` 0.5 + `_WHERE_CLAUSE_RE` 0.5) purely because a literal sat where the withdrawn code expected either a literal or a placeholder; with the trailing `\b` restored, a symbolic operator followed by whitespace (the case with a literal, `id = 5`) no longer corroborates at all, exactly as it did not before any of the withdrawn rounds.
  The missing tautology detection is a new, standalone pattern, `_SQLI_TAUTOLOGY_RE = r"(?i)\b(?:OR|AND)\s*(\d+|'[^']*'|\"[^\"]*\")\s*=\s*\1\b"`, added to the `sqli` category at its default 1.0 category weight so it detects on its own, with no dependency on `_WHERE_CLAUSE_RE` or `_SELECT_FROM_RE` corroborating (`1 OR 1=1--` carries neither `SELECT` nor `WHERE`). It matches an `OR`/`AND` token followed, after optional whitespace, by a value that is then compared back to itself via a backreference: a self-equal digit run or a self-equal quoted string. The leading `\b` requires `OR`/`AND` to start a token (so `COLOR`/`MAJOR`/`ORDER` never match, since the preceding or following character is a letter, not a boundary), but nothing is required between `OR`/`AND` and the value, so the attacker obfuscation of omitting the space (`;OR1=1--`) still matches. The backreference is the reason this cannot flag two distinct column predicates: `status = 1 OR verified = 2` requires the token after `OR` to equal, verbatim, whatever came before the next `=`, and `verified` is not `2`, so it never matches; `active OR admin` has no `=` after `OR` at all. Both are pinned as regression guards (`sqli_boolean_two_columns_or`, `sqli_boolean_bare_columns_or`) alongside the three false-positive cases above (`sql_select_int_compare`, `sql_select_bool_compare`, `sql_json_wrapped`), all five asserting `is_threat is False` through `_PRODUCTION_MANAGER.detect()` directly. All nine required attack shapes now detect through the real `detect_penetration_attempt()` wire path, all five delivery mechanisms (`raw_body`, `form_body`, `multipart_body`, `json_body`, `query_param`): the six numeric tautologies (`1 OR 1=1--`, `1 OR 1=1`, `1 or 1=1`, `5 OR 5=5`, `1 AND 1=1`, `id=1 OR 1=1--`) and the three placeholder-prefixed tautologies (`WHERE id = %s;OR1=1--`, `WHERE id = ?;OR1=1--`, `WHERE id = $1;OR1=1--`), the latter three detecting via the tautology signal alone since the placeholder itself carries no detection weight either way, closing the withdrawn approach's placeholder-specific reasoning without replacing it with a different placeholder-specific guard.
  Four of the twenty benign corpus cases in the shared wire-level harness were already false positives on the pre-`3.13.0` base (`d588e92c`), independent of this fix and unrelated to `_WHERE_CLAUSE_RE`: `SELECT * FROM orders WHERE status = 'shipped'`, `SELECT * FROM u WHERE status = 1 OR verified = 2`, `SELECT * FROM u WHERE active OR admin`, and `SELECT * FROM t WHERE id = #{id}` (MyBatis) all corroborate `sqli` from `_SELECT_STAR_RE` (0.5) plus `_SELECT_FROM_RE` (0.5) both matching the same `SELECT * FROM` substring, reaching 1.0 with no `WHERE`-clause involvement at all. This fix does not reach that mechanism (a `SELECT *`-specific double-count, a different root from the WHERE-clause corroboration this entry addresses) and leaves all four unchanged, measured before and after.
  This round also folds in a crash found while auditing the same code: `SusPatternsManager._normalize_context(None)` raised `AttributeError: 'NoneType' object has no attribute 'split'`. `_normalize_context` now returns `"unknown"` for `None`, the same fallback already used for any other unrecognized context string, without deciding whether `None` is a context callers should ever legitimately pass.
  Every round-2-through-4 test corpus entry and dedicated test built on the withdrawn placeholder-marker design is removed (`_WHERE_CLAUSE_UNBOUNDED_VALUE_SHAPE_PAYLOADS`, `_WHERE_CLAUSE_PLACEHOLDER_MARKER_EXCLUSION_PAYLOADS`, `_WHERE_CLAUSE_PLACEHOLDER_MARKER_EVASION_PAYLOADS`, and their three tests, which measured through `SusPatternsManager.detect()` directly rather than the wire entry point), replaced by two focused parametrized tests, `test_sqli_tautology_detected_regardless_of_placeholder_or_literal` and `test_sqli_where_clause_literal_value_not_flagged`. Two `JS_TEMPLATE_LITERAL_STRINGS_NOT_FLAGGED` pins added for the withdrawn `IN`/`LIKE` placeholder-marker exclusion (`js_template_literal_sql_where_in_interpolation`, `_where_like_interpolation`) are also removed: `_WHERE_CLAUSE_RE`'s `LIKE`/`IN` branches always corroborated regardless of what followed, on the true `d588e92c` base as much as on this lane, since the trailing `\b` after a word-ending keyword is satisfied by the next whitespace character alone; this is a real, pre-existing, disclosed gap unrelated to the symbolic-operator root this fix addresses, and widening `LIKE`/`IN` to also require a literal is out of scope for this round. Pattern-layer benchmark after this fix: `sqli` 36/36 (down from the withdrawn approach's 51/51, by design: every removed pin tested the withdrawn placeholder-marker mechanics), overall recall 328/362, benign corpus 356 cases with 25 documented known false positives, 0 new; legacy (no-preprocessor) singleton smoke 320 malicious detected (`BASELINE_MALICIOUS_DETECTED_TOTAL_LEGACY_SMOKE`, down from 335 for the same reason). End-to-end benchmark (`test_detect_penetration_attempt_benchmark.py`, all seven delivery mechanisms): recall 315/348 (`BASELINE_MALICIOUS_DETECTED_TOTAL`, down from 329), benign 24/352 (unchanged); legacy singleton smoke 309 malicious detected (`_LEGACY_BASELINE_MALICIOUS_DETECTED_TOTAL`, down from 323). Both trees mirror byte-for-byte via `scripts/unasync.py`.

- **The generic pickle `GLOBAL`-opcode marker anchored `deserialization` detection to the start of the scanned content or immediately after a literal newline (`(?:^|\n)c<module>\n<name>\n...`), so any single legal pickle opcode prepended before `GLOBAL` defeated detection while the stream still executed identically.** A real pickle stream is a positional opcode sequence: `GLOBAL` legitimately follows any number of preceding opcodes, not only a line start. `N`, the zero-argument `NONE` opcode, is the simplest bypass byte, confirmed with a recording `find_class`: `Ncshutil\nrmtree\n(S'/tmp/x'\ntR.` still calls `shutil.rmtree('/tmp/x')` under a real `pickle._Unpickler`. A systematic enumeration against the full `pickletools` opcode table, each candidate proven the same way, found 24 further zero/short-argument opcodes with the same property (`NEWTRUE`, `NEWFALSE`, `EMPTY_TUPLE`, `EMPTY_LIST`, `EMPTY_DICT`, `EMPTY_SET`, `MARK`, `PROTO`, `FRAME`, `BININT`/`BININT1`/`BININT2`, `LONG1`, `LONG4`, `(SHORT_)BINSTRING`, `(SHORT_)BINBYTES`, `BINBYTES8`, `BYTEARRAY8`, `(SHORT_)BINUNICODE`, `BINUNICODE8`, `BINFLOAT`, and short opcode pairs such as `NONE`+`BINPUT`); opcodes needing a pre-existing stack or memo entry (`POP`, `DUP`, `GET`/`BINGET`, `PUT`/`BINPUT` alone, `MEMOIZE` alone, `EXT1`-`EXT4`, `PERSID`) correctly cannot precede `GLOBAL` from a fresh unpickler and are not part of the family. The anchor is dropped from the base pattern and replaced with a bounded, non-executing structural validator, `_pickle_global_prefix_is_opcode_stream` (`guard_core/handlers/suspatterns_handler.py`): the up-to-32-byte window immediately preceding a candidate `GLOBAL` match is replayed, one opcode at a time, against a `pickle._Unpickler` subclass whose `find_class`, `get_extension`, and `persistent_load` unconditionally raise before any import or lookup can occur, so a candidate is only accepted as real when that window parses cleanly as a self-contained pickle opcode run from an empty stack; nothing the window contains can ever be imported, resolved, or called, regardless of content. The identifier quantifiers in the base pattern (`_PICKLE_IDENT_RE`, `_PICKLE_DOTTED_MODULE_RE`) are bounded, 100 characters per identifier segment and 20 dotted segments, closing a quadratic-backtracking ReDoS an unbounded version of this pattern measured at 0.365s for a 5000-byte adversarial input; the bounded form measures 0.031s at the production 10000-byte cap and grows linearly.

- **The request/response ingestion boundary decoded invalid UTF-8 inconsistently across four sites, so a security decision reached through one decode path could disagree with the same bytes reached through another; all four are now `errors="surrogateescape"` instead of `errors="replace"` or a hardcoded stdlib default, and two of the four were live bypasses, not merely a consistency gap.** `errors="replace"` collapses every invalid byte onto the same `U+FFFD`, indistinguishable from any other invalid byte and from a genuine `U+FFFD` in the payload; `surrogateescape` maps each invalid byte one-to-one onto its own private-use-adjacent surrogate codepoint (`U+DC80`-`U+DCFF`) instead, so the original byte value round-trips losslessly back through `.encode(..., errors="surrogateescape")` at any point that still needs it.

  **Site 1 (live bypass, confirmed with a before/after delta), `guard_core/utils.py:1505`, `guard_core/sync/utils.py:1542`:** the top-level raw-body decode `detect_penetration_attempt` uses before handing content to every pattern in the engine. Every byte in `0x80`-`0xFF` that is not part of valid UTF-8 became `U+FFFD` before detection ever ran; a `\x88`-prefixed pickle payload (see below) measured `is_threat=False` through this decode and `is_threat=True` once fixed.

  **Site 2 (live bypass, confirmed with a before/after delta), `parse_qsl(raw_body, keep_blank_values=True)` in `_scan_form_body` (`guard_core/utils.py:1142`, `guard_core/sync/utils.py:1140`):** `parse_qsl` defaults to `errors="replace"` when unquoting percent-encoded bytes, so a form field carrying `%88` still collapsed to `U+FFFD` before any pattern saw it, independently of the site-1505 fix; a form-urlencoded delivery of the same attack measured `is_threat=False` even after site 1 was fixed. Now passes `errors="surrogateescape"` explicitly. `parse_qsl`'s output here only ever feeds pattern scanning of the decoded field value, which already handles surrogate-range input the same way the raw-body path does, so no further consumer check was needed.

  **Site 3 (live bypass, confirmed with a before/after delta), `_multipart_text_parts` in both trees:** `part.get_payload(decode=False)`, for a payload string containing surrogates, round-trips it through `bpayload.decode(charset, "replace")` internally with no parameter to override it. `decode=True` was measured and rejected as the naive fix: for a part with no surrogates (ordinary non-ASCII text like `café`) it falls back to `payload.encode("ascii")` then `raw-unicode-escape`, which is not the original UTF-8 encoding and corrupts legitimate accented text that worked correctly before this change. `email.parser.Parser.parsestr` builds each leaf part's payload directly from the already-surrogate-escaped `raw_body` string with no re-encoding of its own, so reading that attribute directly (via `getattr`, to keep mypy's public-API check satisfied without `# type: ignore`) instead of calling `get_payload` at all is both simpler and correct for every case measured: the binary/surrogate case, the legitimate multi-byte UTF-8 case, and the plain-ASCII case, verified against real `email.parser`-constructed multipart bodies, not constructed strings.

  **Site 4 (consistency fix, no live bypass claimed), `guard_core/decorators/advanced.py:72,81` (honeypot trap-field validator, both branches, plus sync mirror):** `_validate_form_data` decoded with `parse_qs(raw_body.decode("utf-8", errors="replace"))`, `_validate_json_data` with `json.loads(raw_body.decode("utf-8", errors="replace"))`. Both now use `errors="surrogateescape"`. Checked, not assumed: `parse_qs`'s output here is only ever tested for dict-key membership and truthiness (`field in data and data[field]`) against caller-supplied trap-field names, never re-encoded or compared byte-for-byte, so collapsing distinct invalid bytes onto the same `U+FFFD` never changed the true/false outcome; `json.loads` on a string containing a lone surrogate parses cleanly (`json.loads('{"a": "\udc88x"}')` returns `{'a': '\udc88x'}`, confirmed directly), and the parsed value is used the same truthy-only way. This path also decodes its own, independently-read body for a self-contained trap-field feature; no other decode of the same bytes exists for it to disagree with, so today there is no differential to exploit, only a latent inconsistency. The existing malformed-UTF-8 regression tests (`test_honeypot_form_non_utf8_body_with_trap_value_is_blocked`, `test_honeypot_json_non_utf8_body_with_trap_value_is_blocked`, `tests/test_decorators/test_advanced_edge_cases.py`) already exercise this path and continue to pass unchanged; no new test was added here because truthiness detection cannot be made to observably differ between `replace` and `surrogateescape`, so no test could discriminate old from new behavior, and a test that cannot fail is not coverage.

  **Site 5 (consistency fix, no live bypass claimed), `guard_core/handlers/behavior_handler.py:211` (`_check_response_pattern`, plus sync mirror):** decoded the upstream response body with `errors="replace"`; now `errors="surrogateescape"`. Checked, not assumed: the decoded string is only ever passed to `json.loads` (parses cleanly on a lone surrogate, confirmed as above), `re.search(..., re.IGNORECASE)`, `str.lower()`, and a plain substring test, all confirmed directly to accept a string containing a lone surrogate without raising and without altering match semantics for ordinary ASCII search patterns. This path decodes a response, not a request, for behavioral rule matching, a data flow disjoint from request-body attack-payload scanning; no security decision here currently spans two differing decode results for the same bytes, so, like site 4, this is a consistency fix rather than a demonstrated bypass.

  Sites 4 and 5 were correctly assessed as inert in the round that fixed sites 1-3, since neither today lets an attacker exploit the inconsistency; inert is not grounds for leaving a fourth of the boundary inconsistent going into release, since a parser fix applied to two of four sites is exactly how the next differential bypass gets built, so all four now agree. The blast radius of `surrogateescape` was independently traced end to end for logging/events/Redis/responses: every consumer of scanned content there either never re-encodes the raw matched text or already serializes through an ASCII-safe path (`json.dumps` with default `ensure_ascii=True` escapes a lone surrogate as literal `\udcXX` text instead of raising). This is the same decode boundary every other raw-view, byte-sensitive detection pattern shares (base64-joined payloads across several other categories go through the identical mangling at site 1); fixing the boundary is necessary but, on its own, not sufficient for those other categories, and auditing or updating their own byte-sensitive patterns to take advantage of the now-consistent boundary is out of scope for this change and remains open. `guard_core/decorators/advanced.py`'s honeypot decode and `guard_core/handlers/behavior_handler.py`'s response decode were previously, incorrectly, described as "untouched, out of scope" for this release; they are not out of scope, and are fixed above.

- **Round-two review found the pickle `GLOBAL`-opcode validator broken in both directions on real traffic, and both are now fixed at their root, depending on the boundary fix above for the false negative.** First, a blocking false negative: before the site-1 fix above, `\x88`-prefixed and every other high-byte family member measured `is_threat=True` when the codepoint was injected directly into a Python string, and `is_threat=False` when the same bytes were decoded the way `utils.py` actually decodes them, so the round-one unit's own corpus and test cases, which injected raw opcode codepoints directly rather than routing them through the real decode call, never actually exercised the code path a real attacker's bytes travel through and overstated coverage as a result. `_pickle_global_prefix_is_opcode_stream` now maps the surrogate range the fixed boundary produces back onto its real byte value instead of failing outright on `UnicodeEncodeError`, so the validator reasons about the actual bytes a real request carried, not a guess.

  The reason the round-two regression suite could not catch this matters as much as the bug: the end-to-end corpus (`test_detect_penetration_attempt_benchmark.py`) picks exactly one delivery mechanism per case via `zlib.crc32(case_id) % len(mechanisms)`, and both high-byte pickle cases happened to hash to `query_param`, a mechanism that never touches body decoding at all, so the suite stayed green over a live bypass on every other mechanism. A new test, `test_byte_sensitive_deserialization_detected_via_every_mechanism`, now runs both byte-sensitive corpus cases (`..._proto_header_prefixed`, `..._newtrue_prefixed`) through all four body mechanisms (`raw_body`, `form_body`, `json_body_nested`, `multipart_body`) unconditionally instead of the single sampled one; this stays scoped to just these two cases, the only ones a crc32 sample could hide a mechanism-specific bug behind. The dedicated pickle test file had the identical blind spot, calling `detect()` directly on an already-decoded string; it now also runs every high-byte family member (11 of the 26: any prefix containing a byte `>= 0x80`) through `detect_penetration_attempt` over `raw_body`, `form_body`, and `multipart_body`, the real scan entry points, in both trees.

  Second, a blocking false positive, introduced by dropping the anchor: the unanchored pattern can match starting mid-body, gated only by the prefix-window check, and a `GLOBAL`-shaped construct glued onto ordinary prose (`Build log: step finished(cache\nvalue\nR.\n`) validated as a clean opcode run and flagged `deserialization` on content with no attacker involvement at all, confirmed both ways against base `d588e92c` (`is_threat=False`) and the round-one lane (`is_threat=True`). The root cause was a second, independent defect in the validator: `io.BytesIO.read()`/`readline()`/`readinto()` silently truncate at end-of-stream instead of raising, so a length-prefixed opcode (`SHORT_BINBYTES`, `SHORT_BINUNICODE`, and siblings) whose declared length exceeds what remains in the artificial 32-byte window still reports a clean, fully-consumed parse, because the short read is never detected; `'U' + 'Z' + ('x' * 30)`, 32 bytes of arbitrary padding with no real pickle content at all, validated as clean under the old read primitives for exactly this reason. The validator's `read`/`readline`/`readinto` are now wrapped to raise `_PickleOpcodePrefixShortRead` whenever fewer bytes come back than were asked for or a `readline()` call runs off the end of the window without finding `\n`, closing the short-read acceptance at its root; this alone was enough to reject the `SHORT_BINBYTES`-glued benign build-log shape without needing to touch the regex or the family's accept set. A 21-case adversarial-benign sweep (Python/JS/YAML/Markdown/email/diff/log-trace/CSV/INI-shaped text with `c`/`(`/`)` glued into `GLOBAL`-adjacent positions) stays clean. A full 256-byte single-prefix sweep through the real decode path (`bytes([b]) + payload`, decoded via `errors="surrogateescape"`, run through `detect()`) accepts 11 of 256: the 8 genuine zero/short-argument opcodes in the family that fit a 1-byte prefix (`MARK`, `EMPTY_TUPLE`, `NONE`, `EMPTY_LIST`, `EMPTY_DICT`, `NEWTRUE`, `NEWFALSE`, `EMPTY_SET`), the pre-existing newline-precedes-`GLOBAL` shortcut (`0x0A`), and two bytes (`0x43`/`'C'`, `0x63`/`'c'`) that never reach the validator at all: the pattern set compiles case-insensitively (a pre-existing, unrelated property of every pattern this handler compiles, `guard_core/detection_engine/compiler.py`), so `'C'`/`'c'` immediately preceding the payload's own leading `c` fold onto the `GLOBAL` opcode's own literal marker and the match starts there with an empty prefix, never invoking the prefix validator; this is unchanged by, and unrelated to, this round's fix.

  `deserialization` recall on the pattern-layer corpus (`test_detection_benchmark.py`) is honestly re-derived at 18/18: every binary-opcode corpus case is now constructed as `bytes`, decoded via `errors="surrogateescape"` exactly as `utils.py` does, so every one of them is proven detectable on the real ingestion path, not merely on directly-injected codepoints; the round-one unit's claimed 17/17 counted two cases (`PROTO`-prefixed, and the round-one corpus never added a zero-argument high-byte case at all) that could not have survived that round trip as originally written. A new `NEWTRUE`-prefixed case was added to cover the family's most representative high-byte, zero-argument bypass shape, which the round-one corpus never covered despite it being central to the critic's finding. The pattern-layer benign false-positive table is unaffected, 24/335, and the deserialization-specific case above is covered by a dedicated regression test rather than a corpus addition. The crc32 mechanism-keyed end-to-end corpus (`test_detect_penetration_attempt_benchmark.py`) required two of its own fixes to keep testing the real path instead of crashing: `_body_request`'s `payload.encode()` and `_form_body_request`'s `urlencode(...)` both defaulted to strict UTF-8 encoding, which raises on the lone surrogate codepoints a byte-honest deserialization payload can now legitimately contain; both now pass `errors="surrogateescape"` through, the same round trip `utils.py` performs, so a payload built for the real decode path can also be re-encoded back to the bytes it started from instead of crashing the harness.

  Covered by `tests/test_sus_patterns/test_pickle_global_opcode_prefix_bypass.py` (and its generated sync mirror), rebuilt this round: every binary-opcode case in the opcode-prefix family is now constructed as `bytes` and decoded via `errors="surrogateescape"` before being handed to `detect()`, the 11-of-26 high-byte members of the family additionally routed through `detect_penetration_attempt` over `raw_body`, `form_body`, and `multipart_body`, the real scan entry points (not `detect()` on an already-decoded string), a `FRAME`-prefixed case pinned as the one remaining disclosed gap (see below), a recording-`find_class` test proving the baseline payload genuinely executes `shutil.rmtree` under a real, unstubbed `pickle._Unpickler`, the pinned 256-byte sweep result and its breakdown into genuine-opcode/case-fold/newline-shortcut buckets, the glued-benign-prose false positive as a permanent regression case, the short-read boundary exploit as a permanent regression case, and direct unit coverage of every branch in `_pickle_global_prefix_is_opcode_stream`/`_pickle_prefix_is_clean_opcode_run` including the surrogate-recovery path, `EXT1`, `PERSID`, an embedded `GLOBAL` opcode reaching `find_class`, a `BYTEARRAY8` opcode reaching the `readinto` short-read path, and the `read`/`readline` short-read rejection paths; 100 percent line and branch coverage on the changed code in both trees. One family member, `FRAME` (the protocol-4 framing opcode, byte `0x95`), was a disclosed gap in the validator at the time; see below for why and how it closed. Both trees; the sync mirror is generated (`scripts/unasync.py`), not hand-written.

- **`FRAME` closed, and the predicted cause was wrong: the real defect was an `AttributeError`, not a short read, and the fix exposed a corpus row that cannot be closed without detecting a byte sequence that does not execute.** The hypothesis going in was that a `FRAME`-declared length exceeding the validator's 32-byte window would trip the short-read wrapper. Measured instead: `_pickle_prefix_is_clean_opcode_run` never calls `Unpickler.load()`, so it never constructs `self._unframer`, the object `pickle`'s own `load_frame` opcode handler unconditionally reaches into; every `FRAME`-prefixed window raised `AttributeError: '_PickleOpcodePrefixUnpickler' object has no attribute '_unframer'`, caught by the validator's blanket `except Exception`, and rejected regardless of window size or declared length. `FRAME` is now handled directly in the dispatch loop: it consumes its fixed 9-byte header (1-byte opcode, 8-byte little-endian length) and rejects only a length that exceeds `sys.maxsize`, the same sanity bound `pickle.py`'s own `load_frame` applies; the declared length itself is otherwise discarded rather than used to pre-validate the rest of the window, since a real payload's true frame length will almost always exceed a deliberately narrow 32-byte prefix window, and that is a property of the window, not of the payload's legitimacy.

  Proven against a real, unstubbed `pickle.loads`, the same standard applied to every other family member: `\x80\x04\x95\x15\x00\x00\x00\x00\x00\x00\x00\x8c\x06shutil\x94\x8c\x06rmtree\x94\x93\x94.`, the actual output of `pickle.dumps(shutil.rmtree, protocol=4)`, and `\x95\x00\x00\x00\x00\x00\x00\x00\x00cshutil\nrmtree\n(S'/tmp/x'\ntR.`, a `FRAME` header directly in front of the classic-opcode baseline payload, both now validate as a clean prefix and both genuinely execute; `find_class` resolves to `("shutil", "rmtree")` and `shutil.rmtree` is called with `"/tmp/x"` under a real, unstubbed `_Unpickler`, confirmed with `shutil.rmtree` patched out rather than assumed safe to run.

  The mandated gate's `pickle_frame_prefix` row, however, glues `\x95` directly onto the baseline payload with no length field at all (`b"\x95" + PICKLE_TAIL`), and that specific byte sequence is not a working attack: `pickle.loads` on it raises `UnpicklingError: pickle data was truncated`, measured directly, because `FRAME`'s mandatory 8-byte length field consumes the payload's own `cshutil\n` bytes, the literal text the classic `GLOBAL` opcode's marker needs, so the parse desyncs and the next byte (`r`, `LONG_BINPUT`) fails on an empty stack. No window size and no leniency choice recovers this, because the bytes the attack needs are structurally consumed as `FRAME`'s own argument, not merely out of view; this is not the same failure shape as the short-read false positive this validator already closes; there, an opcode's declared length overran a window that still held no real content behind it, and rejecting was correct. Here, the validator is asked to accept a sequence that cannot execute at all. Per the standing rule not to detect a byte sequence that is not a working attack, it correctly remains a miss, and the mandated gate confirms zero new false positives and zero new misses against the base corpus (`pickle_newtrue_prefix` and `pickle_proto_prefix` still in `fixed`, `pickle_frame_prefix` unchanged in both). Closing that specific corpus row would need the fixture corrected to include a genuine 8-byte length field, a change to shared harness code outside this lane.

  Covered by four renamed/rewritten tests in `test_pickle_global_opcode_prefix_bypass.py` (both trees): the family and validator-level tests that previously pinned `FRAME` as a disclosed gap now assert detection instead; a new test pins the corpus-style malformed sequence as a permanent non-working-attack regression case, asserting both the `UnpicklingError` and the correct non-detection in the same test; the `sys.maxsize` overflow rejection and the real-entry-point routing across `raw_body`/`form_body`/`multipart_body` each get dedicated coverage; and the existing genuine-execution test now also covers the `FRAME`-prefixed shape. 100 percent line and branch coverage on the changed code in both trees.

- **The surrogateescape boundary fixed decoding but broke reporting: a lone surrogate reaching Python's `logging` module through a `FileHandler` raised `UnicodeEncodeError` inside `emit()`, which `logging`'s own `handleError` swallows, so the audit-log entry for the attack just detected was silently dropped, 0 bytes written, with a traceback to stderr on every occurrence. `log_suspicious_level` defaults to `WARNING`, so this fired by default. Detection itself was unaffected (`is_threat` still returned correctly), so this was never a DoS, only a silent loss of exactly the audit trail this feature exists to produce, confirmed absent on base `d588e92c` with byte-identical input.** This is fixed as a class, not as one call site: a single helper, `_sanitize_for_reporting` (defined once per module, `guard_core/utils.py` and `guard_core/handlers/suspatterns_handler.py`, both with sync mirrors), round-trips a string through `.encode("utf-8", errors="surrogateescape").decode("utf-8", errors="backslashreplace")`. For clean text this is a no-op (byte-identical output, confirmed for ASCII and for genuine multi-byte UTF-8 like `café`); for a surrogate produced by the decode boundary, it recovers the original byte and renders it as literal, readable, ASCII-safe text (`\udc88` becomes the 4-character string `\x88`, not a lossy `U+FFFD`), so the forensic byte value survives in a form every downstream sink can carry.

  Applied at the six places raw scanned content becomes a reporting string, not at each downstream sink: `_log_at_level` (`guard_core/utils.py`), the single choke point both `_log_detected_component`'s raw-value excerpt and `log_activity`'s trigger-info message funnel through before reaching any logging handler; `_build_detection_hit`, sanitizing `trigger_info` once before it is returned in every `DetectionResult`, which is what `core/checks/implementations/suspicious_activity.py` embeds into both `log_activity` and `event_bus.send_middleware_event`'s `reason`; and the four independent constructors of a threat dict's `"match"` field (`_build_regex_threat` and the decoded-view path-traversal builder in `suspatterns_handler.py`, `_check_always_scan_header` and the NoSQL operator-key check in `utils.py`), since `threat["match"]` is exposed verbatim in `DetectionResult.threats`, which the middleware never reads back out today but a framework adapter can. `_send_pattern_event`'s `content_preview` (`suspatterns_handler.py`) is included too, for the same uniform policy, even though it was independently confirmed not to crash (see below).

  **Every other sink was enumerated and empirically exercised, not read-and-assumed, per the same standard that missed the FileHandler bug in the first place.** Console `StreamHandler` was already safe: `sys.stderr.errors` is `"backslashreplace"` unconditionally in CPython, independent of locale, confirmed under both a UTF-8 and a `C` locale. `JsonFormatter` was already safe: `json.dumps(..., default=str)`'s default `ensure_ascii=True` escapes a lone surrogate to literal ASCII (`\udc88` as six characters) rather than embedding the raw codepoint, so the formatted line encodes cleanly. The guard-agent event pipeline (`send_event` → `guard_agent.transport`) was already safe end to end against the real installed `guard_agent` 2.8.1: `SecurityEvent(reason=..., metadata=...)` construction, `model_dump()`, and `safe_json_serialize` (`json.dumps` with the same default `ensure_ascii=True`) all tolerate a lone surrogate, confirmed by constructing a real event with one and running it through each stage; `EventBuffer`'s Redis persistence goes through the same `safe_json_serialize` call first, so it inherits the same safety. One landmine was found but confirmed dormant: `model_dump_json()` (pydantic-core's native serializer) does raise on a lone surrogate, but grepping the installed package confirms it is never called anywhere in guard-agent's transport or buffer code. Redis writes in guard-core's own code were confirmed to never carry raw scanned content today (`redis.connection.Encoder` with default `encoding_errors="strict"` was confirmed to raise on a lone surrogate in isolation, so this remains a real property to preserve, not a currently reachable path). Response bodies/headers, exception messages, `repr()` calls, and metrics labels were all confirmed to carry only static or developer-configured strings, never scanned content.

  **OpenTelemetry and Logfire were the one sink found to be broken in the same class as logging, contained rather than crashing.** `OtelHandler.send_event` sets `span.set_attribute("guard.reason", event.reason)`, which can carry the same trigger-info leak. `span.set_attribute()` itself never validates; the real UTF-8 boundary is the OTLP exporter's protobuf encoder, confirmed empirically against the real installed `opentelemetry-exporter-otlp-proto-http`: `_encode_value`'s `PB2AnyValue(string_value=value)` raises `UnicodeEncodeError` on a lone surrogate. The library's own `_encode_attributes` catches this per-attribute and drops just that attribute, logging a warning through its own logger, so the span still exports, only the `guard.reason` attribute silently vanishes from it, exactly the same shape of defect as the logging bug, contained to the telemetry record instead of the log line. Logfire shares this exporter (confirmed by source: `logfire`'s network exporter subclasses the same OTLP HTTP exporter class), so it is reasoned to fail the same contained way; this was not independently exercised at the encode boundary and is disclosed as reasoned, not measured. Sanitizing `trigger_info` at its source (`_build_detection_hit`, above) closes this too, since `event.reason` is built from the now-clean `trigger_info`.

  Pinned by `test_detected_component_with_lone_surrogate_writes_intact_log_line` (`tests/test_utils/test_logging.py`, and its generated sync mirror): a real pickle-shaped body carrying a lone-surrogate-producing byte goes through `detect_penetration_attempt` with `setup_custom_logging` writing to a real file, and the test asserts the log file is non-empty, contains the expected message text, and contains the readable `\x88` escape rather than the raw surrogate. Verified red-first: reverting just the `_log_at_level` sanitization line reproduces the exact defect, `UnicodeEncodeError` inside `FileHandler.emit()`, swallowed by `logging.Handler.handleError`, log file left at 0 bytes; re-applying the fix turns the same test green. Both trees; 100 percent line and branch coverage on the changed lines.

- **A negative JSON-structure result suppressed the raw pattern scan entirely, so an attack living in JSON structure rather than in a value was invisible on every non-body component.** `_check_value_enhanced`'s embedded-JSON pre-check (`guard_core/utils.py`, non-`request_body` contexts only) called `_try_check_json_value`, and returned unconditionally on any non-`None` result, whether or not it actually found a threat. `_try_check_json_value`'s own walker (`_check_json_fields`) only inspects top-level key names and top-level *string* values, so `{"username":{"$ne":null}}`'s injection, nested one level down inside a dict value, was invisible to it and it correctly, if uselessly, reported no threat, which the old code then treated as license to skip the raw regex scan too, the one pass that actually has a `nosql`-category pattern shaped to catch `"$ne":null` as raw text. Confirmed on `query_param`: `{"username":{"$ne":null}}`, `{"$where":"1==1"}`, and `{"__proto__":{"isAdmin":true}}` all measured `is_threat=False` before the fix and `is_threat=True` on `raw_body`, where this pre-check never runs at all, the same gap the coordinator's `x={"username":{"$ne":null}}` one-byte flip shape already demonstrated: breaking JSON parsing with a leading `x` forced the fallthrough this fix now makes unconditional, and detection returned. Fixed at the root, in the pre-check itself: the early return now happens only when `detected` is true; a negative JSON result falls through to the raw scan exactly as if no JSON pre-check had run at all. Both trees.

  Falling through doubles work on JSON-bearing, non-body components whose embedded-JSON walk finds nothing: the value is now JSON-parsed *and* raw-regex-scanned, where before it was only parsed. Measured directly on the affected path (`_check_value_enhanced` and `_try_check_json_value` in isolation, not the pattern-layer benchmark, which calls `SusPatternsManager.detect()` directly and never touches this pre-check), building a nested `$gt`/`$lt`/`$where`-bearing JSON blob at 1x/4x/16x size (147/588/2358 bytes): the added raw-scan cost is a roughly constant 18-28ms per call across all three sizes (not a multiplicative blowup, since the pre-existing top-level-key walk, unchanged, already dominates and itself scales with the size), falling from 2.6x to 1.12x of the old walk-only cost as size grows. The cost is paid only on the branch that used to be a blind spot: a JSON value where the embedded-JSON walk finds nothing still returns immediately with no added cost when it does find something, unchanged from before.

  False-positive delta measured on the full `tests/attack_simulation` benign corpus (56 samples across 11 categories, not just the shared defect-corpus set), run through the actually-affected code path (`detect_penetration_attempt` over `query_param`, not the pattern-layer harness's own `context="unknown"` benchmark, which bypasses this pre-check and was unaffected by construction): 2 false positives found, both on `legit_templates_docs` (`${user.displayName}`/`${HOME}`-shaped shell-substitution text), and both confirmed pre-existing and unrelated to this fix, not a new regression: `json.loads()` fails on both samples (they are not JSON), so `_try_check_json_value` returns `None`, and the new `if detected:` guard added by this change is on a branch this input never reaches, with or without the fix; the false positive is `cmd_injection`'s existing `${...}` pattern, already reachable via `query_param` before this change, confirmed unaffected by re-checking the same two samples directly through `_check_value_enhanced` with the JSON pre-check isolated. The delta attributable to this fix, the actual gate, is zero new false positives. Flagged, not fixed: this pre-existing false positive predates this round and is outside this fix's scope; it is disclosed here rather than silently left for a later round to rediscover.

  No header-scoped JSON-structure pin was added: the only two categories whose patterns require JSON-literal structure to match (`nosql`, matching `"$ne":`/`{$gt:`-shaped text, and `proto_pollution`, matching `"__proto__":`-shaped text) both exclude `header` from their context set (`_CTX_NOSQL`, `_CTX_PROTO_POLLUTION`, `guard_core/handlers/suspatterns_handler.py`), confirmed by reading every `_CTX_*` context set in the file, not assumed; the four categories that are header-scoped (`xss`, `xml`, `file_upload`, `http_split`) match on HTML tags, XML declarations, filenames, and CRLF sequences respectively, none of which need JSON punctuation to fire, so a JSON-structure-shaped bypass has no header-scoped category to hide behind.

  Covered by `tests/test_utils/test_structured_json_nosql.py` (and its generated sync mirror): the three JSON-structure cases above, parametrized, routed through `detect_penetration_attempt` over `query_param`; and the coordinator's exact one-byte-flip pin (`x={"username":{"$ne":null}}` on `query_param`) as its own permanent regression case. 100 percent line and branch coverage on the changed line in both trees.

- **Test-hygiene: a "not a working attack" pin called real, unrestricted `pickle.loads` on attacker-shaped bytes, safe only for as long as the payload stays malformed.** `test_frame_opcode_without_length_field_is_not_a_working_attack` (`tests/test_sus_patterns/test_pickle_global_opcode_prefix_bypass.py`) proved the corpus's `pickle_frame_prefix` bytes do not execute by calling `pickle.loads()` on them directly and asserting the resulting `UnpicklingError`; if any future edit to this file or to CPython's own `pickle` module ever made those exact bytes well-formed, the same line would execute `shutil.rmtree('/tmp/x')` for real, since nothing in the test blocks class resolution. It now loads through a local `_BlockingUnpickler` subclass whose `find_class` records the call and raises immediately rather than resolving, and asserts the call list stays empty, same evidential value (the malformed stream never reaches a resolvable `GLOBAL`/`REDUCE` opcode), with no path to real code execution regardless of how the bytes are shaped, matching the same blocking pattern the production validator (`_PickleOpcodePrefixUnpickler`) already uses. The test's own former assertion that the malformed stream is *not detected* is dropped too: a malformed stream being flagged is not a false positive worth protecting, and pinning it only constrains future detection improvements against a payload that was never a real attack shape to begin with, the same "never pin a bypass as intended" principle in a milder form. Both trees.

- **A client's IP identity is now canonicalized once, at extraction, so one host cannot present as several distinct identity strings to bans, rate limits, and suspicious-activity counters.** `_extract_from_forwarded_header` (`guard_core/utils.py:196`) called `ip_address(candidate)` to validate the forwarded-for entry, then discarded the parsed result and returned the raw header text; `extract_client_ip` (`:264`) did the same with `request.client_host`, never parsing it at all. Every downstream consumer keys on that exact string: `IPBanManager.banned_ips[ip]` / its Redis mirror, `RateLimitCheck`/`check_rate_limit_by_ip`'s sliding-window buckets, `SuspiciousActivityCheck`'s `middleware.suspicious_request_counts[client_ip]`, and (missed by the first pass of this audit, found by adversarial review and corrected below) `EmergencyModeCheck`'s `emergency_whitelist` membership check, all do plain string comparisons with no normalization of their own, confirmed by reading every one of those call sites; a single host writing `2001:db8::1`, `2001:0db8::1`, `2001:DB8::1`, or `2001:db8:0:0:0:0:0:1` (four textual forms of one address, e.g. across proxy hops or client libraries with different IPv6 formatting) previously produced four distinct ban/rate-limit/counter identities for the one client, and a dual-stack listener reporting `::ffff:1.2.3.4` for the same host a bare `1.2.3.4` request arrives from split a single client into two. Three new module-level helpers, `_canonical_ip_text`, `_strip_ip_brackets`, and `_canonicalize_ip` (`guard_core/utils.py:172-193`), parse via `ipaddress.ip_address` and fold an IPv4-mapped IPv6 address to its wrapped `IPv4Address` (`addr.ipv4_mapped`, present only on `IPv6Address`, guarded with `isinstance` rather than the unguarded attribute access a naive port would use, since `IPv4Address` has no such attribute and would raise) before taking `str()`; every genuine identity-extraction boundary now routes through them: the forwarded-header candidate (`_extract_from_forwarded_header`, rejecting on invalid input exactly as before, now returning the canonical form on success instead of the raw candidate), the connecting host in `extract_client_ip` (all three return points, `connecting_ip`'s value for the untrusted-proxy `send_agent_event` telemetry, kept the raw text only for the pre-existing `_warn_forwarded_header_preempted` misconfiguration-detection heuristic and the `_is_trusted_proxy`/`_is_private_or_loopback` checks, both of which are unaffected by textual form and where canonicalizing first would have silently weakened the heuristic's exact-text match against the proxy's own raw `X-Forwarded-For` chain), the log-only `_extract_request_context` (`:324`), `_extract_ip_from_request`/`check_ip_country`'s request-object branch (`:431`), and `detect_penetration_attempt`'s independently-derived `client_ip` (`:1474`, fed to the pattern-detection engine's telemetry). No canonicalization was added anywhere downstream; every consumer above is unchanged and simply now receives one consistent string.
  **`_strip_ip_brackets` is a single shared helper, not duplicated bracket-stripping logic, precisely because a first pass at this fix landed it only on the forwarded-header path and missed the connecting-host path, leaving `[2001:db8::1]` and `2001:db8::1` arriving as two different identities depending on which path the request took, the exact defect this unit exists to close, just relocated rather than closed.** `_extract_from_forwarded_header` already stripped a bracket pair before validating (pre-existing); `_canonicalize_ip` now calls the same `_strip_ip_brackets` before its own `ip_address()` call, so both the forwarded-header candidate and the connecting host resolve `[2001:db8::1]`/`[2001:0DB8::1]`/`2001:db8::1` to the identical `2001:db8::1`, verified by routing both paths through the real `extract_client_ip` for both a lowercase and an uppercase bracketed form. Because both call sites now go through the one helper, they cannot drift apart again the way the first pass did.
  **The IPv4-mapped merge (`::ffff:a.b.c.d` == `a.b.c.d`) is correct because the block IPv4-mapped addresses occupy, `::ffff:0:0/96`, is structurally disjoint from every prefix IANA assigns to a real IPv6 host: it sits inside the `0000::/8` special-purpose block (alongside `::/128` unspecified and `::1/128` loopback), while global unicast comes from `2000::/3`, link-local from `fe80::/10`, and unique-local from `fc00::/7`. No independently-routed IPv6 host address can ever fall inside `::ffff:0:0/96`; the form exists solely as the wire representation dual-stack sockets and NAT64 use to wrap an IPv4 endpoint, never as an address a real IPv6-native host is assigned. Verified directly against the installed `ipaddress` module: `2000::/3`, `fe80::/10`, and `fc00::/7` each test `False` for `.overlaps(ip_network("::ffff:0:0/96"))`, and neither a documentation-range global-unicast-shaped address (`2001:db8::1`) nor two live-allocated public resolvers (`2606:4700:4700::1111`, `2001:4860:4860::8888`) test as members of that block. Treating `::ffff:1.2.3.4` and `1.2.3.4` as one identity is therefore always correct, not deployment-dependent.**
  **IPv6 zone identifiers are deliberately left distinct.** `ip_address()` accepts a zone-qualified link-local address (`fe80::1%eth0`) and `str()` preserves the zone suffix verbatim, so `fe80::1%eth0` and `fe80::1` canonicalize to two different strings, on purpose: a link-local address cannot cross a router to reach a public listener, so two zone-bearing link-locals name different local interfaces, not the same client, and merging them would be the wrong fix. Pinned by `test_zone_identifier_link_local_stays_distinct_by_design`, named so a future change cannot "fix" this into a merge without deliberately renaming the test that documents it is intentional.
  **A port suffix is dropped, not merged, also deliberately.** `1.2.3.4:8080` fails `ip_address()` (even after bracket-stripping, which does nothing here since it is unbracketed) and the forwarded-header candidate is dropped (returns `None`, as before); no port parsing was added, since a port is not part of host identity and a header carrying one is malformed. Pinned: `test_forwarded_header_candidate_with_port_suffix_is_dropped`.
  **A non-IP connecting host (`request.client_host` itself, not a header) passes through completely unmodified, and stays that way on purpose.** Unlike the forwarded-header candidate, the connecting host is not attacker-supplied text riding in a header the client controls; it is whatever the application server itself reports as the peer, so there is nothing to canonicalize it against and nothing safe to normalize a non-IP value into. `_canonicalize_ip`'s `except ValueError: return value` fallback already preserved this before the bracket fix; pinned explicitly now as `test_direct_path_non_ip_client_host_stays_raw_not_attacker_supplied`, named so this reads as an intentional trust distinction, not an unfinished edge, the same treatment the zone-identifier pin above got. The pre-existing `''`/`None` to `"unknown"` fallback in `extract_client_ip` is untouched by any of this.
  **`_canonical_ip_text` does not unmap an IPv4-mapped address when a `scope_id` is present, closing a defect an adversarial critic found in this unit's own first draft.** `IPv6Address.ipv4_mapped` silently discards `scope_id`, so `::ffff:1.2.3.4%eth0` and `::ffff:1.2.3.4%eth1` both unmapped to the bare `1.2.3.4`, merging two zone-qualified, genuinely distinct local hosts into one identity, directly contradicting the zone-identifier invariant stated above and pinned only for the non-mapped case, the exact cross-product this unit's own test suite had not covered. `_canonical_ip_text` now unmaps only when `addr.scope_id is None`; a zone-qualified mapped address keeps its full zoned form (`::ffff:1.2.3.4%eth0` stays `::ffff:1.2.3.4%eth0`), so two different zones stay distinct and a zone-free mapped address still canonicalizes to bare IPv4 exactly as before. Both directions of the cross-product are now pinned: `test_ipv4_mapped_addresses_with_different_zones_stay_distinct_by_design`, `test_ipv4_mapped_address_without_zone_still_canonicalizes_to_bare_ipv4`.
  **The direct (connecting-host) path did not strip a bracket pair, so `[2001:db8::1]` diverged from the same host's identity via the forwarded-header path, which does strip brackets: one host, two identities, depending on which path the request took, precisely the defect this unit exists to close.** `_strip_ip_brackets` (`guard_core/utils.py`) is a single shared helper both `_extract_from_forwarded_header` and `_canonicalize_ip` now call, so the two paths cannot drift apart again the way this first pass did. Pinned in both directions: `test_direct_and_forwarded_paths_agree_on_bracketed_ipv6_identity` (parametrized over a lowercase and an uppercase bracketed form, asserting the direct path, the forwarded path, and the unbracketed direct path all agree on `2001:db8::1`) and the pre-existing `test_direct_path_non_ip_client_host_stays_raw_not_attacker_supplied`, reconfirmed unaffected (`garbage` still passes through the direct path unchanged, since the connecting host is server-supplied, not attacker-supplied, and there is nothing to canonicalize it against).
  **`_ip_in_list` (`guard_core/utils.py`), the comparison behind `whitelist`/`blacklist`, now compares canonical forms instead of raw `ipaddress` equality, and `EmergencyModeCheck.check()` (`guard_core/core/checks/implementations/emergency_mode.py`, mirrored) now routes through it instead of raw Python list membership, closing a second introduced defect: it was the only IP-list consumer in the package using bare `client_ip not in self.config.emergency_whitelist`, string-for-string, against admin-configured entries.** A whitelist entry written as `::ffff:203.0.113.5` (a real, plausible admin value, e.g. copied from a dual-stack proxy's own logs) previously matched a client presenting in that exact same raw form, matched only because both sides happened to share the same non-canonical text; once `extract_client_ip` always returns canonical form, that same client now presents as `203.0.113.5`, and the raw comparison silently stopped matching, denying under emergency mode a host the admin explicitly meant to keep in. Routing through `_ip_in_list` alone was not sufficient by itself: `ipaddress.IPv4Address("203.0.113.5") == ipaddress.IPv6Address("::ffff:203.0.113.5")` is `False` in the standard library, since the two are different types by version, so `_ip_in_list`'s pre-existing `ip_addr == ip_address(entry)` comparison would have reproduced the identical miss. `_ip_in_list` now canonicalizes both the caller's already-canonical `ip` and each `entry` through `_canonicalize_ip` before comparing (`canonical_ip == _canonicalize_ip(entry)`), which folds the mapped-form entry to the same string the canonical client presents, and this is comparison-time normalization inside the existing per-call loop, not a rewrite of `config.whitelist`/`config.blacklist`/`config.emergency_whitelist` at read time; the stored config entries are never mutated. This also simplifies `_ip_in_list`'s CIDR/exact split down to one branch, since `_canonicalize_ip`'s own `except ValueError: return value` fallback already reproduces the old fallback-to-raw-string-equality behavior for a malformed entry. `EmergencyModeCheck.check()` now parses `client_ip` via `ip_address()` first (an unparseable value, e.g. `"unknown"`, denies under emergency mode exactly as the old raw-membership check did, since no unparseable string was ever going to equal a whitelist entry either) and calls `_ip_in_list` on success. Pinned: `test_emergency_whitelist_mapped_entry_matches_canonical_client_ip` (through the real `EmergencyModeCheck.check()`) and `test_emergency_check_denies_unparseable_client_ip`.
  **A ban written under a pre-canonicalization key becomes permanently unreachable the moment this fix deploys, reproduced live against real Redis: a ban set as `::ffff:203.0.113.5` is invisible to a lookup for `203.0.113.5` after upgrade, silently clearing an in-flight attacker's active ban.** This is inherent to any canonicalization change to an already-identity-addressed store, not specific to this unit's mistakes, but inherent is not the same as unavoidable, and no flawed library ships here. `IPBanManager.initialize_redis()` now runs `_migrate_legacy_ban_keys()`, an idempotent, `SCAN`-based migration (never `KEYS`, over `{prefix}banned_ips:*`, the same namespace `reset()` already scans): for each key whose identity segment does not equal its own canonical form, it fetches the value and remaining `PTTL` in one atomic `pipeline()` round trip (eliminating the GET-then-PTTL race a two-step read would have), writes the value under the canonical key with `px=` the same remaining TTL if the canonical key does not already exist with an equal-or-longer TTL (keeping the longer expiry rather than overwriting blindly), then deletes the legacy key; a legacy key with no valid remaining TTL (`<= 0`, including a persistent no-TTL key, which should not occur through normal `ban_ip` but is handled defensively) is simply deleted rather than migrated, since there is no TTL to preserve.
