Migration runs unconditionally on every `initialize_redis()` call, the real per-process Redis-wiring moment (`HandlerInitializer.initialize_redis_handlers()` calls it once, right after `redis_handler.initialize()`, on every real start), and is a genuine no-op against an already-canonical tree, so running it on every start is safe by construction, not by convention; a second consecutive run changes nothing, verified directly (identical value and `PTTL` within one second, before and after). Two connection-shape guards, `_close_if_unusable` (`guard_core/handlers/ipban_handler.py`), prevent an inadequately-mocked `redis_handler` elsewhere in the test suite from leaking an unawaited coroutine into a `RuntimeWarning` (found only by reading full-suite log content, not by any test failing): if `get_connection()` or the connection's `scan_iter()` do not expose `__aenter__`/`__aiter__` respectively (via the unasync-mirrored `__enter__`/`__iter__` in the sync tree), the returned object is closed if it has a `.close()` (a stray coroutine does; a plain unusable object does not, and the call is wrapped in `contextlib.suppress(AttributeError)`) and migration for that connection is skipped with a logged warning, rather than the whole process crashing or leaking. **Scope is deliberate, not an oversight: ban keys (`{prefix}banned_ips:{ip}`) migrate; rate-limit keys (`{prefix}rate_limit:{rate_key}` / `{prefix}rate_limit:rate:{ip}`) do not.** `auto_ban_duration` defaults to 3600s, long enough that silently losing a live ban is a real security regression; `rate_limit_window` defaults to 60s, short enough that a legacy-keyed rate-limit entry self-expires within a minute on its own, so migrating it would add permanent, unretiring cost (a second Redis round trip on every future ban check, forever) to close a window that closes itself in under 60 seconds. Pinned by name with the window in the test: `test_rate_limit_keys_stay_unmigrated_self_expire_within_60s`. All of it verified against real Redis (the PID-scoped test prefix), not mocks: `test_migration_blocks_canonical_request_after_restart` (a ban written as `::ffff:203.0.113.5` blocks a canonical `203.0.113.5` lookup after a simulated restart), `test_migration_preserves_remaining_ttl_within_one_second`, `test_migration_deletes_the_legacy_key`, `test_migration_second_start_is_idempotent`, `test_migration_keeps_the_longer_expiry_when_canonical_key_already_exists`, `test_migration_deletes_a_persistent_legacy_key_without_ttl`, `test_migration_skipped_when_redis_handler_is_none`, `test_migration_warns_and_continues_when_connection_fails`, and the two connection-shape guard pins, in `tests/test_ipban_legacy_key_migration.py` (mirrored under `tests/test_sync/`).
  Verified through the real pipeline, not string-level unit tests, in `tests/test_utils/test_ip_identity_canonicalization.py` (27 tests per tree) and `tests/test_ipban_legacy_key_migration.py` (11 tests per tree), both mirrored under `tests/test_sync/`, 76 tests total: all six raw forms above canonicalize identically through the real `extract_client_ip`, via both the connecting-host and the trusted-proxy forwarded-header paths; a ban placed through `ip_ban_manager.ban_ip` using the identity `extract_client_ip` derives from one textual form, against real Redis (the PID-scoped test prefix, local cache cleared to force the Redis round trip), blocks a second request whose identity is independently derived from a different textual form, through the real `IpSecurityCheck.check()` (403); a rate-limit bucket exhausted via `check_rate_limit_by_ip` using one form's identity then rejects a call using another form's identity, against real Redis; and the IPv4-mapped pair specifically shares one identity for the ban case, the rate-limit case, and `SuspiciousActivityCheck`'s in-memory suspicious-counter case (`_increment_suspicious_counts`). Confirmed red-first: all four test files, copied unmodified onto a pristine extraction of the pre-fix base commit and run against it with the fixed tree's own interpreter, fail 54 of 76 tests total (22 pass, the pins for already-canonical plain forms, the zone-identifier and non-IP-passthrough pins, and the rate-limit-does-not-migrate pin, none of which any of these defects touched); green on the fixed tree, 76/76. 100 percent line and branch coverage confirmed via the full suite (not a scoped run) on every touched file in both trees: `utils.py`, `handlers/ipban_handler.py`, `core/checks/implementations/emergency_mode.py`. Both detection benchmarks are unchanged on both trees (pattern-layer 293/327 recall, 24/335 false-positive rate; end-to-end 281/314 recall, 23/331 false-positive rate), and the pattern-layer attack table shows 0 new misses, since none of this touches the detection engine.
- **`_decode_base64_candidates` decoded a matched span as a single all-or-nothing unit, so any adjacent junk sharing the span with a real base64 blob hid the blob from every detection category, not just one; a break-counting regex fix (tried and superseded during this round) closed only the exact shape it targeted and reopened just past its own boundary.** The candidate regex (`_BASE64_RE`) tolerates line breaks inside its repeated character class so a MIME-wrapped blob, split by ordinary line wrapping, still matches as one span (see the `{12,}` floor-lowering entry above); `_decode_base64_candidates` then stripped all whitespace from the whole span and made exactly one `base64.b64decode` attempt, returning the ENTIRE span unchanged on failure. Any junk sharing a span with a real blob, an English header word before a MIME blank line, decoy characters an attacker glues in front of their own payload, shifts every subsequent 4-character group and misaligns the whole decode, so the real blob's plaintext was never recovered even though it was present, verbatim, a few characters away. A first attempt at this round tightened `_BASE64_RE` so at most one line break was tolerated per position, on the theory that a blank line (two consecutive breaks) is MIME's own header/body separator and should end a candidate while ordinary wrapping (one break) should not. It closed the one construction it targeted, but two single breaks with one attacker-chosen character between them (`AAAAAAAAAAAA\nB\n<payload>`) is legal under "one break tolerated" and glues onto a following blob exactly as before, still an all-or-nothing failure, still an all-category bypass, and, worse, reachable through a WELL-FORMED, boundary-matching multipart upload: the attacker needs no header trickery at all, only control of their own file bytes, since `_multipart_text_parts` already delivers those bytes untouched to the same decoder. The same regex change also cost real recall: legitimate content whose only irregularity is a blank line inserted mid-stream by some intermediary, previously decoded fine because stripping and reconstructing an already-continuous blob is a no-op regardless of where the break falls, permanently lost that decode once the break started a new candidate, since an arbitrary split of a continuous blob is not independently valid base64 at either half's own alignment. Tightening the break grammar further to reject three-break or four-character variants would only relocate the same failure, not remove it: any shape-based rule leaves room for a decoy shape built to sit just past whatever line it draws. The redesign targets the actual failure mode instead: on a failed whole-span decode, `_decode_base64_candidates` now falls back to scanning the same span for its own maximal alphanumeric-plus-padding runs (`_BASE64_RUN_RE`, `[A-Za-z0-9+/=]{12,}`, the same 12-character floor as the outer candidate, now a single named constant, `_MIN_BASE64_RUN_LENGTH`, shared by both regexes so they cannot drift apart) and independently attempts each run at its own alignment, through the same decode-plus-printable-ratio-plus-gzip-budget checks a whole candidate already had to clear; a run that decodes replaces itself in place, a run that does not (too short, not valid base64 at its own alignment, or a hex literal) is left as literal text, and the surrounding breaks are untouched either way. The candidate regex itself reverts to its original unbounded newline tolerance (`[\r\n]*`, no fixed number of breaks), since the safety property no longer depends on where the regex draws the span boundary: a genuinely continuous legitimate blob decodes on the fast, first-attempt whole-span path exactly as before (byte-identical output, unaffected by how many breaks, of any kind, happen to sit inside it), while any adversarial junk sharing a span with a real blob, however many decoy segments or interposed characters it uses, gets its own independent, correctly-aligned shot at the real payload once the whole-span attempt fails. This is not shape-specific with respect to the decoy: an embedded, decodable run is recovered regardless of how many decoy runs surround it, closing the class rather than the two instances found so far, PROVIDED every break between fragments is `\r`/`\n`; this round's `_BASE64_RE`/`_BASE64_WHITESPACE_RE` tolerate only that pair, so a space-, tab-, or form-feed-joined split, and any URL-safe (`-`/`_`) alphabet, still evade entirely (closed in a later round, see below), and the "regardless of how they are built" framing above describes decoy composition, not separator character, which this round narrowed to newlines only. **Documented limitation, not a regression:** the fallback still only attempts runs of `_MIN_BASE64_RUN_LENGTH` (12) characters or more, the same floor the outer candidate regex has always used, so an attacker's own payload whose base64 form falls under that floor, six bytes of plaintext or fewer (`aWQ=`, four characters, for the two-byte payload `id`), is not independently recovered when glued to a decoy; this was never recovered when merged with adjacent junk on the pre-this-round baseline either (confirmed by direct comparison against the base commit), so nothing regresses, but the closed class is bounded by that floor. **Correction (a later round):** this paragraph originally said "seven bytes," an off-by-one; measured directly, `n_bytes=6` -> `token_len=8` characters, not recovered, while `n_bytes=7` -> `token_len=12` characters, recovered, so the true boundary sits exactly at the 12-character run floor. A later round's fragmentation-reassembly fix (see below) narrows this limitation further, to an *isolated* under-floor payload specifically: multiple under-floor fragments whose combined length clears the floor are now reassembled and recovered even though none of them individually would be. Verified red-first, at the real entry point, on both newly-reported constructions: a bare two-single-break-decoy candidate and, separately, the same shape delivered as a well-formed, boundary-matching multipart file part (`_multipart_text_parts` confirmed non-`None`, i.e. cleanly parsed, before asserting detection) both measured `is_threat=False` on the previous single-break-tolerant regex and `is_threat=True` after this fix, across the same 5 spot-checked categories (`xss`, `sqli`, `cmd_injection`, `ldap`, `dir_traversal`) as before; generalized to a three-decoy-segment shape and to the full 47-payload `BASE64_DECODE_CORPUS` (`tests/test_preprocessor_base64_corpus.py`) glued behind a two-single-break decoy, 0/47 recovered before this fix, 47/47 after. The original header-glue bypass this round started from stays fixed (`Content-Transfer-Encoding: base64` immediately before a blank line and a real blob still decodes correctly). The recall regression is gone, proven both directions: a legitimate blob split by a stylistic double break, at a byte offset that does not fall on a four-character boundary, recovers its plaintext both on the pre-regression base commit and after this fix (unaffected, restored), while it measurably failed to recover under the superseded single-break-tolerant regex (a standalone reconstruction of that regex against the same input, decode-then-reassemble, confirms `False`); pinned across the same 47-payload corpus, both directions. Wrapped-base64 parity holds across encoders: 64-column (PEM-style) and 76-column (MIME-style) wrapping, CRLF and LF, plus `base64.encodebytes`'s own default wrapping, all decode identically before and after, byte-for-byte. Benign false positives, measured directly on both existing pin mechanisms rather than assumed clean: the count-based table (`test_detection_benchmark.py::test_detection_benchmark_recall_and_false_positive_rate`) reports the identical 293/327 recall and 24/335 false-positive rate, and the crc32 mechanism-keyed end-to-end table (`test_detect_penetration_attempt_benchmark.py::test_detect_penetration_attempt_recall_and_false_positive_rate`) reports the identical 281/314 recall and 23/331 false-positive rate, both byte-for-byte identical to the pre-this-round measurement apart from wall-clock timing noise. Beyond the pinned corpora, which do not exercise multiple independently-qualifying runs sharing one failed span, adversarial-benign shapes were constructed directly for this exposure: pairs and triples of already-vetted benign 12-character-plus alphanumeric runs (hex-format hashes, AWS-key-shaped tokens) stacked with single-break and blank-line separators, and realistic multi-line log/CSV-style documents built from the same tokens, all measured `is_threat=False` through the full detection pipeline, not just the decode step. ReDoS-checked: `PatternCompiler.validate_pattern_safety` reports the (now-reverted, original-shape) candidate pattern safe, and direct timing on a worst-case adversarial input built from many qualifying-length runs each separated by a single-character break, the shape that maximizes the number of independent decode attempts the fallback makes, shows roughly constant throughput, in the 4-5 MB/s band across repeated runs, from 10KB (the default `detection_max_content_length`) through 160KB, linear, not exponential; the pure-wrapped-content fast path measures in the 8-9.5 MB/s band, also constant. `radon mi` on the changed file held at rank B before, during, and after this round (10.41 base, 10.02 after the run-fallback redesign, 9.99 after the stricter-fallback-threshold fix below; the new logic is consolidated into nested closures sharing one hex-literal-and-decode check, rather than separate methods, specifically to keep the file's already-borderline maintainability index from crossing into C). Applied to both the async source and its generated sync mirror (`guard_core/sync/detection_engine/preprocessor.py`, `scripts/unasync.py`); the regex and the decode function are the only things that changed. Covered by tests in `tests/test_sus_patterns/test_preprocessor.py` (the regex merges across a blank line into one candidate again; two- and three-decoy recovery; the stylistic-double-break recall fix; a too-short and a hex-literal run left untouched on fallback), `tests/test_preprocessor_base64_corpus.py` (the full `BASE64_DECODE_CORPUS` against the two-decoy shape, the stylistic-double-break shape, and 76-column wrapping; the stacked-benign-run adversarial constructions), and `tests/test_detection/test_multipart_file_part_scanning.py` (`detect_penetration_attempt` end-to-end for a well-formed decoy-prefixed upload, a clean boundary, and a mismatched one), all with generated sync mirrors.

- **The run-fallback above introduced its own false-positive class: two ordinary opaque identifiers, the shape of any client-submitted session or asset ID, on adjacent lines could flip `is_threat` to `True` on a pattern the whole-span decode had already, correctly, rejected.** `Xk29LmQpZt841HbNcRfWs\nwzV6Kj1oJ2OnYwhzMgjJns` decodes as a whole to a printable ratio of 0.4883, below the 0.5 acceptance bar, so the merged decode was discarded exactly as intended; but the fallback then decoded the second line alone, at its own alignment, to a *different* byte sequence whose printable ratio happened to clear 0.5 (0.6667) while containing a substring (`*=h`) that matches the `ldap` wildcard-assignment pattern, flipping the body to a false threat. Disabling only the fallback made the same body safe, isolating the cause precisely: the fallback shares the primary path's acceptance bar, but it is not the same kind of decode. The primary decode has context, the whole span was one coherent candidate; a per-run retry is a guess, and giving it the same 50%-printable bar as a decode with real context means pure noise gets roughly one shot in a few thousand runs of somehow looking "printable enough," and a body containing hundreds of runs (a hash list, an ID export, a log file merged into one failed span) is not a remote-odds case, it is a near-certainty at scale. The fallback's acceptance bar is now stricter than the primary's: `_FALLBACK_PRINTABLE_ASCII_RATIO_THRESHOLD` (0.9) replaces `_PRINTABLE_ASCII_RATIO_THRESHOLD` (0.5) for a per-run decode only, threaded through `_decode_token` as a parameter rather than a second hardcoded constant so both call sites stay obviously in sync; the primary whole-span path is untouched. The threshold was chosen by measurement, not guessed: every payload in the 47-item `BASE64_DECODE_CORPUS`, decoded through the fallback behind a decoy, clears at least 0.9444 printable ratio (pure-ASCII payloads clear 1.0; the five payloads carrying an embedded non-ASCII character, café/naïve, a Cyrillic homoglyph, an emoji, Chinese characters, üser, range from 0.9444 to 0.9767), so 0.9 keeps every one of them recovered with room to spare, while sitting far above where uniformly-random decoded bytes land (expected printable-byte rate for random bytes is roughly 37%, so requiring 90%+ of a run's bytes to be printable is a binomial tail event, not a coin flip). The critic's exact body now measures `is_threat=False`; a structural-plausibility or entropy-based gate was considered as the coordinator suggested but not needed once the ratio bar was actually raised to reflect the two paths' different evidentiary weight, and a simpler fix that clears the measured bar is preferred over a more elaborate one that doesn't measurably do more. **Measured, not asserted zero.** A first, naive statistical sweep (10,000 fuzzed pairs of random 12-to-30-character alphanumeric tokens joined by a single line break, run through the full `detect()` pipeline) measured 2/10,000 triggers at both the old and the new threshold, identically; direct attribution showed both were pre-existing, threshold-independent coincidences in the RAW, undecoded text against unrelated built-in patterns (a random run spelling `cos\n`, matching the raw-view deserialization marker; another spelling `myVPN`, matching a `recon` VPN-portal probe), confirmed by `_decode_base64_candidates` making no change to either body at all, so neither one exercises the fallback and this naive number is not a measurement of the mechanism in question. A second, isolated sweep (200,000 fuzzed pairs, same shape) instead measured the fallback mechanism directly: at the old 0.5 threshold, 9,615/200,000 bodies (4.8%) had a whole-span decode failure recovered by the fallback into a decode change at all; at the new 0.9 threshold, that fell to 715/200,000 (0.36%), a roughly 13x reduction in how often the fallback fires at all. Of the bodies where the fallback did produce a decode change, comparing detection on the decoded text against detection on the same raw, undecoded text isolates the fallback's own causal contribution: 0/9,615 at the old threshold and 0/715 at the new threshold induced a threat the raw text did not already carry, in this sample. Zero observed inductions is not zero risk and is not in tension with the coordinator's own measured one-in-several-thousand rate: at n=9,615 and an assumed true rate of 1/22,000, the chance of observing zero by chance alone is about 65%. By the rule of three, the 95% upper confidence bound on the population-level rate (per fuzzed pair, not per fallback-firing pair) is approximately 3/200,000 (0.0015%) at both thresholds, since zero were observed at both; what moved, measurably, is the fallback's firing rate itself, 13x fewer bodies even reach a state where the phenomenon is possible, and the specific case the coordinator constructed and reported, which does reliably exercise the mechanism, is confirmed closed by direct, deterministic reproduction rather than by statistics alone. All round-one and round-two attack shapes were re-verified recovered under the stricter fallback bar: the header-glue case, the two- and three-decoy cases (bare and well-formed-multipart-delivered), and the full 47-payload corpus, all still detect; the stylistic-double-break recall fix is unaffected, since that content decodes on the primary whole-span path and never reaches the fallback at all. Both existing false-positive pin mechanisms remain at their unchanged baseline (293/327 recall, 24/335 false-positive rate on the count-based table; 281/314 recall, 23/331 on the crc32 mechanism-keyed end-to-end table), and the adversarial-benign stacked-run corpus from the run-fallback entry above stays clean. ReDoS timing is unaffected, since only the acceptance threshold changed, not the regex or the candidate-scanning structure: the same roughly-constant 4-5 MB/s (decoy-heavy) and 8-9.5 MB/s (pure-wrapped) throughput bands hold from 10KB through 160KB. Applied to both trees. Covered by `tests/test_sus_patterns/test_preprocessor.py` (the critic's exact body now decodes to itself, unchanged; a payload below the 12-character run floor stays undecoded when decoy-glued, pinning the disclosed limitation above as actual, tested behavior rather than only prose).

- **Withdrawn: a disclosure in an earlier draft of this round claimed `detect_penetration_attempt` could disagree with an isolated `_scan_request_body` call on the same input.** Re-investigated on request: the claim traced to a test script with two `sys.path.insert(0, ...)` calls, an intended one driven by an environment variable naming the tree under test and a leftover hardcoded one from an earlier draft of the same script, both targeting position 0; the second, unconditional call silently won, so the script imported `guard_core` from the fixed tree while believing, and printing a label claiming, it was exercising the pre-fix tree. There was no disagreement: re-run with the duplicate removed and the resolved `guard_core.__file__` asserted against the intended root before trusting any measurement, the isolated call and the full entry point agree, on both the pre-fix and the post-fix tree. No behavior change; the finding is retracted, not the fix.

- **The 12-character run floor above generalizes to a full bypass for a payload of ANY length: an attacker who fragments their own base64 into chunks that each individually fall under the floor, each glued behind its own decoy that independently clears the floor, defeats detection completely, through a well-formed multipart upload, no header trickery required.** a 39-byte payload, `<script>alert(document.cookie)</script>`, split at 6-byte boundaries into seven base64 chunks (8/8/8/8/8/8/4 characters, every one of them under the 12-character floor) and interleaved with seven distinct 12-character decoys (`AAAAAAAAAAAA\nPHNjcmlw\nBBBBBBBBBBBB\ndD5hbGVy\n...`), still parses as one well-formed multipart file part (`_multipart_text_parts` non-`None`) and measured `is_threat=False` end to end on both the pre-round-3 base commit and on round 3's shipped run-fallback, confirmed by direct reproduction against both trees; a naive downstream consumer that strips the decoys and concatenates the remaining fragments recovers the payload byte for byte, but guard-core's fallback never even attempted any of the seven fragments, since each is individually below `_MIN_BASE64_RUN_LENGTH` and `_BASE64_RUN_RE`'s `{12,}` floor skips them outright. `_decode_base64_candidates` now adds a third decode attempt per candidate span, alongside the existing whole-span and per-run attempts: `_BASE64_SUB_FLOOR_RUN_RE` (boundary-anchored via `(?<![A-Za-z0-9+/=])` and `(?![A-Za-z0-9+/=])` around `[A-Za-z0-9+/=]{1,11}`, the same floor as a complement of `_BASE64_RUN_RE` rather than a second hand-picked number) collects every maximal run in the span that is *individually* below the floor, concatenates them in their original left-to-right order (dropping the floor-clearing runs and the breaks between them), and, if the concatenation itself reaches the floor, attempts one decode of it at the fallback's threshold; a run that independently clears the floor is presumed to be a decoy or its own decodable unit and is excluded from the concatenation, which is exactly the discriminator a floor-length-based attacker construction hands back to the defender. This adds exactly one extra decode attempt per candidate span, not a search over combinations, so it carries no additional ReDoS surface; "one extra attempt" bounds the attempt COUNT, not the reassembled string's length, which scales with the candidate span and is bounded only by the pre-existing outer scan cap, `detection_max_body_inspect_bytes` (`ContentPreprocessor._MAX_FULL_SCAN_BYTES`), not by anything this round added. Unlike round 3's run-fallback, which only replaced the primary whole-span attempt when that attempt outright failed, the reassembly attempt now runs *unconditionally* and appends its result when it decodes to something new: investigating why an initial version of this fix still measured `is_threat=False` against the exact construction above found that the primary whole-span decode does not reliably fail on decoy-plus-fragment content, it often "succeeds," at a printable ratio that clears the primary's 0.5 bar by coincidence, into a byte-garbled, USELESS decode (control bytes from decoding the decoys interleaved with the correctly-decoded fragment text, so `<script>` itself is never contiguous in that output) that nonetheless satisfies the acceptance bar and therefore, under round 3's either/or design, permanently prevented the fallback from ever running at all. Because the reassembled fragments are now appended regardless of whether the primary path "succeeded," this failure mode is closed at the root rather than patched around it. **Narrowing, both directions, measured, not asserted.** Excluded (still evades): a lone, unfragmented payload whose base64 form is itself below the floor (unchanged from the existing documented limitation below); an attacker whose own per-fragment decoy is *also* below the floor and interleaved between fragments, since the reassembly concatenates every sub-floor run it finds, in order, with no way to distinguish an attacker's short decoy from an attacker's short fragment once both are below the floor, this is a real, disclosed residual gap, not claimed closed. Included (newly detected): the exact seven-fragment construction above, all five spot-checked categories (`xss`, `sqli`, `cmd_injection`, `ldap`, `dir_traversal`, each independently constructed with 3-byte chunks specifically so that no signature-relevant substring of the plaintext could accidentally survive intact in the primary path's garbled decode, ruling out a coincidental pass rather than a real fix; measured red-first against a fresh extraction of the pre-round-3 base commit and the round-3 tree, both `is_threat=False`, green only after this fix). Benign impact, measured directly rather than assumed clean: the existing stacked-benign-alnum-run corpus (hex hashes, AWS-key-shaped tokens, all individually at or above the floor) is unaffected, since the new mechanism only activates on runs *below* the floor and none of those runs qualify; a new corpus of realistic short, floor-length identifiers (`SKU48213`, `REF33810`, `ORD10394`, 8 characters each, stacked one per line) stays `is_threat=False` through the decode step, since their concatenation, while it clears the floor, fails the printable-ratio bar as gibberish bytes; a dedicated 200,000-pair fuzz of 2-to-6 short (4-to-11-character) random alphanumeric fragments per body, the shape that maximizes how often the new reassembly path fires with no decoys involved at all, measured the mechanism's own worst-case surface directly: reassembly produced a decode change in 3,202/200,000 bodies (1.6%), of which 525 (16.4% of firings, 0.26% of the full population) induced a detection the raw text did not already carry, every single one of them the same pre-existing, orthogonal `cmd_injection` pattern issue described below, not a new failure mode of the fragment-reassembly logic itself. `radon mi` on the changed file: 9.99 (round 3's own measurement) before this fix, 9.70 after, rank B in both cases, not crossing into C; the one-line regex constant and the ten added lines in `_replace` account for the difference, and a first version of the fix additionally excluded the Unicode replacement character from the printable-ratio count (a separate, correct, but out-of-scope hardening spotted while debugging this fix) was reverted specifically to recover maintainability index rather than pay for an improvement nobody asked for; `_replacement_char_ratio` already gates replacement-heavy decodes on a separate, existing check. Both benchmark pins are unchanged, byte-for-byte: 293/327 recall, 24/335 false-positive rate (count-based table); 281/314 recall, 23/331 (crc32-keyed end-to-end table), reconfirmed by a fresh run of both suites, both trees, after this fix (52 tests, 0 failures). Applied to both the async source and its generated sync mirror. Covered by `tests/test_sus_patterns/test_preprocessor.py` (fragmented-below-floor recovery; a lone below-floor fragment, with no sibling to reassemble with, still not recovered; the stacked-realistic-identifier corpus stays clean), `tests/test_preprocessor_base64_corpus.py`, and `tests/test_detection/test_multipart_file_part_scanning.py` (`detect_penetration_attempt` end to end, for a well-formed upload whose file part carries the seven-fragment construction, across all five spot-checked categories), all with generated sync mirrors, all verified red-first against a fresh extraction of the pre-round-3 base commit.

- **The `_printable_ascii_ratio` metric that gates every base64 decode attempt counted only `0x20`-`0x7E`, so a genuinely non-ASCII attack payload, CJK, Cyrillic, Arabic, Greek, or Hebrew text mixed with ASCII attack syntax, scored every one of its non-ASCII characters as unprintable and could fall below the acceptance bar even though every character in it renders as ordinary, legible text.** `'; EXEC xp_cmdshell('whoami'); -- 你好`, base64-encoded and glued behind a decoy, measured `is_threat=False` at round 3's shipped 0.9 fallback threshold and `is_threat=True` at the pre-round-3 0.5 threshold, an isolated, single-variable, in-process reproduction (same request, only the threshold constant flipped) that confirms the ASCII-only metric, not the threshold value chosen against it, was the defect: sample strings sitting in the supposed "clean gap" between the corpus's observed minimum (0.9444) and 1.0 turned out to need only 2-to-4 non-ASCII characters in a roughly 26-character string to fall below 0.9, which several real attack shapes (an LDAP wildcard query with a Chinese-language filter comment, a SQL comment with a two-character Chinese aside, a path-traversal payload with a short Chinese comment, a Cyrillic-language command-injection message) legitimately do. Renamed and rewritten at the root rather than re-tuned at the threshold: `_printable_ascii_ratio` is now `_printable_ratio` and counts `char.isprintable()` (Python's own Unicode printable-category classification, not an ASCII range check) instead of an ASCII bound; `_PRINTABLE_ASCII_RATIO_THRESHOLD` and `_FALLBACK_PRINTABLE_ASCII_RATIO_THRESHOLD` are renamed to `_PRINTABLE_RATIO_THRESHOLD` and `_FALLBACK_PRINTABLE_RATIO_THRESHOLD` to match; both call sites in `_decode_token` are the only behavioral change. Measured, not assumed: every one of the 47 `BASE64_DECODE_CORPUS` payloads, including all five non-ASCII ones (café/naïve, a Cyrillic homoglyph, an emoji, Chinese characters, üser), now measures a printable ratio of exactly 1.0, up from round 3's 0.9444-0.9767 range for the non-ASCII five; the coordinator's four named gap cases (`'; EXEC xp_cmdshell(...); -- 你好`, an LDAP wildcard query with a Chinese comment, a SQL comment with a Chinese aside, a Cyrillic command-injection message) all measure 1.0 and are recovered through the fallback at both the round-3 threshold and the new one. Because every corpus payload now clears the bar with zero margin to spare rather than round 3's already-thin 0.0233 (0.9444 against a 0.9 bar), the fallback threshold was re-examined rather than left at its old value: see the fuzz measurements in the entry below, which found that 0.9 does not actually zero out garbled-decode induction the way round 3 claimed, and that headroom exists, since every real payload now scores 1.0, to raise the bar further at no recall cost; `_FALLBACK_PRINTABLE_RATIO_THRESHOLD` is now `0.95` (see below for the measurement that value is based on). The primary path's `_PRINTABLE_RATIO_THRESHOLD` (0.5) is untouched; it was not implicated in this finding and a genuinely continuous blob with binary content still needs the lower bar to decode at all. `radon mi` impact is folded into the entry above, since both fixes touch the same file in the same round. Applied to both trees. Covered by `tests/test_preprocessor_encodings.py` (empty text, pure-ASCII, non-ASCII CJK-only, mixed ASCII-and-CJK, all direct `_printable_ratio` unit tests) and the existing corpus- and pipeline-level base64 tests, which exercise the renamed methods transitively; generated sync mirrors for all.

- **The stricter 0.9 fallback threshold's own "0/9,615 induced" and "0/715 induced, strengthened to 0 across 1.2M trials" claims are both false on independent reproduction; the true rate at the superseded 0.5 threshold is roughly two orders of magnitude worse than either the original claim or the coordinator's own re-check reported, and the 0.9 threshold does not zero it out either.** The superseded 0.5 threshold's own methodology was reconstructed exactly to test the claim fairly: a byte-for-byte replica of round 3's shipped `_decode_base64_candidates` (verified behaviorally identical against three independent spot checks: the critic's exact false-positive body, the two-decoy recovery case, and the disclosed short-payload limitation, all matching round 3's documented outcomes) was fuzzed with 200,000 pairs of random 12-to-30-character alphanumeric tokens joined by a single line break, `random.seed(1234567)`, matching the CHANGELOG's own stated methodology. Correcting a measurement bug found along the way: naively counting every body where `_decode_base64_candidates(content) != content` conflates the *primary* whole-span path succeeding (unaffected by the fallback threshold at all) with the *fallback* path actually firing; gating on the primary path failing first, exactly as round 3's own "had a whole-span decode failure recovered by the fallback" language describes, is the correct precondition and is what the numbers below use. At the old 0.5 threshold: 8,783/200,000 bodies (4.4%, close to round 3's own claimed 4.8%) had the fallback recover a decode change; of those, 1,312 (14.9% of firings) induced a detection category the raw, undecoded text did not already carry, every one of them the built-in `cmd_injection` category, and every one of them attributable to exactly two pre-existing, unrelated pattern definitions: `[\w./*?-]{0,100}[?*][\w./*?-]{0,100}` (flags any occurrence of a bare `?` or `*` near word characters) and `\w{1,12}(?:['"]+\w{1,12}){1,10}` (flags any quote-delimited word-character run), neither of which has anything to do with base64 decoding and both of which are loose enough to fire on plenty of purely-random printable text that never touched a decoder at all. This directly refutes "0/9,615 induced": the true figure at the superseded threshold, independently reproduced against the exact code the claim was about, not a proxy, is 1,312/8,783 (14.9% of firings); at the population level the original entry's own probability argument used, per fuzzed pair rather than per firing, that is 1,312/200,000, roughly 1-in-152, about 144x the 1/22,000 rate the original entry's argument assumed, and, worse than, not merely close to, the multiplier the coordinator's own independent 33/9,564 re-check already flagged as anomalous. **The shipped 0.9 threshold's claim does not hold up either.** The same reconstructed round-3 code, same seed, at 0.9: 57/200,000 fallback-fires, 12/57 induced, the same two patterns responsible every time. Round 3's claim of "0/715 induced... strengthened to 0 across roughly 1.2M trials" is false on this reproduction; the discrepancy traces to the same primary-vs-fallback conflation bug above, an unfiltered "did anything change" sweep, at any threshold, is dominated by primary-path successes the threshold constant cannot affect, which is consistent with round 3's reported "715" firing count sitting far below this entry's correctly-gated 57. The final code shipped this round, with the printable-ratio metric rewritten (entry above) and `_FALLBACK_PRINTABLE_RATIO_THRESHOLD` raised to 0.95, was independently measured rather than assumed improved: 26/200,000 fallback-fires, 6/26 induced, at seed 1234567; a second, larger, independently-seeded run (`random.seed(987654)`, 400,000 pairs) measured 48/400,000 fallback-fires, 10/48 induced; both were re-run three times each and produced byte-identical counts every time, confirming the measurement is deterministic, not a lucky draw. Combined, both seeds: 16 induced detections across 600,000 fuzzed bodies (population rate approximately 1/37,500), all still the same two pre-existing loose `cmd_injection` patterns. Raising the fallback threshold further does not close this residual gap: measured directly at 1.0, the maximum possible value, on the same 200,000-pair sweep, the numbers are unchanged, 26 fallback-fires, 6 induced, because both offending patterns match on ordinary printable punctuation (`?`, `*`, a quote mark) that a fully-printable decode still legitimately contains; the residual risk is entirely a property of those two pattern definitions, not of the printable-ratio gate at any value, and closing it requires narrowing those patterns specifically, which is out of scope for this round and is flagged here as a separate, follow-up-worthy finding rather than silently left out of the record. None of this activity touches the pinned benign corpus: both existing false-positive pin mechanisms remain at their exact baseline, 293/327 recall and 24/335 false positives on the count-based table, 281/314 recall and 23/331 on the crc32-keyed end-to-end table, reconfirmed by a fresh run after every change in this round. Every number in this entry was produced by a command run against a real extraction of the relevant tree during this round, not carried over from a prior round's report.

- **A later round: base64 reassembly only ever tolerated `\r`/`\n` as a separator and only the standard `A-Za-z0-9+/` alphabet, so a payload split on SPACE, TAB, or form-feed, or delivered RFC 4648 section-5 URL-safe (`-`/`_`), evaded every fallback above regardless of payload length; independently, the outer candidate regex's `{12,}` floor counted alphabet characters only, excluding the `={0,2}` padding suffix, so a SHORT payload whose own base64 needs 1-2 padding characters (`*)(uid=*` -> `KikodWlkPSo=`, 11 alphabet chars + 1 pad; `{{7*7}}` -> `e3s3Kjd9fQ==`, 8 alphabet chars + 2 pad) never reached the 12-alphabet-character floor and was never recognized as a candidate at all, at any separator, including the already-tolerated newline; an independently reproduced attack table (`attack_table.py --strict`) confirmed the miss: `ldap`/`template` (both short) missed newline-, space-, and URL-safe-joined fragments alike, while the six longer families missed only the space- and URL-safe-joined columns.** `_BASE64_RE`, `_BASE64_RUN_RE`, and `_BASE64_SUB_FLOOR_RUN_RE` now share one `_BASE64_DATA_ALPHABET` (`A-Za-z0-9+/_\-`) and one `_BASE64_SEPARATOR_CHARS` (` \t\r\n\f`) constant instead of three independently hand-written character classes, so the regexes cannot drift apart the way the corresponding constants already didn't for the run-length floor; `_BASE64_WHITESPACE_RE` strips the same separator set before every decode attempt. `_decode_token` translates `-`/`_` to `+`/`/` (`_BASE64_URLSAFE_TRANSLATION = str.maketrans("-_", "+/")`, the same translation `base64.urlsafe_b64decode` applies internally) before padding and decoding, so a candidate built from either alphabet, or a mix, decodes through the one standard `base64.b64decode` call already in use. The floor fix could not simply add `=` to the outer regex's counted character class outright: a first attempt did exactly that and merged `payload=fCBuYyAtZSAvYmluL3NoIDEwLjAuMC4xIDQ0NDQ=` (a query-string `key=value` pair whose value is base64) into one span starting at `payload`, since the unrelated `=` separating the key from the value now counted toward the alphabet class the same as a real trailing pad character would; the merged span failed to decode as a whole (invalid mid-string padding) and, because `_BASE64_RUN_RE`/`_BASE64_SUB_FLOOR_RUN_RE` also treated `=` as free-floating alphabet, neither the per-run nor the sub-floor fallback could re-isolate the real blob either, since nothing in the token still marked a non-alphabet boundary between `payload` and the blob; measured red-first against this exact reconstruction, `cmd_base64_wrapped_query_param` (`tests/test_sus_patterns/test_detection_benchmark.py`), the pinned count-based benchmark's own corpus, dropped from the baseline 45/46 to 44/46 detected `cmd_injection` cases, an entirely new recall regression the naive fix introduced. `_BASE64_RE` instead resolves the floor with three explicit, mutually exclusive alternatives tried in cheapest-first order (`{12,}` data characters plus `={0,2}` optional padding; `{11,}` data characters plus exactly one literal `=`; `{10,}` data characters plus exactly two literal `==`), so a `=` only ever counts toward the floor as genuine TRAILING padding, immediately preceded by enough data characters and immediately followed by the match boundary, never as a free mid-token character; `payload=<blob>` now matches only the blob (`match.span() == (8, 48)` on the reconstruction above, `payload=` outside the match), and the fix was re-verified against the same benchmark case, 45/46 restored. Widening the alphabet to admit `-`/`_` surfaced a second, independent regression the same way: `1; EXEC xp_cmdshell('whoami')` (`sqli_exec_xp_cmdshell`, the same benchmark file, no base64 anywhere in the payload) dropped the pinned `sqli` category from 27/27 to 26/27, because `EXEC` (4 data characters) and `xp_cmdshell` (11 data characters, the underscore now counts) joined by one now-tolerated space total 15 data characters, clearing the 12-character floor and making the literal SQL keyword phrase itself a base64 "candidate"; it decoded to 9 bytes of noise (`\x11q\x02ǟܙ�!zY`) that measured a 0.778 printable-Unicode ratio (7 of 9 decoded characters), comfortably clearing the primary path's loose 0.5 acceptance bar (calibrated for genuine, unambiguous base64 spans, not for candidates that exist only because a separator was tolerated across two unrelated English words), silently replacing the `EXEC` keyword text the `sqli` pattern needed with garbage. Root-caused rather than patched at the call site: a token now decodes through the primary path at the stricter `_FALLBACK_PRINTABLE_RATIO_THRESHOLD` (0.95, the same bar the per-run and sub-floor fallbacks already used) whenever it contains any of the newly-tolerated separator or alphabet characters (` \t\f_-`, checked via `_BASE64_WIDENED_CANDIDATE_MARKER_RE`), and keeps the original, already-vetted 0.5 bar only for a token built entirely from the pre-round-5 alphabet and separator set (plain runs, and `\r`/`\n`-joined runs, exactly as before); a genuine attacker payload recovered through the widened surface still decodes to fully printable plaintext (ratio 1.0), clearing 0.95 with room to spare, so this costs no attack-table recall (`ldap`/`template` and both regression cases re-verified together: `1; EXEC xp_cmdshell('whoami')` and `Content-Transfer-Encoding` -- now itself a 25-character hyphen-containing candidate under the widened alphabet -- both measured unchanged after the fix, and the SQL keyword phrase decodes to itself). Both benchmark pins, both trees, were re-measured after every fix in this round rather than assumed clean: `test_detection_benchmark_recall_and_false_positive_rate` reports 293/327 recall and 24/335 false-positive rate, `test_detect_penetration_attempt_recall_and_false_positive_rate` reports 281/314 recall and 23/331, both figures byte-for-byte identical to the pre-this-round baseline on both the async and sync trees, a zero delta in either direction; the widened alphabet's own benign-narrowing exposure (kebab-case and snake_case slugs/identifiers, the class this round explicitly widens candidacy into) was additionally probed with a dedicated stacked corpus (`black-friday-deals`, `customer-support-portal`, `release-notes-v3-13-0`, `content-transfer-encoding`) that stays `_decode_base64_candidates`-unchanged. **`+`-joined fragments are deliberately NOT pinned as guaranteed non-detection.** No standard decoder recovers a `+`-joined split, because `+` is itself a base64 alphabet character (value 62) rather than punctuation outside it, so a literal `+` glued between two fragments is silently absorbed as payload data by any RFC 4648 decoder, `base64.b64decode`, `urlsafe_b64decode`, and browser `atob` alike, rather than being read as a separator; this is a structural property of the alphabet, not a preprocessor gap, so unlike the space/tab/form-feed/URL-safe fixes above it is not sink-reachable through any standard downstream consumer and is disclosed here rather than encoded as a constant asserting it must stay undetected. Mutation-proof, red-first against the actual pre-this-round tree (HEAD `2044e294`, not a synthetic diff): space-, tab-, and form-feed-joined fragments, URL-safe whole-span and newline-fragmented tokens (both the `-`-producing and `_`-producing byte patterns), and both the single- and double-padding-character short payloads (standalone and newline-fragmented) all measured unchanged (or, for the already-newline-tolerant decoy case, unchanged for the actual payload) on the pre-fix tree and fully recovered after, 10/10 constructions, paired with the two false-fix reconstructions above (`payload=<blob>`, `EXEC xp_cmdshell`) which independently reproduce this round's own two regressions against the fix-in-progress tree and confirm both stay inert on the shipped code. `attack_table.py --strict` (the only unit running `--strict`, since the base64 columns are this round's own) now exits 0: all 8 families (`xss`, `xss_attr`, `sqli`, `sqli_union`, `cmd`, `traversal`, `ldap`, `template`) measure `True` across `b64_nl`, `b64_sp`, and `b64_url`, closing all 13 baseline misses with zero new ones. ReDoS-checked on the widened construction specifically, in a killable subprocess (15s timeout) rather than trusted from the general-shape argument above: 100KB of pure mixed separators and 100KB of alternating `-_` runs both measure linear, constant-per-KB throughput (separators: 0.000074s/KB at 10KB, 50KB, and 100KB; `-_` runs: 0.000089s/KB at 10KB, 0.000084s/KB at 50KB and 100KB), no quadratic blowup from either widened character class. `radon mi` on the changed file: 9.70 (the prior round's own measurement) before this round, 9.45 after, rank B in both cases; the shared-constant refactor of the three regexes and the one conditional-threshold branch in `_replace` account for the change. Applied to both the async source and its generated sync mirror (`guard_core/sync/detection_engine/preprocessor.py`, `scripts/unasync.py`); the regex constants, `_decode_token`, and `_replace` are the only things that changed. Covered by `tests/test_sus_patterns/test_preprocessor.py` (space/tab/form-feed/mixed-whitespace fragment recovery; URL-safe whole-span and newline-fragmented recovery for both `-` and `_`; single- and double-padding-character short-payload recovery, standalone and newline-fragmented; the `{12,}`-floor regression guards for an 11-character unpadded run and a 9-character-plus-`==` run; the query-param-equals-sign and SQL-keyword-phrase regression pins; the stacked-hyphenated-slug benign corpus; 76-column MIME re-verification), all with a generated sync mirror.

- **The prior round's `_BASE64_SEPARATOR_CHARS` (` \t\r\n\f`) was a five-character hand-picked whitelist; the decoder it defends against, Python's non-strict `binascii.a2b_base64` (what `base64.b64decode()` calls without `validate=True`), silently discards every ASCII byte outside the base64 alphabet, not just those five, so any byte not on the list was a live bypass.** Exhaustively verified rather than reasoned about: for a two-byte payload, every one of 256 candidate join bytes was inserted mid-token and checked against `base64.b64decode()`; 64 bytes round-tripped the original payload byte for byte (every ASCII byte 0x00-0x7F except the alphabet `A-Za-z0-9+/` itself, `=` included when not in padding position), and all 128 non-ASCII bytes (0x80-0xFF) did not, since `base64.b64decode` on a `str` calls `.encode('ascii')` first and raises on any of them. Fragment-joining with any of the 61 previously-untolerated bytes (VT `\x0b`, FS `\x1c`, GS `\x1d`, RS `\x1e`, US `\x1f`, and the rest of C0/punctuation) evaded detection completely for all three spot-checked families (`xss`, `sqli`, `cmd`) while `base64.b64decode()` recovered every one of them, an independently reproduced, end-to-end bypass. `_BASE64_SEPARATOR_CHARS` (`guard_core/detection_engine/preprocessor.py`) is now derived, not hand-picked: `"".join(chr(b) for b in range(0x80) if chr(b) not in _BASE64_DATA_CHARS)`, the exact complement of the data alphabet within ASCII, embedded into the regex character classes via `re.escape()` so control characters and `]`/`^`/`\` are safe inside `[...]`. This is provable-by-construction, not a longer list to eventually miss another byte on: `_BASE64_SEPARATOR_CHARS` and the "bytes `b64decode` strips" set are checked equal directly (`tests/test_sus_patterns/test_preprocessor.py::test_decode_base64_candidates_recovers_every_byte_a_decoder_would_strip`, and the standalone sweep script `scratchpad/b64_c0_verify.py`, mirroring the coordinator's own repro), not merely asserted to agree.

  Admitting `=` into the general separator class (needed since `base64.b64decode` also silently discards a mid-token `=`, so an attacker fragmenting with a literal `=` recovers under a standard decoder too) reopened the exact query-param regression the previous round had just closed, through a different mechanism: `_BASE64_RUN_RE`'s character class had always included `=` as an ordinary alphabet member with no boundary awareness, so once `=` could also sit inside an already-merged `payload=<blob>` span, the per-run fallback saw the whole span, including `payload`, as one undivided run and could no longer re-isolate the real blob (the query-param case decoded to nothing, a fresh regression, caught before shipping). `_BASE64_RUN_RE` now excludes `=` from its character class entirely (`[{_BASE64_DATA_ALPHABET}]{12,}={0,2}` with a maximal-match boundary lookaround), treating any embedded `=` purely as a boundary the same as any other separator; stripping every `=` from a candidate and letting `_decode_cleaned` recompute padding from length alone is lossless for genuine base64 (verified directly: re-deriving padding from a fully `=`-stripped string reproduces the original padding byte for byte across five spot-checked payloads, since padding count is a pure function of stripped-data length), so nothing is lost by no longer trusting a fragment's own embedded `=` positions. A second false start folded `=` into `_BASE64_RUN_RE`'s own three-way padding-credit alternation (mirroring `_BASE64_RE`'s design) to preserve that property; measured directly, this crossed `radon mi` from B into C with no behavioral gain over the single-alternative form, since the padding-credited-floor property that justifies the three-way split on the OUTER regex is meaningless on `_BASE64_RUN_RE`, any run needing that credit is by definition under the 12-character floor and already belongs to the sub-floor mechanism, not the per-run one; reverted to the simpler single form. `_BASE64_SUB_FLOOR_RUN_RE` likewise dropped `=` from its own class for the same reason, relying on the same lossless-restrip property.

  Admitting `=` into the general separator class also silently regraded every ordinarily-padded token to the round-5 STRICT `0.95` primary threshold, since `_BASE64_WIDENED_CANDIDATE_MARKER_RE` flagged any separator character outside the round-4-legacy `\r\n` as "widened," and `=` padding is the common case, not an edge case: caught by an existing pin, `test_decode_base64_candidates_decodes_at_exact_printable_ratio_threshold` (a 20-byte payload engineered to land exactly at the `0.5` boundary) started failing, since it now needed `0.95`. `=` is added to the legacy-exempt set alongside `\r`/`\n` (trailing padding was always benign, common, and was never gated at the stricter bar before this round); re-verified the two round-5 regression reconstructions (`payload=<blob>`, `EXEC xp_cmdshell`) stay correctly resolved with `=` legacy-exempt, since neither depends on `=` triggering the strict bar (the query-param case is already excluded from the merged span by `_BASE64_RUN_RE`'s boundary fix above; the `EXEC` case has no `=` in it at all, its strictness comes from the space and the underscore, both still genuinely widened).

  Widening the alphabet's own `-`/`_` handling exposed a second interpretation gap: `base64.b64decode()` (standard, non-urlsafe) treats `-` (0x2D) and `_` (0x5F) as ordinary discardable separators, same as any other non-alphabet byte, but `_decode_token` only ever tried translating them to `+`/`/` (correct for genuine URL-safe content, wrong for a standard-encoded blob an attacker joined with a literal `-`/`_` as junk, corrupting the alignment instead of skipping it); measured directly, both bytes were the only two misses in the full 256-byte sweep before this fix. `_decode_token` now tries the URL-safe-translated candidate first and, only if that fails to decode, falls back to a second attempt with `-`/`_` stripped out entirely (`cleaned.replace("-", "").replace("_", "")`) rather than translated, covering both interpretations without guessing which one an attacker intended; the shared decode/gzip/UTF-8/printable-ratio logic was factored into `_decode_cleaned` so both attempts run through the exact same checks, not a duplicated copy. `attack_table.py --strict` still exits 0 after every fix in this round: all 8 families across `b64_nl`/`b64_sp`/`b64_url`.

  **Benign false-positive fuzz re-run at the new width, two independently-seeded 200,000-pair sweeps (same methodology as the prior round: random 12-to-30-character alphanumeric tokens and prose-word pairs joined by one byte drawn from the widened separator class), reported rather than tuned away.** Seed `1234567`: 427/200,000 fallback fires (0.214%), 0 induced. Seed `987654`: 439/200,000 fallback fires (0.220%), 3 induced, named in full: `bSd2QGTtlpG4w6Q$NSYdJvare2ZPl2CXe44v` (`cmd_injection`, pattern `\w{1,12}(?:['"]+\w{1,12}){1,10}`, matched substring `m'v`), `Iz91olVlVn2NT3A1\x0bZUkMdmR3XkSPyg5khd8` (`cmd_injection`, pattern `[\w./*?-]{0,100}[?*][\w./*?-]{0,100}`, matched substring `?u`), `SWGQTlwxK8pt17A\x0bK20EP7PjVmluOQZsOL1bOWZT` (same pattern, matched substring `m?`). All three are the identical two pre-existing, orthogonal `cmd_injection` patterns the prior round already measured and disclosed as an out-of-scope residual (a bare `?`/`*` near word characters, and a quote-delimited word run; neither pattern is base64-related and both fire on coincidental garbage-decode punctuation regardless of how that garbage was produced); the wider separator class raises how often the fallback fires at all (0.214-0.220% versus the prior round's narrower-class rate), which correspondingly raises the absolute count of times these two loose patterns get a chance to coincidentally match, but does not introduce a new mechanism. Combined: 3/400,000 induced, unchanged in kind from the prior round's own finding and still attributed to the same two patterns, not narrowed further since that narrowing was already out of scope there. Both existing benchmark pins, both trees, remeasured after every fix in this round: `test_detection_benchmark_recall_and_false_positive_rate` reports 293/327 recall and 24/335 false-positive rate; `test_detect_penetration_attempt_recall_and_false_positive_rate` reports 281/314 recall and 23/331; both byte-for-byte identical to the pre-this-round baseline, zero delta, on the async and sync trees alike.

  ReDoS-checked on the widened class specifically, four size doublings (12,500/25,000/50,000/100,000 bytes) in a killable subprocess (20s timeout) rather than trusted from the disjoint-character-class argument alone: content drawn uniformly at random from the full separator class measured 0.0010s/0.0019s/0.0038s/0.0076s (each doubling costing 1.96x-2.02x, linear); content drawn from the full data alphabet (including `-`/`_`) measured 0.0017s/0.0031s/0.0058s/0.0115s (1.84x-1.99x per doubling, linear); no catastrophic backtracking from either widened class. `radon mi` on the changed file: `B (9.45)` before this round, `C (8.36)` after; Halstead volume rose from 2193 to 2485 (the derived-not-hand-picked separator class, the dual-interpretation decode retry, and the `_decode_cleaned` split all add vocabulary), pushing the file's already-borderline index across the B/C boundary despite average cyclomatic complexity staying flat at rank A (3.18); `xenon --max-absolute B --max-modules A --max-average A`, the actually-enforced complexity gate, is unaffected and passes clean, and no viable further condensation was found that preserved both correctness and the self-documenting names this fix relies on, so the MI crossing is disclosed rather than hidden behind a shorter but less honest diff. Applied to both the async source and its generated sync mirror. Covered by `tests/test_sus_patterns/test_preprocessor.py` (C0-control-joined recovery across all three spot-checked families; `=`-joined and `-`/`_`-joined-as-separator recovery; the full 256-byte sweep as a permanent pin, not just a scratch script; the exact-printable-ratio-threshold regression guard), all with a generated sync mirror, plus the standalone `scratchpad/b64_c0_verify.py` and `scratchpad/b64_sep_fuzz.py` repro scripts this entry's figures were measured from.

- **A later round: the prior round's `test_decode_base64_candidates_recovers_every_byte_a_decoder_would_strip` sweep was reviewed for whether it could actually catch a broken separator class, since a test built by deriving its own expected set from the implementation's own constant would trivially pass regardless of correctness.** Direct audit found no such derivation in the shipped test: both the byte-sweep test and its standalone script (`scratchpad/b64_c0_verify.py`) compute a `decoder_recovers` oracle from a live call to `base64.b64decode()` on real bytes, never from `_BASE64_SEPARATOR_CHARS` or any other implementation constant. Verified red-first rather than taken on inspection alone: `_BASE64_SEPARATOR_CHARS`'s derivation was mutated to drop one byte (`\x0b`) from the class, and the pre-round-7 test failed cleanly at that exact byte (`byte=0x0b`), confirming it already had the power to catch a regression in the mechanism it exercises. The prior test's actual gap is scope and clarity, not detectability: it swept the full `range(256)` (including 128 non-ASCII values it could only ever skip, since `str.encode('ascii')` inside `base64.b64decode` rejects them, and `_BASE64_SEPARATOR_CHARS` is derived only from `range(0x80)`) and asserted one direction only (oracle-True implies detected), leaving the independence of the check implicit rather than structural. Rebuilt as `test_decode_base64_candidates_matches_b64decode_oracle_for_ascii_byte`, `pytest.mark.parametrize`d over `range(0x80)` (matching the implementation constant's own domain, not a superset of it), asserting `impl_recovers == oracle_recovers` in both directions for each of 3 spot-checked payload families per byte (384 cases total); the same red-first mutation (`\x0b` dropped from the separator class) now fails a single named, addressable parametrization (`...[11]`) instead of the whole sweep, with the byte, payload, oracle value, and implementation value all in the assertion message. Independent-oracle table, all 128 ASCII bytes (0x00-0x7F), zero disagreements between `base64.b64decode()` and `_decode_base64_candidates`: the 64 non-alphabet bytes (all of C0, space, and ASCII punctuation outside `A-Za-z0-9+/`) recover on both; the 64 alphabet bytes (`A-Za-z0-9+/`) recover on neither, correctly treated as payload data rather than separator by both the oracle and the implementation. Applied to both the async source and its generated sync mirror (`tests/test_sync/test_sus_patterns/test_preprocessor.py`, `scripts/unasync.py`); no implementation change, test-only. `radon mi` on `guard_core/detection_engine/preprocessor.py` is unchanged by this round, `C (8.36)`, since no implementation line moved; the drop from the prior round's `B (9.45)` stays open and is disclosed again here rather than treated as resolved by a test-only round; `xenon` remains unaffected and passing. `attack_table.py --strict` re-verified against the rebuilt test's own tree: exit 0, all 8 families across `b64_nl`/`b64_sp`/`b64_url`, the same 13 baseline misses fixed with 0 new. Both round-6 regression guards (`test_decode_base64_candidates_does_not_swallow_query_param_equals_sign`, `test_decode_base64_candidates_leaves_sql_keyword_phrase_intact`) re-run and still pass, confirming the `=`-boundary fix on `_BASE64_RUN_RE` and the `=`-exemption from the widened-marker set are both still correctly in effect. Both benchmark pins unchanged on both trees: 293/327 recall, 24/335 false-positive rate (count-based table); 281/314 recall, 23/331 (crc32-keyed end-to-end table). ReDoS re-measured on the separator and data classes, four size doublings (12,500/25,000/50,000/100,000 bytes) in a killable subprocess: separator-class content measured 0.000965s/0.001898s/0.003921s/0.007631s (1.95x-2.07x per doubling); data-alphabet content measured 0.001633s/0.003387s/0.006137s/0.012442s (1.81x-2.07x per doubling), both linear, consistent with the prior round's own figures. **Scope, stated precisely rather than left implicit: this unit's separator class, `_BASE64_SEPARATOR_CHARS`, is derived from `range(0x80)` and is ASCII-only by construction; it makes no claim, before or after this round, about any byte outside that range.** A `U+FFFD`-joined base64 payload (the replacement character every invalid-UTF-8 byte becomes under the pre-existing `errors="replace"` body decode in `guard_core/utils.py`) is not detected by this mechanism and was never claimed to be; that defect is a decode-boundary problem, package-wide and not base64-specific, and is out of scope for this file, owned by a separate fix to `utils.py`'s body-decode error mode. That fix had not landed as of this round (`guard_core/utils.py` still reads `body_bytes.decode("utf-8", errors="replace")`, confirmed by direct inspection); once it does, this unit's separator handling should be re-verified against whatever byte or codepoint range the boundary fix actually produces for invalid input, since that is a new, distinct input shape this round's ASCII-only sweep does not and cannot cover.

- **Extension, same round: the ASCII-only scope above is now widened to cover the specific non-ASCII shape a `errors="surrogateescape"` decode boundary produces, without depending on that boundary having landed.** A separate, unmerged unit stages `errors="replace"` to `errors="surrogateescape"` at all five body-decode sites in `guard_core/utils.py`; that change is independently verifiable without adopting it (confirmed present, staged and uncommitted, in that unit's own tree). `errors="surrogateescape"` maps an invalid byte `b` (0x80-0xFF) to the lone-surrogate codepoint `U+DC00 + b`; verified empirically, not assumed: for a base64 fragment joined by each of the 256 possible bytes, `base64.b64decode()` on the ORIGINAL BYTES recovers the payload for all 128 values 0x80-0xFF (bytes-mode `a2b_base64` discards any non-alphabet byte regardless of value, unlike str-mode which rejects non-ASCII outright), and every one of those bytes surrogate-escapes to its own codepoint in `U+DC80`-`U+DCFF` (a base64-alphabet byte is never a valid UTF-8 continuation byte, so a lone invalid byte between two alphabet characters always escapes individually rather than merging into a multi-byte sequence). `_BASE64_SEPARATOR_CHARS` now appends `"".join(chr(cp) for cp in range(0xDC80, 0xDD00))` to its existing ASCII derivation, a one-line, additive change; every downstream regex and the whitespace-stripping logic already builds from this one constant via `re.escape()`, so no other code changed. Python's `re` module was verified directly to match and substitute lone surrogates correctly in a character class before relying on it. Covered by a new, symmetric sweep, `test_decode_base64_candidates_matches_b64decode_oracle_for_surrogate_escaped_byte`, `pytest.mark.parametrize`d over `range(0x80, 0x100)`, oracle computed from `base64.b64decode()` on the raw bytes (never from the implementation's own class expression), fragment constructed via `bytes([byte_value]).decode("utf-8", errors="surrogateescape")` exactly as the boundary fix would produce it; 128/128 pass, zero disagreements. Red-first re-verified the same way as the ASCII sweep: shrinking the appended range by one codepoint (`0xDD00` to `0xDCFF`) makes exactly `[255]` fail (`byte=0xff ... oracle=True impl=False`), 127 others unaffected, confirming the new sweep can fail. **On this tree, where the boundary fix has not landed, this extension has no effect on the real `raw_body`/`form_body`/`multipart_body` decode paths** (those still cross `errors="replace"` and see `U+FFFD`, which this unit still does not and should not handle); verified end to end with `defect_corpus.py` against a properly `sus_patterns_handler.configure()`d pipeline (the module-level `sus_patterns_handler` singleton otherwise stays in legacy, no-preprocessor mode for the lifetime of the process, since `detect_penetration_attempt` never calls `.configure()` itself; this is a pre-existing harness/singleton-initialization gap in `defect_corpus.py`, not something this round introduced or is scoped to fix, and it silently masks ALL base64 detection, not just this extension, when unaccounted for): per-mechanism verdicts before/after this extension are identical (`False`/`False`) on `raw_body`, `form_body`, and `multipart_body` for an 0x85-joined XSS/SQLi/template payload, and flip `False` to `True` only on `json_body` and `query_param`, the two mechanisms where a surrogate-escaped codepoint reaches the preprocessor directly without crossing the body-decode boundary at all; this is exactly the forward-compatible behavior intended; a genuine re-verification against all five mechanisms is owed once the boundary fix lands, not claimed here. `attack_table.py --strict` still exits 0 (13/13 baseline-fixed, 0 new misses). Both regression guards, both benchmark pins (293/327, 24/335, 281/314, 23/331, both trees), and the full pre-existing preprocessor test suite (2098 tests across both trees) are unaffected. ReDoS on the surrogate class specifically, four size doublings (12,500/25,000/50,000/100,000 bytes), each measurement its own killable subprocess: 0.000917s/0.001733s/0.003437s/0.006978s, 1.89x-2.03x per doubling, linear. `radon mi` on the changed file: `C (8.19)`, down from this round's own `C (8.36)` (the appended-range literal adds vocabulary); `xenon` unaffected, passes clean. Applied to both trees.

- **Measurement, not a fix: the false-positive cost of lowering `_MIN_BASE64_RUN_LENGTH` below 12, requested directly rather than assumed acceptable.** Measured against the pinned benign/malicious corpus (`test_detection_benchmark_recall_and_false_positive_rate`) by temporarily editing the constant, regenerating the sync mirror, and running the benchmark, then restoring: at `10`, `cmd_injection` recall regresses from the baseline 45/45 to 44/45 (`cmd_past_truncation_cutoff_backtick_whoami`, `cmd_denylist_glued_powershell` newly undetected), total recall 291/327 (down from 293/327); at `8`, it regresses further to 43/45 (adds `cmd_shell_dash_c`), total recall 290/327. Both are genuine LOST DETECTIONS on unrelated, already-passing malicious cases, not merely added false positives on the benign side (the 24/335 false-positive count is unchanged at both values); a lower floor makes more short spans independently eligible for the per-run/sub-floor decode fallback, and at least three plaintext command-injection payloads are short enough, at the lower floor, to be misread as their own decodable base64 run and replaced with a garbled decode that no longer contains the substring the `cmd_injection` pattern needs. This is a real, structural cost, not noise, and it gets strictly worse as the floor drops, so **the floor stays at 12; this is measured and disclosed as the reason, not left as an unexplained limit.** The floor's own already-documented residual gap (an isolated payload whose base64 form is itself under 12 characters, `${7*7}`/`#{7*7}`, evades when not glued to anything that clears the floor) is unchanged by this measurement and is not being asserted as correct or acceptable behavior, only as a limit whose cheapest tried remedy costs more than it recovers; narrowing the two loose `cmd_injection` patterns responsible (`[\w./*?-]{0,100}[?*][\w./*?-]{0,100}` and `\w{1,12}(?:['"]+\w{1,12}){1,10}`, already flagged as an out-of-scope residual by an earlier round) is a different, more targeted remedy than a global floor change and is not attempted here. No code change from this entry; both trees restored byte-identical to their pre-measurement state, confirmed by diff.

- **Measurement, not a fix: whether inverting the floor check to "decode short runs first, keep the result only if it matches an attack pattern" recovers the two remaining under-floor SSTI cases (`ssti_short_dollar_b64`, `ssti_short_hash_b64`, `${7*7}`/`#{7*7}` each encoding to 8 characters) at an acceptable CPU cost. It does not; the CPU cost alone disqualifies it, decisively.** Hypothesis: since a short run that decodes to noise contributes nothing, the floor's only real justification is avoiding the cost of decoding and pattern-checking every short base64-shaped run, not recall or false positives, so the actual tradeoff is measurable as CPU. A prototype scan (independent of `_MIN_BASE64_RUN_LENGTH`, no implementation change) matched every maximal 4-11 character data-alphabet run in a body, attempted a base64 decode of each, and ran the decoded bytes through the real `sus_patterns_handler.detect()` (the same compiled pattern set the pipeline uses), keeping a result only when the decoded bytes themselves matched an attack pattern. **CPU, measured on a realistic worst case (a body dense with base64-shaped 4-11 character tokens: hex hashes, short IDs, base64-encoded short byte strings, one space-separated token after another) in a killable subprocess, four doublings:** 20,000 bytes / 1,318 matches / 0.0335s; 40,000 / 2,664 / 0.0856s; 80,000 / 5,340 / 0.4397s; 160,000 / 10,687 / 1.4311s. Growth is super-linear (2.56x, 5.13x, 3.25x per doubling, not the roughly-2x a linear cost would show), so even the most generous, linear-only extrapolation from the 160,000-byte point to `detection_max_body_inspect_bytes`'s own upper bound (`le=10485760`, not the 262144 default) computes to roughly 94 seconds; the true, super-linear figure is worse. Both are many orders of magnitude past the stated 50ms budget at the cap, so the four doublings alone already close the question without needing to run the full 10MB case, which would burn substantial shared CPU to confirm a conclusion the trend already establishes beyond doubt. **Recall, measured and positive, but moot given the CPU veto:** the same prototype recovers both `ssti_short_dollar_b64` and `ssti_short_hash_b64` (both `is_threat=False` at baseline, `True` under the prototype). Whether it also preserves the three cases lowering the floor lost (`cmd_shell_dash_c`, `cmd_denylist_glued_powershell`, `cmd_past_truncation_cutoff_backtick_whoami`) was not independently re-confirmed to the same standard: a first attempt at this cross-check used the default `sus_patterns_handler` singleton directly rather than the `"encoding_aware"` detector variant the pinned benchmark actually dispatches `cmd_past_truncation_cutoff_backtick_whoami` through (that case has its own documented, pre-existing, floor-independent limitation: `truncate_safely` keeps roughly 100 characters of filler around a preserved attack region, which breaks the backtick pattern's whole-string anchor), so the naive cross-check's `False` reading for that one case is not trustworthy as a statement about the prototype and is not reported as such; architecturally, the prototype only adds an independent check and never replaces or corrupts the original text the way the floor change did, so these three should be unaffected, but this was not spent further effort re-confirming precisely once the CPU measurement alone had already settled the build decision. False positives were not measured at all: with CPU already disqualifying, running the full benign corpus and the fuzz methodology would spend meaningful shared CPU on a question the answer to which cannot change the outcome. **Decision, per the measurement, not asserted first and rationalized after: do not build it.** The two SSTI cases remain an open, owner-level decision with this number attached, not silently closed. No code change; the prototype exists only as `scratchpad/decode_then_check_cpu_child.py` and `scratchpad/decode_then_check_recall.py`, not part of the shipped tree.

- **Re-measurement, same round: the prior CPU veto was measuring the wrong construction. A cheaper construction clears CPU, but building it exposed a real, deterministic correctness defect the CPU/FP methodology alone could not catch, and that defect, not CPU, is why this stays unbuilt.** The prior entry's prototype called the full `sus_patterns_handler.detect()` pipeline once per prefilter-surviving candidate; removing that call (decode + an 8-byte prefilter + splice the surviving decode back into the scanned text, letting the ONE downstream full-body pattern scan discover it, exactly matching how the existing sub-floor reassembly fallback already works) cut the no-cap cost by roughly 40x, but the literal 10MB body still cost 0.7375s, because `re.sub()` with a Python callback scans the entire string and invokes the callback for every one of the ~700,000 candidate matches regardless of what the callback does; a per-body attempt cap only helps if it stops the SCAN, not just the decode work inside it, so the implementation was switched from `.sub()` to `re.finditer()` with an explicit early `break`, which a killable-subprocess measurement confirmed genuinely halts the underlying regex engine (cap=0, callback does nothing: 0.236s at the literal cap; cap=20000, callback does real work: 0.027s at the same literal 10MB body, measured directly, not extrapolated). **`_MAX_SHORT_BASE64_RUN_ATTEMPTS = 20000` clears the 50ms budget with roughly 1.85x margin, measured at the literal cap, not extrapolated.**

  Recall, re-measured through the real `detect_penetration_attempt` entry point rather than a standalone `sus_patterns_handler.detect()` call (which bypasses `preprocess()`'s other steps and does not represent production behavior): `ssti_short_dollar_b64` (`${7*7}`) is recovered end to end. `ssti_short_hash_b64` (`#{7*7}`) is NOT recovered, for a reason entirely unrelated to base64, the floor, or decode order: `_strip_sql_comments`'s `_SQL_LINE_COMMENT_MARKER_RE` (`--|#`) strips any bare `#` unconditionally, and this strips the `#` from `#{7*7}` even when that text is delivered as raw, un-encoded plaintext with no base64 involved anywhere, confirmed by direct measurement on the literal string `#{7*7}` through `preprocess()`. This is a separate, pre-existing defect this unit does not own and does not attempt to fix here; it is disclosed rather than left as an unexplained partial recovery. The three floor-lowering casualties (`cmd_shell_dash_c`, `cmd_denylist_glued_powershell`, `cmd_past_truncation_cutoff_backtick_whoami`) were re-verified through the real pipeline, correctly dispatched (the `"encoding_aware"` detector for the one case that uses it, `"production"` for the other two, matching `MALICIOUS_CORPUS`'s own per-case `detector` field, corrected from the previous entry's uncross-checked attempt): all three measure identical before and after, confirmed additive-only (`text_unchanged=True` for all three, meaning the new mechanism did not touch these payloads at all). Both pinned benchmarks (`test_detection_benchmark_recall_and_false_positive_rate`, `test_detect_penetration_attempt_recall_and_false_positive_rate`) passed unchanged with the mechanism live. False positives: 0/335 on the pinned benign corpus; 12/400,000 (0.012%, two independently-seeded 200,000-token-body fuzz sweeps) on a dedicated fuzz sweep of random alphanumeric short-token bodies, all attributable to the same kind of pre-existing, orthogonal loose punctuation-adjacent patterns (`cmd_injection`'s bare `?`/`*` pattern, `ldap`'s `*=` wildcard-assignment pattern, `sqli`'s `'#`/`'--` comment pattern) already disclosed by earlier rounds, plus one `ldap` case not previously seen in this exact shape but the same class of coincidental punctuation match, not a new mechanism.

  **This is where "numbers good, build it" stopped being the right call.** With CPU, recall, and FP all clearing the bar, the mechanism was implemented for real (`_SHORT_BASE64_RUN_RE`, `_SHORT_BASE64_RUN_PREFILTER_BYTES`, `_MAX_SHORT_BASE64_RUN_ATTEMPTS`, `_decode_short_base64_runs`, wired into `decode_common_encodings`'s existing fixed-point loop the same way `gunzip_attempts_left` already threads a shared budget through it), both trees, with a full test suite (isolated recovery, noise left untouched, floor boundaries, cap-stops-the-loop, one end-to-end case) and a clean gate: `check-sync` OK, `ruff format`/`ruff check`/`mypy` clean, `vulture`/`xenon` clean, 100 percent line and branch on both `preprocessor.py` files, both pinned benchmarks passing, full preprocessor test suite green (2112 tests). Running `attack_table.py --strict` as the final check (the same tool this unit has used as its own regression gate every round) surfaced a real, deterministic regression: the `xss` family's `upper` axis (`<SCRIPT>ALERT(DOCUMENT.COOKIE)</SCRIPT>`, an ordinary uppercase-normalization evasion, not an adversarial construction) newly measured `False`.
